\chapter*{Econometrics and statistics}\label{intro}

This book develops the statistical foundations of econometrics and then uses them to study regression, panel data, parametric estimation, binary-choice models, and time series. Mathematical results are paired with simulations, empirical applications, and exercises. The material has been developed by \href{https://www.jprenne.com}{Jean-Paul Renne}.

The chapters are cumulative. The first three establish the probability and inference tools used later. The linear-regression chapter develops the central econometric framework; the chapters on panel regressions, estimation methods, and binary-choice models build on it. The time-series chapter introduces time-indexed data and forecasting. The mathematical appendix collects supporting results and longer proofs; the data appendix documents the empirical material.

Definitions and propositions state the core results. Examples show how those results operate in data, while practice boxes point to exercises that reinforce the surrounding material. Readers who use the computational examples can inspect the R code in the HTML edition; the PDF can be rendered with or without that code.

The computational examples use \textbf{AEC 0.2.0}. The checked source
archive is supplied in this course's \texttt{packages/} directory. From the complete
course source directory, first run \texttt{Rscript\ -\/-vanilla\ scripts/setup.R} to
restore the recorded environment. The following R commands then select the
course's private package installation. See the repository README for details.

\textbf{Notation.} Bold letters denote vectors and matrices; a prime denotes transpose. The identity matrix is \(\mathbf{Id}_n\), the vector of ones is \(\mathbf{1}_n\), and \(\mathbb{I}_{A}\) is the indicator of event \(A\). Fisher information uses a different symbol: \(\mathcal{I}_Y(\boldsymbol\theta)\) is information per observation and \(\mathbf{I}(\boldsymbol\theta)\) is full-sample information. For i.i.d. observations, \(\mathbf{I}=n\mathcal{I}_Y\). The second argument of \(\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)\) is a variance, or a covariance matrix in the multivariate case. We distinguish an exact distribution (\(\sim\)), a large-sample approximation (\(\overset{a}{\sim}\)), and a limiting distribution (\(\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\)). Dimensions and any local changes of notation are stated where introduced.

\textbf{Data and examples.} Appendix \ref{data-appendix} describes the packaged data and maps them to the applications. Simulated and hypothetical examples are identified as such. A recorded-data comparison, a probability experiment that samples from those records, and a model for a wider population are different objects; each example states which one it uses.

\chapter{Basic statistical results}\label{Basics}

Econometrics describes populations, compares groups, and learns about unknown quantities from samples. This chapter introduces the probability tools used for those tasks. We begin with discrete random variables, for which joint and conditional distributions can be read directly from a table. Continuous distributions are introduced afterwards as the corresponding integral-based formulation.

\textbf{Prerequisites.} Basic probability, differentiation, and integration.

\textbf{After working through this chapter, you should be able to:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate moments of a random variable and interpret variance and covariance.
\item
  Move between joint, marginal, and conditional distributions.
\item
  Use densities and distribution functions to calculate probabilities.
\item
  Apply iterated expectations and decompose variance into within-group and between-group components.
\item
  Distinguish bias, variance, mean squared error, and consistency.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Roadmap.} We start with moments and discrete joint distributions, then move to continuous distributions. The laws of iterated expectations and total variance connect conditional and unconditional quantities. The final section introduces the main criteria used to assess estimators. The exercises in Section \ref{basics-exercises} develop these skills through calculations, proofs, and statements to assess.

\section{Random variables and moments}\label{basics-moments}

A \textbf{random variable} assigns a numerical value to each possible outcome of an experiment. Its distribution describes which values it can take and how likely they are. If a discrete random variable \(X\) takes values \(x_1,\ldots,x_m\), write
\[
p_X(x_j)=\mathbb{P}(X=x_j),\qquad \sum_{j=1}^m p_X(x_j)=1.
\]
Its expectation is the probability-weighted average
\[
\mathbb{E}(X)=\sum_{j=1}^m x_jp_X(x_j).
\]
The expectation describes the centre of the distribution. It is a population quantity: it need not be one of the possible realizations of \(X\).

The variance measures dispersion around this centre. For random variables with finite second moments,
\[
\mathbb{V}ar(X)=\mathbb{E}[(X-\mathbb{E}(X))^2],\qquad
\mathbb{C}ov(X,Y)=\mathbb{E}[(X-\mathbb{E}(X))(Y-\mathbb{E}(Y))].
\]
The covariance is positive when above-average values of \(X\) tend to occur with above-average values of \(Y\), and negative when above-average values of one variable tend to occur with below-average values of the other. Its magnitude depends on the units in which the variables are measured.

Expectation is linear:
\[
\mathbb{E}(a+bX+cY)=a+b\mathbb{E}(X)+c\mathbb{E}(Y),
\]
for constants \(a,b,c\). Independence is not required for this property. If \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent, their covariance is zero; the converse does not hold in general.

\begin{example}[Bernoulli variable]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:bernoulli-moments}{}\label{exm:bernoulli-moments}Let \(D\) equal one when a credit-card client has a recorded default payment in the following month and zero otherwise, and suppose that \(\mathbb{P}(D=1)=p\). Then
\[
\mathbb{E}(D)=p,\qquad \mathbb{V}ar(D)=p(1-p).
\]
Although each realization is either zero or one, the expectation \(p\) is the probability of default.

We use the payment-default indicator from \texttt{UCIcredit}, described in Appendix \ref{data-credit}.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
Clients & Defaults & Default share & Variance\\
\midrule
30000 & 6636 & 0.2212 & 0.1723\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

If we draw one client uniformly from these recorded clients, the probability of a default is exactly 0.2212. The variance of this finite distribution is \(p(1-p)=\) 0.1723. This calculation describes the recorded clients; interpreting their default share as the risk of a different borrower population requires additional assumptions.
\end{example}

\begin{practicebox}
\textbf{Practice:} Exercise \ref{exr:basics-covariance} develops two useful covariance identities.

\end{practicebox}

\section{Joint distributions: the discrete case}\label{basics-discrete}

A \textbf{joint distribution} describes how two random variables vary together. For discrete variables, it can be displayed as a probability table. Each cell is a joint probability; row and column totals are marginal probabilities.

\begin{example}[A joint probability table]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:commuting-table}{}\label{exm:commuting-table}Let \(X\) be daily commuting time, measured in hours, and let \(Y=1\) indicate a remote worker (\(Y=0\) indicates an on-site worker). Suppose the population distribution is:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrrrr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
& \(X=0\) & \(X=1\) & \(X=2\) & \(\mathbb{P}(Y=y)\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(Y=0\) & 0.10 & 0.20 & 0.20 & 0.50 \\
\(Y=1\) & 0.30 & 0.15 & 0.05 & 0.50 \\
\(\mathbb{P}(X=x)\) & 0.40 & 0.35 & 0.25 & 1.00 \\
\end{longtable}

For example, \(\mathbb{P}(X=2,Y=0)=0.20\). Summing the \(X=2\) column gives \(\mathbb{P}(X=2)=0.25\).
\end{example}

In general, marginal probabilities are obtained by summing the joint probabilities over the other variable:
\[
\mathbb{P}(X=x)=\sum_y\mathbb{P}(X=x,Y=y).
\]
When \(\mathbb{P}(Y=y)>0\), the conditional probability
\[
\mathbb{P}(X=x\mid Y=y)
=\frac{\mathbb{P}(X=x,Y=y)}{\mathbb{P}(Y=y)}
\]
describes the distribution of \(X\) within the subpopulation for which \(Y=y\). In Example \ref{exm:commuting-table},
\[
\mathbb{P}(X=2\mid Y=0)=\frac{0.20}{0.50}=0.40.
\]

The variables are independent when conditioning on one variable does not change the distribution of the other. Equivalently,
\[
\mathbb{P}(X=x,Y=y)=\mathbb{P}(X=x)\mathbb{P}(Y=y)
\]
for every pair \((x,y)\). The commuting variables in the example are not independent: \(\mathbb{P}(X=0\mid Y=1)=0.60\), whereas \(\mathbb{P}(X=0)=0.40\).

\subsection{Conditional expectation}\label{basics-conditional-expectation}

The \textbf{conditional expectation} \(\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y=y)\) is the mean of \(X\) within the subpopulation \(Y=y\):
\[
\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y=y)=\sum_x x\,\mathbb{P}(X=x\mid Y=y).
\]
In the commuting example,
\[
\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y=0)=1.2,\qquad
\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y=1)=0.5.
\]
The notation \(\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y)\), without a specified value of \(Y\), denotes a random variable. It equals \(1.2\) for an on-site worker and \(0.5\) for a remote worker. This distinction is important: \(\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y=y)\) is a number for a given \(y\), whereas \(\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y)\) varies across groups.

\subsection{Bayes' rule}\label{basics-bayes}

Conditional probabilities depend on the direction of conditioning. For events \(A\) and \(C\) with \(\mathbb{P}(C)>0\),
\[
\mathbb{P}(A\mid C)=\frac{\mathbb{P}(A\cap C)}{\mathbb{P}(C)}.
\]
If \(0<\mathbb{P}(A)<1\), splitting the population into \(A\) and its complement \(A^c\) yields
\[
\mathbb{P}(A\mid C)=
\frac{\mathbb{P}(C\mid A)\mathbb{P}(A)}
{\mathbb{P}(C\mid A)\mathbb{P}(A)+\mathbb{P}(C\mid A^c)\mathbb{P}(A^c)}.
\]
Bayes' rule reverses the direction of conditioning. The distinction between \(\mathbb{P}(C\mid A)\) and \(\mathbb{P}(A\mid C)\) is essential: the accuracy of a signal does not by itself tell us the probability of an underlying event after observing that signal.

\begin{practicebox}
\textbf{Practice:} Exercises \ref{exr:basics-bayes} and \ref{exr:basics-joint-probabilities} apply conditioning to a screening system and a joint probability table.

\end{practicebox}

\section{Continuous distributions}\label{basics-densities}

For discrete variables, probabilities are attached to individual values and expectations are calculated using sums. A continuous variable instead assigns probability to intervals. Individual points typically have probability zero, so the distribution is described by a cumulative distribution function and, when it exists, a density.

\subsection{Distribution and density functions}\label{distribution-and-density-functions}

\begin{definition}[Cumulative distribution function (c.d.f.)]
\protect\hypertarget{def:cdf}{}\label{def:cdf}The random variable (r.v.) \(X\) admits the cumulative distribution function \(F\) if, for all \(a\):
\[
F(a)=\mathbb{P}(X \le a).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Probability density function (p.d.f.)]
\protect\hypertarget{def:pdf}{}\label{def:pdf}A continuous random variable \(X\) admits the probability density function \(f\) if, for all \(a\) and \(b\) such that \(a<b\):
\[
\mathbb{P}(a < X \le b) = \int_{a}^{b}f(x)dx,
\]
where \(f(x) \ge 0\) for all \(x\).
\end{definition}

When \(F\) is differentiable, the density is its derivative. In particular,
\begin{equation}
f(x) = \lim_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\mathbb{P}(x < X \le x + \varepsilon)}{\varepsilon} = \lim_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{F(x + \varepsilon)-F(x)}{\varepsilon}.\label{eq:flim}
\end{equation}
and
\[
F(a) = \int_{-\infty}^{a}f(x)dx.
\]
When the relevant integrals exist, moments are calculated by weighting values with the density:
\[
\mathbb{E}(X)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x f_X(x)dx,
\qquad
\mathbb{V}ar(X)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}[x-\mathbb{E}(X)]^2f_X(x)dx.
\]
Thus, probabilities are sums in the discrete case and areas under a density in the continuous case. This \href{https://jrenne.shinyapps.io/density/}{web interface} illustrates the link between a p.d.f. and its c.d.f. Density estimation from data is a separate problem; this \href{https://www.dropbox.com/s/lxkllp9yewh98d8/Additional_Kernel.pdf?dl=0}{extra material} introduces nonparametric kernel methods.

\subsection{Joint and conditional densities}\label{joint-and-conditional-densities}

The logic of a discrete probability table carries over to continuous variables. The joint density replaces the table of cell probabilities, integration replaces summation, and a marginal density is obtained by integrating out the other variable.

\begin{definition}[Joint cumulative distribution function (c.d.f.)]
\protect\hypertarget{def:jointcdf}{}\label{def:jointcdf}The random variables \(X\) and \(Y\) admit the joint cumulative distribution function \(F_{XY}\) if, for all \(a\) and \(b\):
\[
F_{XY}(a,b)=\mathbb{P}(X \le a,Y \le b).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}

\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/essai3d2-1} \hfill{}

\caption{The volume between the horizontal plane ($z=0$) and the surface is equal to $F_{XY}(0.5,1)=\mathbb{P}(X<0.5,Y<1)$.}\label{fig:essai3d2}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[Joint probability density function (p.d.f.)]
\protect\hypertarget{def:jointpdf}{}\label{def:jointpdf}The continuous random variables \(X\) and \(Y\) admit the joint p.d.f. \(f_{XY}\), where \(f_{XY}(x,y) \ge 0\) for all \(x\) and \(y\), if:
\[
\mathbb{P}(a < X \le b,c < Y \le d) = \int_{a}^{b}\int_{c}^{d}f_{XY}(x,y)dy dx, \quad \forall a \le b,\;c \le d.
\]
\end{definition}

The marginal density is the continuous counterpart of a column or row total:
\[
f_X(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}f_{XY}(x,y)dy,
\qquad
f_Y(y)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}f_{XY}(x,y)dx.
\]

In particular, we have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
&&f_{XY}(x,y)\\
&=& \lim_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\mathbb{P}(x < X \le x + \varepsilon,y < Y \le y + \varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^2} \\
&=& \lim_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{F_{XY}(x + \varepsilon,y + \varepsilon)-F_{XY}(x,y + \varepsilon)-F_{XY}(x + \varepsilon,y)+F_{XY}(x,y)}{\varepsilon^2}.
\end{eqnarray*}

\begin{figure}

\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/essai3d-1} \hfill{}

\caption{Assume that the basis of the black column is defined by those points whose $x$-coordinates are between $x$ and $x+\varepsilon$ and $y$-coordinates are between $y$ and $y+\varepsilon$. Then the volume of the black column is equal to $\mathbb{P}(x < X \le x+\varepsilon,y < Y \le y+\varepsilon)$, which is approximately equal to $f_{XY}(x,y)\varepsilon^2$ if $\varepsilon$ is small.}\label{fig:essai3d}
\end{figure}

Figure \ref{fig:figFXYdecompo} illustrates why we have
\begin{eqnarray*}
&&\mathbb{P}(x < X \le x + \varepsilon,y < Y \le y + \varepsilon)\\
&=& F_{XY}(x + \varepsilon,y + \varepsilon)-F_{XY}(x,y + \varepsilon)-F_{XY}(x + \varepsilon,y)+F_{XY}(x,y).
\end{eqnarray*}
Let us denote by \(P_\Omega\) the probability that the point of coordinate \((X,Y)\) is included in the \(\Omega\) area. We have:
\[
P_{B} = P_{B+C+D+E} - P_{E+D} - P_{E+C} + P_E,
\]
which implies that
\begin{eqnarray*}
&&\mathbb{P}(x<X\le x+ \varepsilon,y<Y\le y+ \varepsilon) \\
&=& \mathbb{P}(X\le x+ \varepsilon,Y\le y+ \varepsilon)  - \mathbb{P}(X\le x+ \varepsilon,Y\le y)  - \mathbb{P}(X\le x,Y\le y+ \varepsilon)\\
&&+ \mathbb{P}(X\le x,Y\le y).
\end{eqnarray*}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/figFXYdecompo-1} 

}

\caption{Recovering a rectangle probability from the joint CDF. The highlighted region B is obtained by subtracting C + E and D + E from B + C + D + E, then adding E back because it was subtracted twice. Regions C and E continue indefinitely to the left; D and E continue indefinitely downwards.}\label{fig:figFXYdecompo}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[Conditional probability density function]
\protect\hypertarget{def:condcdf}{}\label{def:condcdf}If \(X\) and \(Y\) are continuous random variables and \(f_Y(y)>0\), the conditional density of \(X\) given \(Y=y\) is denoted by \(f_{X|Y}(x,y)\).

It describes the distribution of \(X\) within the subpopulation characterized by \(Y=y\). The associated conditional expectation is
\[
\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y=y)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x f_{X|Y}(x,y)dx.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional density]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:conditional-density-formula}{}\label{prp:conditional-density-formula}We have
\[
f_{X|Y}(x,y)=\frac{f_{XY}(x,y)}{f_Y(y)}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Condition on a shrinking interval around \(y\). Then
\begin{eqnarray*}
f_{X|Y}(x,y)
&=&\lim_{\varepsilon,\delta \rightarrow 0}
\frac{\mathbb{P}(x<X\le x+\varepsilon\mid y<Y\le y+\delta)}{\varepsilon}\\
&=&\lim_{\varepsilon,\delta \rightarrow 0}
\frac{\mathbb{P}(x<X\le x+\varepsilon,y<Y\le y+\delta)}
{\varepsilon\,\mathbb{P}(y<Y\le y+\delta)}\\
&=&\frac{f_{XY}(x,y)}{f_Y(y)}.
\end{eqnarray*}
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Independent random variables]
\protect\hypertarget{def:independent}{}\label{def:independent}Consider two r.v., \(X\) and \(Y\), with respective c.d.f. \(F_X\) and \(F_Y\), and respective p.d.f. \(f_X\) and \(f_Y\).

These random variables are independent if and only if (iff) the joint c.d.f. of \(X\) and \(Y\) (see Def. \ref{def:jointcdf}) is given by:
\[
F_{XY}(x,y) = F_{X}(x) \times F_{Y}(y),
\]
or, equivalently, iff the joint p.d.f. of \((X,Y)\) (see Def. \ref{def:jointpdf}) is given by:
\[
f_{XY}(x,y) = f_{X}(x) \times f_{Y}(y).
\]
\end{definition}

We have the following:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  If \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent, \(f_{X|Y}(x,y)=f_{X}(x)\). This implies, in particular, that \(\mathbb{E}(g(X)|Y)=\mathbb{E}(g(X))\), where \(g\) is any function.
\item
  If \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent, then \(\mathbb{E}(g(X)h(Y))=\mathbb{E}(g(X))\mathbb{E}(h(Y))\) and \(\mathbb{C}ov(g(X),h(Y))=0\), where \(g\) and \(h\) are any functions.
\end{enumerate}

It is important to note that the absence of correlation between two variables is not a sufficient condition to have independence. Consider for instance the case where \(X=Y^2\), with \(Y \sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)\). In this case, we have \(\mathbb{C}ov(X,Y)=0\), but \(X\) and \(Y\) are not independent. Indeed, we have for instance \(\mathbb{E}(Y^2 \times X)=3\), which is not equal to \(\mathbb{E}(Y^2) \times \mathbb{E}(X)=1\). (If \(X\) and \(Y\) were independent, we should have \(\mathbb{E}(Y^2 \times X)=\mathbb{E}(Y^2) \times \mathbb{E}(X)\) according to point 2 above.)

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}

The correspondence between the two settings can be summarized as follows:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Concept
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Discrete variables
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Continuous variables
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Distribution & probabilities \(p_X(x)\) & density \(f_X(x)\) \\
Marginalization & sum joint probabilities & integrate the joint density \\
Conditioning & divide by a marginal probability & divide by a marginal density \\
Expectation & probability-weighted sum & density-weighted integral \\
\end{longtable}

\end{practicebox}

\section{Law of iterated expectations}\label{basics-iterated-expectations}

The conditional means describe different subpopulations. To recover the population mean, we average those conditional means using the population shares of the groups.

\begin{proposition}[Law of iterated expectations]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:lawiteratedexpect}{}\label{prp:lawiteratedexpect}If \(X\) and \(Y\) are two random variables and if \(\mathbb{E}(|X|)<\infty\), we have:
\[
\boxed{\mathbb{E}(X) = \mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|Y)).}
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Suppose first that both \(X\) and \(Y\) are discrete. Partitioning the population according to the possible values of \(Y\) gives
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{E}(X)
&=&\sum_x x\mathbb{P}(X=x)\\
&=&\sum_x x\sum_y\mathbb{P}(X=x\mid Y=y)\mathbb{P}(Y=y)\\
&=&\sum_y\left[\sum_x x\mathbb{P}(X=x\mid Y=y)\right]\mathbb{P}(Y=y)\\
&=&\sum_y\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y=y)\mathbb{P}(Y=y)
=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y)].
\end{eqnarray*}
For continuous \(Y\), the same argument applies with integrals replacing sums.
\end{proof}

In Example \ref{exm:commuting-table}, the population mean commuting time is therefore
\[
\mathbb{E}(X)
=1.2\times 0.5+0.5\times 0.5
=0.85.
\]
We obtain the same value directly from the marginal distribution of \(X\):
\[
\mathbb{E}(X)=0\times0.40+1\times0.35+2\times0.25=0.85.
\]

The first calculation averages across groups; the second averages across commuting-time outcomes. The law of iterated expectations states that these two routes must agree.

\begin{example}[Mixture of Gaussian distributions]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:mixture}{}\label{exm:mixture}By definition, \(X\) is drawn from a mixture of Gaussian distributions if:
\[
X = \color{blue}{B \times Y_1} + \color{red}{(1-B) \times Y_2},
\]
where \(B\), \(Y_1\) and \(Y_2\) are three independent variables drawn as follows:
\[
B \sim \mbox{Bernoulli}(p),\quad Y_1 \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_1,\sigma_1^2), \quad \mbox{and}\quad Y_2 \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_2,\sigma_2^2).
\]

Figure \ref{fig:mixtureG} displays the densities of three Gaussian mixtures. This \href{https://jrenne.shinyapps.io/density/}{web interface} can be used to explore other parameter values.

\begin{figure}

\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/mixtureG-1} \hfill{}

\caption{Example of pdfs of mixtures of Gaussian distributions.}\label{fig:mixtureG}
\end{figure}

The law of iterated expectations gives:
\[
\mathbb{E}(X) = \mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|B)) = \mathbb{E}(B\mu_1+(1-B)\mu_2)=p\mu_1 + (1-p)\mu_2.
\]
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Buffon (1733)'s needles]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:Buffon}{}\label{exm:Buffon}Suppose we have a floor made of parallel strips of wood, each the same width {[}\(w=1\){]}. We drop a needle, of length \(1/2\), onto the floor. What is the probability that the needle crosses the grooves of the floor?

Let's define the random variable \(X\) by
\[
X = \left\{
\begin{array}{cl}
1 & \mbox{if the needle crosses a line}\\
0 & \mbox{otherwise.}
\end{array}
\right.
\]
Let \(d\) be the distance from the needle's left endpoint to the groove on its left, and let \(\theta\) be the angle with the horizontal, oriented towards the right. Conditional on \(\theta\), the needle's horizontal projection has length \(\ell\cos(\theta)\), where \(\ell=1/2\). It crosses the right groove when \(d+\ell\cos(\theta)\geq w\). Since \(d\) is uniform on \([0,w]\) and independent of \(\theta\),
\[
\mathbb{E}(X\mid\theta)=\mathbb{P}(X=1\mid\theta)=\frac{\ell\cos(\theta)}{w}=\frac{\cos(\theta)}{2}.
\]
Figure \ref{fig:Buffon} shows the geometry and the interval of endpoint positions that yield a crossing.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/Buffon-1} 

}

\caption{Buffon's needle: geometry of a crossing. The shaded strip marks the positions of the left endpoint for which the needle crosses the right groove, at the illustrated angle. Its width is the horizontal projection of the needle, so the crossing probability is this width divided by the strip width w.}\label{fig:Buffon}
\end{figure}

It is reasonable to assume that \(\theta\) is uniformly distributed on \([-\pi/2,\pi/2]\), therefore:
\[
\mathbb{E}(X)=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|\theta))=\mathbb{E}(\cos(\theta)/2)=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\frac{1}{2}\cos(\theta)\left(\frac{d\theta}{\pi}\right)=\frac{1}{\pi}.
\]

This \href{https://jrenne.shinyapps.io/StatEcoII/}{web interface} simulates the experiment (select the worksheet ``Buffon's needles'').
\end{example}

\begin{practicebox}
\textbf{Practice:} Exercise \ref{exr:basics-random-bernoulli} distinguishes conditional and unconditional moments.

\end{practicebox}

\section{Law of total variance}\label{basics-total-variance}

The population variance also has a useful group interpretation. It combines average dispersion \textbf{within} groups and dispersion \textbf{between} group means.

\begin{proposition}[Law of total variance]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:lawtotalvariance}{}\label{prp:lawtotalvariance}If \(X\) and \(Y\) are two random variables and if the variance of \(X\) is finite, we have:
\[
\boxed{\mathbb{V}ar(X) = \mathbb{E}(\mathbb{V}ar(X|Y)) + \mathbb{V}ar(\mathbb{E}(X|Y)).}
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{V}ar(X) &=& \mathbb{E}(X^2) - \mathbb{E}(X)^2\\
&=& \mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X^2|Y)) - \mathbb{E}(X)^2\\
&=& \mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X^2|Y) \color{blue}{- \mathbb{E}(X|Y)^2}) +  \color{blue}{\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|Y)^2)}  - \color{red}{\mathbb{E}(X)^2}\\
&=& \mathbb{E}(\underbrace{\mathbb{E}(X^2|Y) - \mathbb{E}(X|Y)^2}_{\mathbb{V}ar(X|Y)}) + \underbrace{\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|Y)^2) - \color{red}{\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|Y))^2}}_{\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbb{E}(X|Y))}.
\end{eqnarray*}
\end{proof}

Returning to Example \ref{exm:commuting-table}, one obtains
\[
\mathbb{V}ar(X\mid Y=0)=0.56,\qquad
\mathbb{V}ar(X\mid Y=1)=0.45.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}ar(X\mid Y)]}_{\text{within-group variation}}
=0.505,
\qquad
\underbrace{\mathbb{V}ar[\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y)]}_{\text{between-group variation}}
=0.1225.
\]
The total variance is \(0.505+0.1225=0.6275\). This decomposition is often useful in economics because it separates heterogeneity within observable groups from differences in their average outcomes.

\begin{example}[Default probabilities across age groups]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:credit-total-variance}{}\label{exm:credit-total-variance}Return to the credit-card clients of Example \ref{exm:bernoulli-moments}. Let \(D\) denote the recorded payment-default indicator and let \(G\) identify three age groups. We continue to treat a uniformly drawn recorded client as our probability experiment.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Age group & Clients & Default share & Weight\\
\midrule
35 or younger & 16809 & 0.2140 & 0.5603\\
36 to 50 & 10922 & 0.2254 & 0.3641\\
Over 50 & 2269 & 0.2543 & 0.0756\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

Within group \(g\), \(D\) is Bernoulli with default probability \(p_g\). Iterated expectations gives \(p=\sum_g w_gp_g\), where \(w_g\) is the group's share of clients. Total variance then gives
\[
p(1-p)=\underbrace{\sum_g w_gp_g(1-p_g)}_{\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}ar(D\mid G)]\;\text{(within groups)}}
+\underbrace{\sum_g w_g(p_g-p)^2}_{\mathbb{V}ar[\mathbb{E}(D\mid G)]\;\text{(between groups)}}.
\]

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
Default share & Within & Between & Sum & Direct variance\\
\midrule
0.2212 & 0.17215 & 0.00012 & 0.17227 & 0.17227\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The first term averages the conditional variances across age groups: it is the expectation of a variance. The second term measures how the conditional means vary across age groups: it is the variance of an expectation. In the table, these are labelled Within and Between, respectively. The two calculations of total variance agree because they describe the same finite distribution. Differences between these age groups are descriptive; they do not establish that changing a client's age would change their default risk by the observed difference.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Mixture of Gaussian distributions (cont'd)]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:mixture2}{}\label{exm:mixture2}Consider the case of a mixture of Gaussian distributions (Example \ref{exm:mixture}). We have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{V}ar(X) &=& \color{blue}{\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{V}ar(X|B))} + \color{red}{\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbb{E}(X|B))}\\
&=&  \color{blue}{p\sigma_1^2+(1-p)\sigma_2^2} + \color{red}{p(1-p)(\mu_1 - \mu_2)^2}.
\end{eqnarray*}
\end{example}

\begin{practicebox}
\textbf{Practice:} Exercise \ref{exr:basics-total-variance} separates within-group and between-group variation.

\end{practicebox}

\section{Bias, accuracy, and consistency}\label{basics-estimators}

The quantities introduced so far---means, variances, and probabilities---describe a population. In applications, these quantities are usually unknown and must be estimated from a sample.

Let \(X_1,\ldots,X_n\) be an independent and identically distributed sample from a population with mean \(\mu\) and finite variance \(\sigma^2\). The sample mean
\[
\overline X_n=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i
\]
is an estimator of \(\mu\). It satisfies
\[
\mathbb{E}(\overline X_n)=\mu,
\qquad
\mathbb{V}ar(\overline X_n)=\frac{\sigma^2}{n}.
\]
Thus its distribution is centred on the population mean and becomes increasingly concentrated as the sample grows. This example anticipates three properties used to evaluate estimators: bias, precision, and consistency.

More generally, let \(\widehat\theta\) estimate a fixed parameter \(\theta\). Its \textbf{bias} is \(\mathbb{E}(\widehat\theta)-\theta\); an unbiased estimator has zero bias. When its second moment is finite, its \textbf{mean squared error} satisfies
\[
\operatorname{MSE}(\widehat\theta)=\mathbb{E}[(\widehat\theta-\theta)^2]
=\mathbb{V}ar(\widehat\theta)+\operatorname{Bias}(\widehat\theta)^2.
\]
A small variance means the estimator is concentrated around its own mean; it does not guarantee that this mean is close to the parameter. Exercise \ref{exr:basics-estimator-properties} derives this decomposition. Consistency concerns a different question: what happens as the sample grows?

Econometric parameters include causal effects, elasticities, distributional parameters, and preference parameters such as risk aversion. Except in degenerate cases, an estimate differs from its population target in any particular sample. \textbf{Consistency} requires the estimator to approach that target as the sample size increases.

Denote by \(\hat\theta_n\) the estimate of \(\theta\) based on a sample of size \(n\). We say that \(\hat\theta\) is a consistent estimator of \(\theta\) if, for any \(\varepsilon>0\) (even if very small), the probability that \(\hat\theta_n\) is in \([\theta - \varepsilon,\theta + \varepsilon]\) goes to 1 when \(n\) goes to \(\infty\). Formally:
\[
\lim_{n \rightarrow + \infty} \mathbb{P}\left(\hat\theta_n \in [\theta - \varepsilon,\theta + \varepsilon]\right) = 1.
\]

That is, \(\hat\theta\) is a consistent estimator if \(\hat\theta_n\) \emph{converges in probability} (Def. \ref{def:convergenceproba}) to \(\theta\). The sample mean above is consistent when the observations have a finite variance, because \(\mathbb{V}ar(\overline X_n)=\sigma^2/n\) converges to zero. There are also other types of stochastic convergence.\footnote{Appendix \ref{StochConvergences} notably provides the definitions of convergence in distribution (Def. \ref{def:cvgceDistri}) and mean-square convergence (Def. \ref{def:convergenceLr}).}

\begin{example}[Example of non-convergent estimator]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:NonConsist}{}\label{exm:NonConsist}

Assume that \(X_i \sim i.i.d. \mbox{Cauchy}\) with a location parameter of 1 and a scale parameter of 1 (Def. \ref{def:Cauchy}). The sample mean \(\bar{X}_n = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} X_i\) does not converge in probability. This is because a Cauchy distribution has no mean; hence the law of large numbers (Theorem \ref{thm:LLN}) does not apply.

The realization below is especially deceptive. For several thousand observations, the sample mean appears to settle near the location parameter 1. But observation 4,495 is approximately 75,073: it moves the sample mean from about 1.01 to 17.71. Increasing the sample size has not protected the sample mean against a new extreme Cauchy draw.

\begin{figure}

\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/figCauchy-1} \hfill{}

\caption{Simulation of $\bar{X}_n$ when $X_i \sim i.i.d. \mbox{Cauchy}$.}\label{fig:figCauchy}
\end{figure}

\end{example}

\Needspace{12\baselineskip}

\section{Chapter recap}\label{basics-recap}

\begin{recapbox}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Moments summarize the location, dispersion, and co-movement of random variables; Section \ref{basics-moments} introduces expectation, variance, and covariance.
\item
  A joint distribution determines marginal and conditional distributions. Example \ref{exm:commuting-table} illustrates these operations for discrete variables, while Proposition \ref{prp:conditional-density-formula} gives the continuous conditional-density formula.
\item
  Bayes' rule in Section \ref{basics-bayes} reverses the direction of conditioning; Definition \ref{def:independent} states when conditioning leaves a distribution unchanged.
\item
  Proposition \ref{prp:lawiteratedexpect} recovers a population mean by averaging conditional means.
\item
  Proposition \ref{prp:lawtotalvariance} decomposes population variance into within-group and between-group components.
\item
  Section \ref{basics-estimators} distinguishes finite-sample bias and precision from consistency as the sample size grows.
\end{itemize}

\end{recapbox}

\clearpage

\section{Exercises}\label{basics-exercises}

Attempt each question and explain your reasoning, including for true-or-false statements. \textbf{Foundation} exercises apply a result directly; \textbf{Standard} exercises require several steps or a short proof; \textbf{Advanced} exercises combine several results or require a longer derivation. Worked solutions and exercise-specific hints are reserved for teaching sessions and are not included in this student edition.

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Covariance identities]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:basics-covariance}{}\label{exr:basics-covariance}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{basics-moments}.

Let \(X,Y,V\) have finite second moments, and let \(a,b,c\) be constants. Write \(\mu_X=\mathbb{E}(X)\) and \(\sigma_{XY}=\mathbb{C}ov(X,Y)\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Show that \(\mathbb{C}ov(a+bX+cV,Y)=b\sigma_{XY}+c\sigma_{VY}\).
\item
  Show that \(\mathbb{E}(XY)=\mathbb{C}ov(X,Y)+\mathbb{E}(X)\mathbb{E}(Y)\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Bayes rule and facial recognition]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:basics-bayes}{}\label{exr:basics-bayes}

\textbf{Foundation \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{basics-bayes}.

A security system grants access to an authorized person with probability \(0.99\), and to an unauthorized person with probability \(0.02\). Of those attempting to enter, \(5\%\) are unauthorized.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Given that a person is granted access, what is the probability that the person is authorized?
\item
  Given that a person is denied access, what is the probability that the person is unauthorized?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Within-group and between-group variation]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:basics-total-variance}{}\label{exr:basics-total-variance}

\textbf{Foundation \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{basics-total-variance}.

Let \(X\) be daily time spent on social media (in hours), and let \(Y\) indicate age group. The population has the following characteristics:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrrr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Age group & Share & Mean (h) & Variance (\(\mathrm{h}^2\)) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Under 30 & 0.6 & 4 & 1.5 \\
30 or above & 0.4 & 2 & 0.5 \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate \(\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}ar(X\mid Y)]\).
\item
  Calculate \(\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y)]\).
\item
  Calculate \(\mathbb{V}ar[\mathbb{E}(X\mid Y)]\).
\item
  Use the law of total variance to obtain \(\mathbb{V}ar(X)\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Conditioning above a quantile]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:basics-quantiles}{}\label{exr:basics-quantiles}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{basics-densities}.

Let \(X\) have a continuous density \(f\) and cumulative distribution function \(F\). For \(0<\alpha<1\), let \(q_\alpha\) satisfy \(F(q_\alpha)=\alpha\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  How can \(f\) be obtained from \(F\)?
\item
  Derive the c.d.f. \(F_\alpha\) of \(X\) conditional on \(X>q_\alpha\). State its value both below and above \(q_\alpha\).
\item
  Derive the corresponding density \(f_\alpha\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Bias, variance, and mean squared error]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:basics-estimator-properties}{}\label{exr:basics-estimator-properties}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{basics-estimators}.

Let \(\widehat\theta\) estimate a fixed parameter \(\theta\), and suppose \(\widehat\theta\) has a finite second moment.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  What property is expressed by \(\mathbb{E}(\widehat\theta)=\theta\)?
\item
  Express \(\mathbb{E}[(\widehat\theta-\theta)^2]\) in terms of the variance and bias of \(\widehat\theta\).
\item
  Does a small variance necessarily imply a small mean squared error? Explain.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Employment, residence, and independence]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:basics-joint-probabilities}{}\label{exr:basics-joint-probabilities}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{basics-discrete}.

Let \(X\) indicate employment status (\(0\): employed; \(1\): self-employed), and let \(Y\) indicate residence (\(0\): countryside; \(1\): city). Their joint probabilities are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
& \(Y=0\) & \(Y=1\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(X=0\) & 0.10 & 0.30 \\
\(X=1\) & 0.15 & 0.45 \\
\end{longtable}

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(X)=0.7\).
\item
  \(X\) and \(Y\) are not independent.
\item
  \(\mathbb{V}ar(X)=\mathbb{V}ar(X\mid Y=0)+\mathbb{V}ar(X\mid Y=1)\).
\item
  \(\mathbb{V}ar(X\mid Y=0)=0.24\).
\item
  Bayes' rule implies
  \[
  \begin{gathered}
  \mathbb{P}(X=0\mid Y=0)\\
  =\frac{\mathbb{P}(Y=0\mid X=0)\mathbb{P}(X=0)}
  {\mathbb{P}(Y=0\mid X=0)\mathbb{P}(X=0)+\mathbb{P}(Y=0\mid X=1)\mathbb{P}(X=1)}.
  \end{gathered}
  \]
\item
  The correlation between \(X\) and \(Y\) is strictly positive.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[A Bernoulli variable with a random success probability]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:basics-random-bernoulli}{}\label{exr:basics-random-bernoulli}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{basics-iterated-expectations}.

Let \(U\) be uniform on \([0,1]\), and suppose \(Y\mid U\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(U)\).

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(Y\mid U)=U\).
\item
  \(\mathbb{V}ar(Y\mid U)=U^2\).
\item
  \(\mathbb{V}ar(Y)=1/4\).
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(Y)=1/4\).
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(Y+U)=2U\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\chapter{Central Limit Theorem}\label{TCL}

The law of large numbers (LLN) and the central limit theorem (CLT) answer two different questions about sample averages. The LLN explains why a sample mean approaches its population counterpart. The CLT describes the remaining estimation error and thereby makes confidence intervals and tests possible. Under the conditions used in this chapter, that error is approximately Gaussian in large samples even when the observations themselves are not.

\textbf{Prerequisites.} Expectations and variances, independent random variables, sample means, and convergence in probability.

\textbf{After working through this chapter, you should be able to:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Explain the distinct roles of the law of large numbers and the central limit theorem.
\item
  Derive large-sample approximations for sums and sample means.
\item
  Standardize a statistic and use normal quantiles to approximate probabilities.
\item
  Compare means from two independent samples.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Roadmap.} The chapter first establishes why sample averages converge, then studies their large-sample distribution. It concludes by applying these results to differences between independent sample means and to the exercises in Section \ref{clt-exercises}.

\section{Law of large numbers}\label{law-of-large-numbers}

\begin{definition}[Sample and Population]
\protect\hypertarget{def:smplpop}{}\label{def:smplpop}In statistics, a \emph{sample} refers to a finite set of observations drawn from a \emph{population}.
\end{definition}

Empirical work usually relies on a sample because observing an entire population is impractical. A natural estimator of a population mean is the sample average of \(\{x_1,\dots,x_n\}\):
\[
\overline{x}_n = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i.
\]
This sample mean is an estimator of the true (population) mean \(\mathbb{E}(x_i)\) (assuming the latter exists). According to the law of large numbers (Theorem \ref{thm:LLN}), this estimator is \emph{consistent}. (see Def. \ref{def:convergenceproba} for the definition of convergence in probability.)

\begin{theorem}[Law of large numbers]
\protect\hypertarget{thm:LLN}{}\label{thm:LLN}If the random variables \(x_i\) are i.i.d. and \(\mathbb{E}(|x_i|)<\infty\), then the sample mean (\(\overline{x}_n\)) is a consistent estimator of the population mean \(\mu=\mathbb{E}(x_i)\). In other words, the sample mean converges in probability to the population mean.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
See Appendix \ref{CLTappend}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Averages of credit limits]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:creditLimits}{}\label{exm:creditLimits}Consider the credit limits recorded in \texttt{UCIcredit}, described in Appendix \ref{data-credit}. A \textbf{credit limit} is the maximum amount a client is authorised to borrow through their credit card; it is distinct from actual spending or the outstanding balance. In this dataset, it includes individual and supplementary family credit. For example, a limit of 200 thousand NTD represents an authorised credit line of NTD200,000. Figure \ref{fig:clt-credit-distribution} shows their distribution, expressed in thousands of New Taiwan dollars (NTD).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/clt-credit-distribution-1} 

}

\caption{Recorded credit limits in UCIcredit. The dashed line marks the mean of the 30,000 recorded values.}\label{fig:clt-credit-distribution}
\end{figure}

To illustrate the LLN, define a sampling experiment: draw a client uniformly from these 30,000 records, note the credit limit, replace the record, and repeat independently. The fixed dataset is the population for this experiment, so its mean \(\mu\) and variance \(\sigma^2\) are known. They are not estimates of the corresponding quantities for all cardholders.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
Records & Mean & Variance & SD\\
\midrule
30000 & 167.484 & 16833.9 & 129.745\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The mean and standard deviation are in thousand NTD; the variance is in squared thousand NTD.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/clt-credit-running-mean-1} 

}

\caption{A running sample mean from 2,000 independent draws with replacement. The horizontal dashed line is the known mean of the recorded credit limits. The LLN concerns convergence, not a monotonic reduction in the error along every path.}\label{fig:clt-credit-running-mean}
\end{figure}

Sampling with replacement makes the draws i.i.d., even though the records themselves need not form a random sample of a wider population. The LLN then applies directly to this experiment.
\end{example}

Consistency tells us that the approximation improves with the sample size, but it does not yet quantify the error in a given sample. We first obtain a probability bound and then turn to the richer approximation supplied by the CLT.

A first answer is provided by the Chebyshev inequality. Assume the \(x_i\)'s are independently drawn from a distribution of mean \(\mu\) and variance \(\sigma^2\). We have:
\[
\mathbb{E}(\bar{x}_n) = \mu \quad \mbox{ and } \quad \mathbb{V}ar(\mu - \bar{x}_n) = \frac{1}{n}\sigma^2 \underset{n \rightarrow \infty}{\rightarrow} 0.
\]
The Chebyshev inequality (Proposition \ref{prp:chebychev}) states that, for any \(\varepsilon > 0\) (even very small), we have:
\[
\mathbb{P}(|\bar{x}_n-\mu|>\varepsilon) \le \frac{\mathbb{V}ar(\mu - \bar{x}_n)}{\varepsilon^2} = \frac{\sigma^2}{n\varepsilon^2} \underset{n \rightarrow \infty}{\rightarrow} 0.
\]
For the sampling experiment in Example \ref{exm:creditLimits}, take \(n=2{,}000\) independent draws and \(\varepsilon=10\), corresponding to NTD10,000. Using the known variance of the recorded values, Chebyshev's inequality gives
\[
\mathbb{P}(|\bar{x}_n-\mu|>10)\leq\frac{\sigma^2}{2{,}000\times10^2}
=\text{0.0842}.
\]
This is a probability over repeated samples from the fixed dataset. It is an exact upper bound, not a normal approximation or a bound obtained by substituting an estimated variance.

However, this only gives bounds for such probabilities. The central limit theorem provides richer information, as it gives the approximate distribution of the estimation error.

\section{Central Limit Theorem (CLT)}\label{central-limit-theorem-clt}

\begin{theorem}[Lindeberg–Lévy central limit theorem, CLT]
\protect\hypertarget{thm:LindbergLevyCLT}{}\label{thm:LindbergLevyCLT}If \(x_n\) is an i.i.d. sequence of random variables with mean \(\mu\) and (finite) variance \(\sigma^2\), then:
\[
\boxed{\sqrt{n} (\bar{x}_n - \mu) \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2), \quad \mbox{where} \quad \bar{x}_n = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} x_i.}
\]
{[}``\(\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\)'' denotes the convergence in distribution (see Def. \ref{def:cvgceDistri}){]}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
See Appendix \ref{CLTappend}.
\end{proof}

According to the CLT, when \(n\) is large, the distribution of the error \(\bar{x}_n-\mu\) is approximately normal, whatever the distribution of the \(x_i\)'s, provided that their mean \(\mu\) and variance \(\sigma^2\) exist. Its approximate mean is zero and its standard deviation is \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\). (We then say that the convergence rate is \emph{in \(\sqrt{n}\)}.) Quantiles of the normal distribution can therefore be used to compute approximate confidence intervals for \(\mu\) from the sample \(\{x_1,\dots,x_n\}\).

\begin{example}[The sampling distribution of a credit-limit average]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:creditCLT}{}\label{exm:creditCLT}Return to the sampling experiment in Example \ref{exm:creditLimits}. We repeat it 2,000 times, drawing 200 clients with replacement each time. For every sample we compute
\[
Z=\frac{\sqrt{n}(\bar{x}_n-\mu)}{\sigma},\qquad n=200,
\]
using the known mean and standard deviation of the fixed population. Although the individual credit limits are right-skewed, the distribution of their standardized average is already close to the standard normal density in Figure \ref{fig:clt-credit-sampling}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/clt-credit-sampling-1} 

}

\caption{Standardized means of credit limits: 2,000 independent samples of 200 draws with replacement from UCIcredit. The curve is the standard normal density.}\label{fig:clt-credit-sampling}
\end{figure}

The number of repetitions determines how smoothly we can display the sampling distribution. The number of draws in each sample determines the quality of the CLT approximation; these are two different quantities.
\end{example}

This \href{https://jrenne.shinyapps.io/StatEcoII/}{web interface} illustrates the CLT with simulations (select the ``CLT'' block).

The CL theorem was first postulated by the mathematician Abraham de Moivre. In an article published in 1733, he used the normal distribution to approximate the distribution of the number of heads resulting from many tosses of a fair coin. This finding was nearly forgotten until Pierre-Simon Laplace recalled it in his \href{https://archive.org/details/thorieanalytiqu01laplgoog}{Théorie analytique des probabilités}, published in 1812. Laplace expanded De Moivre's finding by approximating the binomial distribution with the normal distribution.

\textbf{Practice:} Exercises \ref{exr:clt-buffon-precision} and \ref{exr:clt-coin-normal-quantiles} use the CLT to design a simulation and to determine its required precision.

The CLT can be extended to the case where each \(x_i\) is a vector. (In that case, and if the dimension of \(x_i\) is \(m\), then \(\mathbb{V}ar(x_i)\) is a \(m\times m\) matrix.) Here is the multivariate CLT:

\begin{theorem}[Multivariate Central limit theorem, CLT]
\protect\hypertarget{thm:MCLT}{}\label{thm:MCLT}If \(\mathbf{x}_n\) is an i.i.d. sequence of \(m\)-dimensional random variables with mean \(\boldsymbol\mu\) and variance \(\Sigma\), where \(\Sigma\) is a positive definite matrix, then:
\[
\boxed{\sqrt{n} (\bar{\mathbf{x}}_n - \boldsymbol\mu) \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Sigma), \quad \mbox{where} \quad \bar{\mathbf{x}}_n = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \mathbf{x}_i.}
\]
\end{theorem}

\section{Comparison of sample means}\label{comparison-means}

The one-sample CLT extends directly to comparisons between populations. The key object is then the difference between two independent sample means.

Consider two independent samples \(\{m_1,\dots,m_{n_m}\}\) and \(\{w_1,\dots,w_{n_w}\}\). Assume that the \(m_i\)'s are i.i.d. with mean \(\mu_m\) and variance \(\sigma_m^2\), and that the \(w_i\)'s are i.i.d. with mean \(\mu_w\) and variance \(\sigma_w^2\).

If \(n_m\) and \(n_w\) are large, the CLT gives the approximations:
\[
\sqrt{n_m}(\bar{m} - \mu_m) \overset{a}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2_m) \quad \mbox{and} \quad \sqrt{n_w}(\bar{w} - \mu_w) \overset{a}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2_w),
\]
or
\[
\bar{m} \overset{a}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\left(\mu_m,\frac{\sigma^2_m}{n_m}\right) \quad \mbox{and} \quad \bar{w} \overset{a}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\left(\mu_w,\frac{\sigma^2_w}{n_w}\right).
\]

Since the two samples are independent, \(\bar{m}\) and \(\bar{w}\) are independent, and we get:
\[
\bar{m} - \bar{w} \overset{a}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\left(\mu_m-\mu_w,\tau^2\right) \quad \mbox{with} \quad \tau^2 = \frac{\sigma^2_m}{n_m}+\frac{\sigma^2_w}{n_w}.
\]
This can be used to test the null hypothesis \(H_0: \; \mu_m-\mu_w = 0\) (see Section \ref{Tests}). In practice, the unknown population variances are replaced by their sample counterparts, giving the estimated standard error \(\hat\tau\). Under \(H_0\), the probability that \(\bar{m}-\bar{w}\) lies in \([-1.96\hat\tau,1.96\hat\tau]\) (respectively \([-2.58\hat\tau,2.58\hat\tau]\)) is approximately 0.95 (respectively 0.99) in large samples {[}see Table \ref{tab:Normal}{]}.

For instance, if \(\bar{m}-\bar{w}\) is not in \([-2.58\hat\tau,2.58\hat\tau]\), then we reject the null hypothesis at the 1\% significance level.

\begin{example}[Comparing default shares across age groups]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:comparSmplMeans}{}\label{exm:comparSmplMeans}

Do recorded default frequencies differ between clients aged 35 or younger and clients over 35? Use the next-month payment-default indicator \(D\) in \texttt{UCIcredit} (Appendix \ref{data-credit}). It equals one for a recorded default and zero otherwise. The mean of a binary variable is its proportion of ones, so the comparison-of-means framework applies directly to default shares.

As in the preceding formulas, let \(m\) denote the older group and \(w\) the younger group. Their means are the corresponding default probabilities: \(\mu_m=p_m\) and \(\mu_w=p_w\). We estimate them by the observed shares \(\widehat p_m\) and \(\widehat p_w\).

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Age group & Clients & Recorded defaults & Default share (\%)\\
\midrule
35 or younger & 16809 & 3597 & 21.40\\
Over 35 & 13191 & 3039 & 23.04\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

We treat the clients as independent observations within and across groups. Unlike the preceding simulation, this is a working sampling assumption about the source data, rather than a property guaranteed by our drawing procedure. The groups can also differ in characteristics other than age.

Estimate \(p_m-p_w\) by \(\widehat p_m-\widehat p_w\). The estimated standard error remains
\[
\widehat\tau=\sqrt{\frac{s_m^2}{n_m}+\frac{s_w^2}{n_w}}.
\]
Here the population variance within group \(g\) is \(p_g(1-p_g)\); its sample counterpart is \(s_g^2=\frac{n_g}{n_g-1}\widehat p_g(1-\widehat p_g)\). We test \(H_0:p_m=p_w\) against \(H_1:p_m\ne p_w\) using \(z=(\widehat p_m-\widehat p_w)/\widehat\tau\).

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrr}
\toprule
Difference (pp) & SE (pp) & z & 95\% lower (pp) & 95\% upper (pp) & p-value\\
\midrule
1.64 & 0.48 & 3.39 & 0.69 & 2.59 & <0.001\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The older group's recorded default share is \textbf{1.64 percentage points higher}, with an estimated standard error of \textbf{0.48 percentage points}. The approximate 95\% confidence interval for the difference is \textbf{{[}0.69, 2.59{]} percentage points}. This is a difference between probabilities, not a percentage change in risk.

The standardized difference is \(z=\) 3.39, with a two-sided p-value of 0.00071. We reject equality at both the 5\% and 1\% levels under the independent-sampling model. Figure \ref{fig:RcompMeans} displays the shares and their difference; the interval for the difference excludes zero.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/RcompMeans-1} 

}

\caption{Recorded next-month default shares by age group in UCIcredit (left) and the older-minus-younger difference (right). Horizontal segments are approximate 95\% confidence intervals under independent sampling. The dashed vertical line marks equal default probabilities.}\label{fig:RcompMeans}
\end{figure}

\end{example}

\Needspace{11\baselineskip}

\section{Chapter recap}\label{clt-recap}

\begin{recapbox}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Theorem \ref{thm:LLN} explains why a sample mean converges to its population mean; it is a consistency result.
\item
  Chebyshev's inequality bounds the probability of a large estimation error but does not describe its full distribution.
\item
  Theorem \ref{thm:LindbergLevyCLT} gives the large-sample distribution of the standardized sample mean and therefore supports confidence intervals and tests.
\item
  The multivariate extension in Theorem \ref{thm:MCLT} applies the same logic to vectors of sample moments.
\item
  Section \ref{comparison-means} combines two independent CLT approximations to compare population means.
\end{itemize}

\end{recapbox}

\clearpage

\section{Exercises}\label{clt-exercises}

Attempt each question and explain your reasoning, including for true-or-false statements. \textbf{Foundation} exercises apply a result directly; \textbf{Standard} exercises require several steps or a short proof; \textbf{Advanced} exercises combine several results or require a longer derivation. Worked solutions and exercise-specific hints are reserved for teaching sessions and are not included in this student edition.

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Financial returns and the CLT]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:clt-financial-returns}{}\label{exr:clt-financial-returns}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Application}\\
Review Section \ref{TCL}.

Consider a financial asset whose value on day \(i\) is \(S_i\). We set \(s_i = \log(S_i/S_{i-1})\) and we assume that \(s_i = \mu + \sigma Z_i\), where the \(Z_i\) are identically and independently distributed (i.i.d.) and are drawn from a Student distribution with \(\nu\) degrees of freedom (i.e.~\(Z_i \sim \text{i.i.d.}\, t(\nu)\)).\\
An investor buys the asset at date \(0\) and holds it until date \(n\). We denote by \(\bar{s}_n\) the average of \(s_i\) between periods \(0\) and \(n\), that is \(\bar{s}_n = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n s_i\).\\
We recall that if \(Z \sim t(\nu)\), then \(\mathbb{E}(Z) = 0\) and \(\mathbb{V}ar(Z) = \frac{\nu}{\nu - 2}\) for \(\nu > 2\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Interpret \(s_i\).
\item
  Express \(S_n\) as a function of \(\bar{s}_n\) and of \(S_0\).
\item
  Assuming that \(n\) is large, give an approximation of the distribution of \(\sqrt{n}\bar{s}_n\).
\item
  Show that \(\mathbb{P}(S_n < S_0) = \mathbb{P}(\sqrt{n}\bar{s}_n < 0)\).
\item
  For large \(n\), derive an approximation to the probability that the investor loses money by holding the asset between dates 0 and \(n\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Roulette and the Central Limit Theorem]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:clt-roulette}{}\label{exr:clt-roulette}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Application}\\
Review Section \ref{TCL}.

The pockets of the roulette wheel are numbered from 0 to 36. There are 18 black pockets, 18 red pockets, and one green pocket (numbered 0). The probability that the ball stops in pocket \(i\), \(i \in\) 0, 1, 2, ..., 36 is 1/37. Each player chooses one color (black or red) and gives 1 \$ to the Casino. If the ball lands in a compartment whose color is as bet by the player, then the player earns 2 \$. Otherwise, she gets 0.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Determine the payoffs of the Casino when there is only one player who plays once.
\item
  One player plays three times. Compute the probability that the Casino loses money.
\item
  There are 100 players. Each one plays 100 times. The 10,000 resulting games are assumed to be independent. Approximate the distribution of the casino's total payoff.
\item
  Propose an analytical approximation of the probability that the Casino loses money after \(n\) games, where \(n\) is a large number.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Repeated-payoff game: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:clt-payoff-true-false}{}\label{exr:clt-payoff-true-false}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{TCL}.

Consider a repeated game. The payoff of each round is:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \(-2\) with probability \(\dfrac{1}{2}\),
\item
  \(7\) with probability \(\dfrac{1}{7}\),
\item
  \(0\) otherwise.
\end{itemize}

We play \(n\) rounds (\(n\) is large). Payoffs are independent across rounds. Let \(x_i\) be the payoff in round \(i\), and let \[z_n=\sum_{i=1}^n x_i
\qquad\text{so that}\qquad
z_n=n\bar{x}_n,
\quad
\bar{x}_n=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i.\] We note that \(\mathbb{E}(x_i)=0\) and we admit (without calculation) that \(\mathbb{V}ar(x_i)=9\).

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \(\mathbb{V}ar(z_n)=9\).
\item
  \(z_n\) approximately follows a Normal distribution of mean \(0\) and variance \(9n\).
\item
  If \(n=900\), an interval containing approximately 95\% of the sampling distribution of \(\bar{x}_n\) is \([-1.96,1.96]\).
\item
  We approximately have \(\bar{x}_n \sim \mathcal{N}(0,9)\).
\item
  \(\mathbb{P}(z_n<0)\) and \(\mathbb{P}(z_n>0)\) are close to \(\dfrac{1}{2}\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Comparing two sample means: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:clt-two-samples-true-false}{}\label{exr:clt-two-samples-true-false}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{TCL}.

Consider two samples, each of size \(n\), where \(n\) is large. The first sample is \(\{x_1,\dots,x_n\}\) and the second sample is \(\{y_1,\dots,y_n\}\). We assume that all observations are independent (within and across samples). The \(x_i\) are drawn from a distribution with mean \(\mu_x\) and variance \(\sigma_x^2\), and the \(y_i\) are drawn from a distribution with mean \(\mu_y\) and variance \(\sigma_y^2\).

We denote by \(\bar{x}_n\) and \(\bar{y}_n\) the sample means: \[\bar{x}_n=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i,
\qquad
\bar{y}_n=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n y_i.\]

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  The distribution of \(\bar{x}_n-\mu_x\) is approximately \(\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_x^2)\).
\item
  The variance of \(\bar{x}_n\) is \(\sigma_x^2\).
\item
  If \(\mu_x=\mu_y\), the distribution of \(\sqrt{n}(\bar{x}_n-\bar{y}_n)\) is approximately \(\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_x^2+\sigma_y^2)\).
\item
  If \(\sigma_x^2=\sigma_y^2=0.5\), \(\bar{x}_n=1\), \(\bar{y}_n=1.1\), and \(n=900\), a test of mean comparison cannot reject \(H_0:\mu_x=\mu_y\) at the 5\% significance level.
\item
  \(\bar{x}_n-\bar{y}_n\) is a consistent estimator of \(\mu_x-\mu_y\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Precision of Buffon's needle estimator]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:clt-buffon-precision}{}\label{exr:clt-buffon-precision}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{TCL}.

Return to Buffon's needle experiment in Example \ref{exm:Buffon}. Let \(X_i\) equal one when needle \(i\) crosses a groove and zero otherwise. In the version of the experiment considered there,
\[
\mathbb{E}(X_i)=p=\frac{1}{\pi}.
\]
After \(n\) independent throws, estimate \(\pi\) by
\[
\widehat\pi=\frac{1}{\overline X_n}.
\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Use the CLT to obtain an approximate distribution for \(\overline X_n\).
\item
  Apply a first-order approximation to obtain the approximate standard error of \(\widehat\pi\).
\item
  Find the smallest integer \(n\) for which the approximate 95\% margin of error for \(\widehat\pi\) is at most \(0.01\). Use the true value of \(\pi\) when planning the required sample size.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Approximating normal quantiles with a coin]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:clt-coin-normal-quantiles}{}\label{exr:clt-coin-normal-quantiles}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Simulation design}\\
Review Section \ref{TCL}.

Suppose that you have only a fair coin and enough time to repeat an experiment many times.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Design a simulation that produces observations whose distribution is approximately \(\mathcal{N}(0,1)\). State how many coin tosses constitute one simulated observation and how that observation is standardized.
\item
  Explain how repeated observations from this simulation can be used to approximate the 1st through 99th percentiles of the standard normal distribution.
\item
  Describe how the accuracy of the approximation changes when you increase (i) the number of tosses per observation and (ii) the number of simulated observations.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\chapter{Statistical tests}\label{Tests}

\textbf{Prerequisites.} Probability distributions, sampling distributions, quantiles, and the central limit theorem.

\textbf{After working through this chapter, you should be able to:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Formulate null and alternative hypotheses and define a rejection region.
\item
  Distinguish size, significance level, power, and the two types of testing error.
\item
  Compute and interpret a p-value for a specified test.
\item
  Explain the terminology used for one-tail and two-tail rejection regions.
\item
  Assess the asymptotic level and consistency of a test.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Roadmap.} We begin with the logic and possible errors of statistical decisions. We then study rejection regions and p-values, introduce asymptotic properties, and finish with normality tests. Section \ref{tests-exercises} provides applications to size, power, and distributional testing.

A statistical test asks whether a hypothesis about an unknown parameter is compatible with the observed data. The data are random, and their distribution depends on that parameter, so the answer must account for sampling uncertainty.

For example, suppose that \(\mathbf{x}=\{x_1,\dots,x_n\}\) is an i.i.d. sample with unknown mean \(\mu\) and variance \(\sigma^2\). We may wish to assess whether \(\mu=0\) or whether \(\sigma=1\). A test translates either question into a decision rule whose possible errors can be quantified.

The hypothesis the researcher wants to test is called the \emph{null hypothesis}. It is often denoted by \(H_0\). It is a conjecture about a given property of a population. Without loss of generality, it can be stated as:
\[
H_0:\;\{\theta \in \Theta_0\},
\]
where \(\Theta_0\) is the set of parameter values allowed by the null hypothesis. It can also be defined through a function \(h\) (say):\footnote{We can relate the two formulations through \(\Theta_0 = \{\theta:\,h(\theta)=0\}\).}
\[
H_0:\;h(\theta)=0.
\]

The \emph{alternative hypothesis}, often denoted \(H_1\), is then defined by \(H_1:\;\{\theta \in \Theta_1\}\), where \(\Theta_1\) contains the parameter values under consideration that are not in \(\Theta_0\).\footnote{Note that researchers may want to find support for the null hypothesis \(H_0\) as well as for the alternative hypothesis \(H_1\).}

The ingredients of a statistical test are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a vector of (unknown) parameters (\(\theta\)),
\item
  a \textbf{test statistic}, that is a function of the sample components (\(S(\mathbf{x})\), say), and
\item
  a \textbf{critical region} (\(\Omega\), say), defined as a set of implausible values of \(S\) under \(H_0\).
\end{itemize}

To implement the test, we need the distribution of \(S\) under \(H_0\). We compute \(S(\mathbf{x})\) and determine whether it lies in the critical region \(\Omega\), which contains values judged sufficiently implausible under the null. If \(S(\mathbf{x})\in\Omega\), we reject \(H_0\). The reasoning is: \emph{if the null were true, an outcome at least this unfavorable to it would be rare}.

There are two possible outcomes:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(H_0\) is rejected if \(S \in \Omega\);
\item
  \(H_0\) is not rejected if \(S \not\in \Omega\).
\end{itemize}

Except in extreme cases, there is always a non-zero probability to reject \(H_0\) while it is true (\textbf{Type I error}, or \textbf{false positive}), or to fail to reject it while it is false (\textbf{Type II error}, or \textbf{false negative}).

This vocabulary is widely used. For instance, the notions of false positive and false negative are used in the context of Early Warning Signals (see Example \ref{exm:FPFN}).

\begin{example}[Early Warning Signals]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:FPFN}{}\label{exm:FPFN}Early-warning systems aim to detect crises before they occur. See, for example, \href{http://www.ecb.europa.eu/events/pdf/conferences/140623/Vasicek-et-al_Comparing-Different-Early-Warning-Systems.pdf?F96bbb525a26071ecf97f9154fb3cc73}{ECB (2014)} for applications to financial crises.

Suppose an index \(W\) tends to be large before a financial crisis. An early-warning rule predicts a crisis when \(W>a\). Lowering \(a\) produces more warnings and therefore more false positives; raising \(a\) produces fewer warnings and therefore more false negatives. The threshold determines the trade-off between the two errors. Example \ref{exm:USrecessionEWS} implements this idea using US recession dates and Treasury yields.
\end{example}

\section{Size and power of a test}\label{size-power}

\begin{definition}[Size and Power of a test]
\protect\hypertarget{def:sizepower}{}\label{def:sizepower}

For a test with rejection region \(\Omega\), define its rejection probability at parameter value \(\theta\) by \(\pi(\theta)=\mathbb{P}_\theta(S\in\Omega)\). Then:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the \textbf{size} of the test is \(\alpha=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_0}\pi(\theta)\); a test has significance level \(\alpha\) if its size is at most \(\alpha\),
\item
  the \textbf{power function} is \(\pi(\theta)\) for \(\theta\in\Theta_1\); at a particular alternative \(\theta\), the probability of a type-II error is \(\beta(\theta)=1-\pi(\theta)\).
\end{itemize}

\end{definition}

For a \emph{simple} null and a \emph{simple} alternative, each hypothesis specifies a single distribution. In that special case, these definitions reduce to
\begin{eqnarray}
\alpha &=& \mathbb{P}(S \in \Omega\mid H_0) \quad \mbox{(probability of a false positive)},\\
\beta &=& \mathbb{P}(S \not\in \Omega\mid H_1) \quad \mbox{(probability of a false negative)}.
\end{eqnarray}

The power is the probability that the test will lead to the rejection of the null hypothesis if the latter is false. Therefore, for a given size, we prefer tests with high power.

In most cases, size and power involve a trade-off. In the early-warning example, increasing \(a\) reduces false positives and therefore the size of the rule, but it also increases false negatives and therefore reduces power. The next section shows how a critical region formalizes this choice.

\begin{example}[A yield-curve warning of US recessions]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:USrecessionEWS}{}\label{exm:USrecessionEWS}Can the slope of the yield curve warn of a recession? We use \texttt{USrecession}, described in Appendix \ref{data-us-recession}. It contains monthly Treasury yields and the NBER recession chronology for 1982--2024. A \textbf{yield} is an annualised interest rate. The \textbf{term spread} is the 10-year yield minus the 3-month yield, measured in percentage points. For example, yields of 4\% and 4.5\% give a spread of \(-0.5\) percentage points: the yield curve is inverted.

At the end of month \(t\), we issue a warning if the spread is negative. Define the score \(W_t\) and the warning rule by
\[
W_t=-(\text{10-year yield}_t-\text{3-month yield}_t),
\qquad\text{warning at }t\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad W_t>a.
\]
Our starting threshold is \(a=0\). The outcome is whether the economy is \textbf{in recession in month \(t+12\)}. This is a fixed twelve-month horizon, not the occurrence of any recession during the next twelve months. The warning uses yields from month \(t\); the later NBER dating is used only to assess its outcome.

There are 504 evaluable warning months, January 1982--December 2023, whose outcomes run from January 1983 to December 2024. The final twelve months of the dataset have no outcome within the snapshot and are excluded from evaluation. Figure \ref{fig:ews-us-timeline} aligns the spread with the month it is intended to predict.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/ews-us-timeline-1} 

}

\caption{The yield spread observed twelve months earlier, aligned with the forecast month. Grey bands show NBER recession months. Orange points mark negative spreads, which trigger warnings. Every plotted spread was observed twelve months before the month on the horizontal axis.}\label{fig:ews-us-timeline}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Count the four possible outcomes.} The warning rule gives the following table. Each cell counts forecast months, rather than distinct recession episodes.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Decision at t & No recession at t + 12 & Recession at t + 12\\
\midrule
No warning & 440 & 23\\
Warning & 28 & 13\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The rule detects 13 of the 36 recession months, so its \textbf{detection rate} is \(13/36=\) 36.1\%. It misses the remaining 23 recession months: the \textbf{false-negative rate} is 63.9\%. Among the 468 non-recession months, 28 receive warnings, giving a \textbf{false-positive rate} of \(28/468=\) 6\%.

Notice the denominators. The fraction of warnings that are false is instead \(28/41=\) 68.3\%. It answers a different question: given that a warning has been issued, how often is the forecast month outside a recession?

\textbf{Change the threshold.} Lowering \(a\) issues more warnings. The following table holds the sample and forecast horizon fixed while changing only that threshold.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
a (percentage points) & Warnings & Detection (\%) & False positives (\%)\\
\midrule
-1.0 & 151 & 83.3 & 25.9\\
-0.5 & 97 & 72.2 & 15.2\\
0.0 & 41 & 36.1 & 6.0\\
0.5 & 17 & 8.3 & 3.0\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

Moving from \(a=0\) to \(a=-1\) raises detection from 36.1\% to 83.3\%, but raises the false-positive rate from 6\% to 25.9\%. Figure \ref{fig:ews-us-roc} displays this trade-off over all thresholds. It is called a \textbf{receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve}: its horizontal coordinate is the false-positive rate and its vertical coordinate is the detection rate.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/ews-us-roc-1} 

}

\caption{Historical detection versus false-positive rates as the warning threshold varies. The highlighted point is the inversion rule a = 0; the other labelled points match the threshold table. The diagonal is the benchmark for warnings unrelated to the outcome. All points use the same 504 forecast months.}\label{fig:ews-us-roc}
\end{figure}

These rates are conditional frequencies computed from the historical data. Adjacent months are dependent, and the 36 recession months belong to four recession episodes. We will return to estimating binary-outcome models and evaluating time-series forecasts in Chapters \ref{binary-choice-models} and \ref{TS}.
\end{example}

\section{Critical regions and p-values}\label{critical-regions}

The shape of the critical region depends on which values of \(S\) contradict the null. Its boundary is calibrated so that \(\mathbb{P}_\theta(S\in\Omega)\le\alpha\) for every \(\theta\in\Theta_0\), with equality at a least-favourable null value when the test has size exactly \(\alpha\). A smaller significance level therefore reserves rejection for more unusual outcomes under \(H_0\).

In this book, the terms \emph{one-sided} and \emph{two-sided} describe the shape of the rejection region for the chosen test statistic. A two-sided (or two-tailed) test rejects in both tails. This is common when large positive and large negative values of a symmetric statistic both count against \(H_0\), as in Figures \ref{fig:Illusttest1} and \ref{fig:Illusttest2}. This \href{https://jrenne.shinyapps.io/tests/}{web interface} illustrates alternative situations.

In much of the statistical literature, however, \emph{one-sided} and \emph{two-sided} primarily describe the direction of the alternative hypothesis (for example, \(\mu>\mu_0\) versus \(\mu\ne\mu_0\)). The two usages often agree, but they are not identical. In particular, an upper-tail chi-square or \(F\) test has a one-tail rejection region even though the underlying departure from the null need not have a single direction.

Figure \ref{fig:Illusttest2} also illustrates the notion of \emph{p-value} for a two-sided test. The p-value is the smallest significance level at which the observed statistic would be rejected by the specified family of rejection regions. Thus, if the p-value is at most \(\alpha\), we reject \(H_0\) at significance level \(\alpha\); otherwise, we do not reject it.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/Illusttest1-1} 

}

\caption{A two-tail rejection region for a Student t statistic with 5 degrees of freedom. The critical values are -2.571 and 2.571; the shaded tails have total probability 0.05.}\label{fig:Illusttest1}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/Illusttest2-1} 

}

\caption{The p-value includes both tails beyond the observed absolute statistic, 1.97. Its value is about 0.106, greater than 0.05: the observation does not lie in the 5\% rejection region.}\label{fig:Illusttest2}
\end{figure}

Figures \ref{fig:IllusTestOneSided1} and \ref{fig:IllusTestOneSided2} illustrate a \textbf{one-tailed}, or \textbf{one-sided}, rejection region in the terminology used in this course. Here only large values of a chi-square statistic count against \(H_0\), so the critical region is in the upper tail. A statistic with support on \(\mathbb{R}^+\) does not by itself force a one-tail test; the rejection region follows from which values provide evidence against the null. Figure \ref{fig:IllusTestOneSided2} also illustrates the corresponding p-value.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/IllusTestOneSided1-1} 

}

\caption{An upper-tail rejection region for a chi-squared statistic with 5 degrees of freedom. The critical value is 11.070; the shaded tail has probability 0.05.}\label{fig:IllusTestOneSided1}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/IllusTestOneSided2-1} 

}

\caption{The upper-tail p-value is the probability of a statistic at least as large as 9.07 under the null. It exceeds 0.05, so the null is not rejected at the 5\% level.}\label{fig:IllusTestOneSided2}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}[Inspecting a factory's production]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:Factory}{}\label{exm:Factory}A factory produces cylinders with a target diameter of 1 cm. A cylinder is \textbf{defective} if its diameter falls outside \([0.99,1.01]\) cm. The client's specification allows \textbf{at most 10\% defective parts}. Let \(\theta\) be the unknown proportion of defective parts. We test
\[
H_0:\theta\leq0.10\qquad\text{against}\qquad H_1:\theta>0.10.
\]
Rejecting \(H_0\) flags production as failing the specification. Not rejecting means that the inspection has not established a violation.
