Measuring every part would be costly, so we inspect \(n\) randomly chosen parts. We model their defect indicators as independent Bernoulli variables with parameter \(\theta\); for sampling without replacement from a lot of one million parts, this is an approximation justified by the small sampling fraction. Define
\[
d_i=\begin{cases}1&\text{if part }i\text{ is defective},\\0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
\qquad X_n=\sum_{i=1}^n d_i\sim\mathcal{B}(n,\theta).
\]
The statistic \(S_n=X_n/n\) is the observed defective share. Large values count against \(H_0\), so we reject when \(X_n>k_n\), or equivalently when \(S_n>b_n=k_n/n\).

\textbf{Choose the rule before inspecting the parts.} Setting \(b_n=0.10\) may seem natural, but the specification and the rejection threshold serve different purposes. When production is exactly at the allowed limit, sampling variation frequently puts the observed share above 10\%. For \(n=100\), the rule would reject as soon as 11 defects are found; its false-positive probability at \(\theta=0.10\) is about 42\%, far above 5\%.

To control false positives at 5\%, choose the smallest integer \(k_n\) satisfying
\[
\mathbb{P}_{0.10}(X_n>k_n)\leq0.05.
\]
For a fixed cutoff, the rejection probability increases with \(\theta\). Its largest value under \(H_0\) therefore occurs at the boundary \(\theta=0.10\). Calibrating at this boundary controls the whole null hypothesis. Because the count is discrete, the size is generally below, rather than exactly equal to, 5\%.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
Inspected parts & Reject from (defects) & Size (\%) & Power at 15\% defects (\%)\\
\midrule
100 & 16 & 3.99 & 43.17\\
500 & 62 & 4.65 & 95.74\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

For 100 inspected parts, the 5\% rule rejects when \textbf{16 or more} are defective. Its size is \textbf{3.99\%}. The 5\% significance level describes the probability of rejecting compliant production under repeated inspections; it is not the probability that production is compliant after observing a particular sample.

\textbf{Read the two errors under two different distributions.} Figure \ref{fig:factory-errors} holds this rule fixed and changes the true defect rate. At \(\theta=0.10\), the shaded rejection probability is the size. At \(\theta=0.15\), the same shaded region gives the power; the remaining probability is the type-II error probability.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/factory-errors-1} 

}

\caption{One decision rule, two sampling distributions. With 100 inspected parts, reject from 16 defects. The highlighted bars show a false positive at the null boundary (left) and detection of a violation at a 15\% defect rate (right).}\label{fig:factory-errors}
\end{figure}

At a true defect rate of 15\%, an inspection of 100 parts detects the violation with probability 43.2\%. It misses it with probability \(\beta(0.15)=\) 56.8\%. With 500 inspected parts and a newly calibrated 5\% rule, detection rises to 95.7\%.

\textbf{Read the entire rejection-probability curve.} For any true defect rate,
\[
\pi_n(\theta)=\mathbb{P}_\theta(X_n>k_n)
=\sum_{j=k_n+1}^{n}\binom{n}{j}\theta^j(1-\theta)^{n-j}.
\]
On the null interval \([0,0.10]\), this is the false-positive probability at each parameter value; its maximum is the size. To the right of 0.10, it is the power function. Figure \ref{fig:FactoryR} compares the calibrated rule with the uncalibrated 10\% cutoff. The latter detects more violations, but does so at the cost of a much larger size.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/FactoryR-1} 

}

\caption{Rejection probabilities in the factory example. The shaded interval contains the null values. Blue curves use an exact binomial rule of level 5\%; orange curves reject whenever the observed defective share exceeds 10\%. The dots at the boundary mark each rule's size. Increasing the sample size raises power while the calibrated rule continues to control size.}\label{fig:FactoryR}
\end{figure}

An inspection outcome also has an exact one-tail p-value. If \(x_{\mathrm{obs}}\) defects are observed, it is
\[
p=\sup_{\theta\leq0.10}\mathbb{P}_\theta(X_n\geq x_{\mathrm{obs}})
=\mathbb{P}_{0.10}(X_n\geq x_{\mathrm{obs}}).
\]
For example, 16 defects out of 100 give \(p=\) 0.0399, so the 5\% rule rejects. With 15 defects, \(p=\) 0.0726, so it does not reject.

Further parameter choices can be explored with this \href{https://jrenne.shinyapps.io/Factory/}{web interface}.
\end{example}

\section{Asymptotic properties of statistical tests}\label{asymptotic-properties-of-statistical-tests}

The preceding examples use a known finite-sample distribution. More often, the null distribution of a statistic is available only as a large-sample approximation, frequently through the CLT in Chapter \ref{TCL}. The rejection probability may then differ from its target in a finite sample even though it approaches that target as \(n\) grows. This motivates the definition of asymptotic size.

\begin{definition}[Asymptotic level]
\protect\hypertarget{def:asmyptlevel}{}\label{def:asmyptlevel}An asymptotic test with critical region \(\Omega_n\) has asymptotic size equal to \(\alpha\) if:
\[
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\;\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_0}\mathbb{P}_\theta(S_n\in\Omega_n)=\alpha.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[The factory example]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:FactAsymptlevel}{}\label{exm:FactAsymptlevel}Return to Example \ref{exm:Factory}. The exact binomial rule controls size for each sample size. We can also construct a simpler rule using the CLT. At the null boundary \(\theta_0=0.10\),
\[
\frac{\sqrt{n}(S_n-0.10)}{\sqrt{0.10(1-0.10)}}
=\frac{\sqrt{n}(S_n-0.10)}{0.30}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}(0,1).
\]
Choose the threshold
\[
b_n=0.10+\frac{0.30}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha),
\qquad\text{and reject when }S_n>b_n.
\]
The exact rejection probability is increasing in \(\theta\), so its supremum under \(H_0\) is attained at 0.10. The CLT gives
\[
\sup_{\theta\leq0.10}\mathbb{P}_\theta(S_n>b_n)
=\mathbb{P}_{0.10}(S_n>b_n)\longrightarrow\alpha.
\]
The normal approximation therefore controls size asymptotically, while the exact binomial rule controls it in finite samples. These are related calibrations, not identical cutoffs.
\end{example}

Controlling asymptotic size addresses behavior under the null. Under a fixed alternative, we instead want the rejection probability to approach one. A test with this property is consistent.

\begin{definition}[Asymptotically consistent test]
\protect\hypertarget{def:asmyptconsisttest}{}\label{def:asmyptconsisttest}An asymptotic test with critical region \(\Omega_n\) is consistent if:
\[
\forall \theta \in \Theta_1, \quad \mathbb{P}_\theta (S_n \in \Omega_n) \rightarrow 1.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[The factory example]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:FactAsymptConsist}{}\label{exm:FactAsymptConsist}Keep the asymptotically calibrated threshold from Example \ref{exm:FactAsymptlevel}. It satisfies \(b_n\to0.10\). Under any fixed alternative \(\theta>0.10\), the LLN gives \(S_n\overset{p}{\to}\theta\). The threshold and the statistic therefore converge to different limits, with the statistic above the threshold:
\[
\mathbb{P}_\theta(S_n>b_n)\longrightarrow1.
\]
Thus the same rule both controls asymptotic size and detects every fixed alternative with probability approaching one. The exact binomial rule has the same consistency property because its threshold \(k_n/n\) also converges to 0.10.

A fixed cutoff \(b=0.10\) is also consistent against \(\theta>0.10\), but its rejection probability at the null boundary converges to \(1/2\). Consistency alone does not ensure a useful significance level.
\end{example}

\section{Example: Normality tests}\label{example-normality-tests}

The general ideas above can now be assembled in a concrete test. Gaussian assumptions support many exact finite-sample results, so it is often useful to assess whether a sample is compatible with normality. The Jarque-Bera test compares sample skewness and kurtosis with their Gaussian values. We first define these population and sample moments, then derive the test statistic and study its large-sample behavior.

Let \(f\) be the p.d.f. of \(Y\). The \(k^{th}\) \textbf{standardized moment} of \(Y\) is defined as:
\[
\psi_k = \frac{\mu_k}{\left(\sqrt{\mathbb{V}ar(Y)}\right)^k},
\]
where \(\mathbb{E}(Y)=\mu\) and
\[
\mu_k = \mathbb{E}[(Y-\mu)^k]= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} (y-\mu)^k f(y) dy
\]
is the \(k^{th}\) \textbf{central moment} of \(Y\). In particular, \(\mu_2 = \mathbb{V}ar(Y)\). Using these notations, \(\psi_k\) rewrites:
\[
\psi_k = \frac{\mu_k}{\left(\mu_2^{1/2}\right)^k},
\]

The \textbf{skewness} of \(Y\) corresponds to \(\psi_3\) and the \textbf{kurtosis} to \(\psi_4\) (Def. \ref{def:skewnesskurtosis}).

\begin{proposition}[Skewness and kurtosis of the normal distribution]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:normSkewKurt}{}\label{prp:normSkewKurt}For a Gaussian var., the skewness (\(\psi_3\)) is 0 and the kurtosis (\(\psi_4\)) is 3.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a centered Gaussian distribution, \((-y)^3f(-y)=-y^3f(y)\). This implies that
\begin{eqnarray*}
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}y^3f(y)dy&=&\int_{-\infty}^{0}y^3f(y)dy+\int_{0}^{\infty}y^3f(y)dy\\
&=&-\int_{0}^{\infty}y^3f(y)dy+\int_{0}^{\infty}y^3f(y)dy=0,
\end{eqnarray*}
which leads to the skewness result.

For the kurtosis, first standardize the variable. If \(Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)\) has density \(f\), then \(f'(z)=-zf(z)\) and therefore \(\frac{d}{dz}(z^3f(z))=3z^2f(z)-z^4f(z)\). Integration over \(\mathbb{R}\) gives \(\mathbb{E}(Z^4)=3\mathbb{E}(Z^2)=3\), which is also the kurtosis of every non-degenerate Gaussian variable.
\end{proof}

Let us now introduce the sample analog of standardized moments. The \(k^{th}\) \textbf{central sample moment} of \(Y\) is given by:
\[
m_k = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i - \bar{y})^k,
\]
and the \(k^{th}\) \textbf{standardized sample moment} of \(Y\) is given by:
\[
g_k = \frac{m_k}{m_2^{k/2}}.
\]

\begin{proposition}[Consistency of central sample moments]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:conssitCentralMoments}{}\label{prp:conssitCentralMoments}If the \(y_i\)'s are i.i.d. and \(\mathbb{E}(|Y|^k)<\infty\), then the sample central moment \(m_k\) is a consistent estimator of the central moment \(\mu_k\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[Asymptotic distribution of sample skewness under normality]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:Asymptg3Normal}{}\label{prp:Asymptg3Normal}If \(y_i\sim\,i.i.d.\,\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)\), then \(\sqrt{n}g_3 \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}(0,6)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
See, e.g.~\href{http://www.springer.com/la/book/9780387985954}{Lehmann (1999)}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Asymptotic distribution of sample kurtosis under normality]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:Asymptg4Normal}{}\label{prp:Asymptg4Normal}If \(y_i\sim\,i.i.d.\,\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)\), then \(\sqrt{n}(g_4-3) \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}(0,24)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[Joint asymptotic distribution of skewness and kurtosis under normality]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:Asymptg3g4Normal}{}\label{prp:Asymptg3g4Normal}If \(y_i\sim\,i.i.d.\,\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)\), then the vector \((\sqrt{n}g_3,\sqrt{n}(g_4-3))\) is asymptotically bivariate Gaussian. Further its elements are uncorrelated (and therefore independent, because Gaussian).
\end{proposition}

The Jarque-Bera statistic is defined by:
\[
JB = n \left( \frac{g_3^2}{6}+\frac{(g_4-3)^2}{24} \right) = \frac{n}{6}\left(g_3^2 + \frac{(g_4-3)^2}{4}\right).
\]

\begin{proposition}[Jarque-Bera asympt. distri.]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:JB}{}\label{prp:JB}If \(y_i\sim\,i.i.d.\,\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)\), \(JB \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \chi^2(2)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This directly derives from Proposition \ref{prp:Asymptg3g4Normal}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Jarque-Bera power against a uniform alternative]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:JB-consistency}{}\label{exm:JB-consistency}

This simulation checks the null approximation and rejection behaviour against a uniform alternative, whose kurtosis differs from three (see Definition \ref{def:asmyptconsisttest}).

For each simulated sample, we compute the Jarque-Bera statistic defined above.

Let us first consider the case where \(H_0\) is satisfied. Figure \ref{fig:JBTest2} displays, for different sample sizes \(n\), the distribution of the JB statistics when the \(y_i\)'s are normal, consistently with \(H_0\). It appears that when \(n\) grows, the distribution indeed converges to the \(\chi^2(2)\) distribution (as stated by Proposition \ref{prp:JB}). The \(\chi^2(2)\) distribution is represented in orange on each plot.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/JBTest2-1} 

}

\caption{Sampling distributions of the Jarque-Bera statistic under normality, with 10,000 simulations per sample size. All panels share the same axes. The orange curve is the asymptotic chi-squared density with 2 degrees of freedom; the dotted line marks its 5\% critical value.}\label{fig:JBTest2}
\end{figure}

We next draw the \(y_i\)'s from a uniform distribution, so \(H_0\) is not satisfied. Figure \ref{fig:JBTest3} shows that, as \(n\) grows, the distribution of the JB statistic shifts to the right. This illustrates consistency against the uniform alternative (see Def. \ref{def:asmyptconsisttest}).

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/JBTest3-1} 

}

\caption{Sampling distributions of the Jarque-Bera statistic under a uniform alternative, with 10,000 simulations per sample size and the same horizontal scale as the preceding figure. As n increases, more statistics exceed the 5\% critical value.}\label{fig:JBTest3}
\end{figure}

\end{example}

\Needspace{13\baselineskip}

\section{Chapter recap}\label{tests-recap}

\begin{recapbox}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Definition \ref{def:sizepower} distinguishes the rejection probability under the null (size) from the rejection probability under an alternative (power).
\item
  Section \ref{critical-regions} explains how the direction of evidence determines the rejection region and how a p-value summarizes the evidence against the null.
\item
  Example \ref{exm:Factory} shows the size-power trade-off in a finite-sample decision rule.
\item
  Definition \ref{def:asmyptlevel} extends size control to tests whose null distribution is known only asymptotically.
\item
  Definition \ref{def:asmyptconsisttest} requires the rejection probability to converge to one under every fixed alternative.
\item
  Proposition \ref{prp:JB} combines sample skewness and kurtosis into the asymptotic Jarque-Bera normality test.
\end{itemize}

\end{recapbox}

\clearpage

\section{Exercises}\label{tests-exercises}

Attempt each question and explain your reasoning, including for true-or-false statements. \textbf{Foundation} exercises apply a result directly; \textbf{Standard} exercises require several steps or a short proof; \textbf{Advanced} exercises combine several results or require a longer derivation. Worked solutions and exercise-specific hints are reserved for teaching sessions and are not included in this student edition.

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Kurtosis of a Gaussian scale mixture]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:tests-kurtosis-mixture}{}\label{exr:tests-kurtosis-mixture}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{Tests}.

Assume that \(Z|Y \sim \mathcal{N}(0, Y^2)\) and that \(Y \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Compute \(\mathbb{V}ar(Z)\).
\item
  Recalling that the kurtosis of a normal variable is equal to 3, compute \(\mathbb{E}(Y^4)\).
\item
  Compute \(\mathbb{E}(Z^4 | Y)\) and deduce \(\mathbb{E}(Z^4)\).
\item
  Compute the kurtosis of \(Z\).
\item
  Is \(Z\) a Gaussian variable?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Testing with a normal statistic: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:tests-normal-test-true-false}{}\label{exr:tests-normal-test-true-false}

\textbf{Foundation \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{Tests}.

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

Consider a null hypothesis \(H_0\). Under that null hypothesis, the test statistic \(z\) is \(\mathcal{N}(0, 1)\). We use a two-tail rejection region (a \emph{two-sided test} in the terminology of this course) and set its size \(\alpha\) at 5\%.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Using our data we get \(z = -3.2\). We reject the null hypothesis.
\item
  Under \(H_0\), the probability of making type-I errors is 5\%.
\item
  The probability of making Type-II error is smaller than 5\%.
\item
  The size (or level) of the test is the probability to make Type-II errors.
\item
  If I am very averse to Type-I error, I choose a low value for the size of the level.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Testing with a chi-squared statistic: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:tests-chi-square-true-false}{}\label{exr:tests-chi-square-true-false}\textbf{Foundation \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{Tests}.

Consider a test with statistic \(z\). Under the null hypothesis (\(H_0\)), \(z\) follows a \(\chi^2(7)\) distribution (chi-squared with 7 degrees of freedom; see table below). The rejection region is in the upper tail (a \emph{one-sided test} in the terminology of this course).

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  If \(z \ge 10\), we always reject the null hypothesis at the 5\% significance level (test size \(\alpha=5\%\)).
\item
  If \(H_0\) is valid, and when the test size (\(\alpha\)) is 10\%, the probability of making a Type I error is 10\%.
\item
  The power of the test is necessarily greater than the size of the test.
\item
  Under \(H_0\), the probability of having \(-1.96<z<1.96\) is approximately 95\%.
\item
  If the p-value of the test is 1.5\%, we reject \(H_0\) at the 5\% significance level (test size \(\alpha=5\%\)).
\end{enumerate}

Use the chi-squared quantiles reported in the statistical tables of the Appendix.
\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Size, power, and sample size]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:tests-power-normal-mean}{}\label{exr:tests-power-normal-mean}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{size-power}.

Let \(X_1,\dots,X_n\) be i.i.d. \(\mathcal{N}(\mu,4)\). Consider \(H_0:\mu=0\) against \(H_1:\mu>0\). The test rejects when \(\bar X_n>c_n\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  For \(n=25\), choose \(c_n\) so that the test has size \(5\%\).
\item
  Compute the power of this test when \(\mu=0.5\).
\item
  Keeping a 5\% significance level, find the smallest integer \(n\) that gives power of at least \(90\%\) when \(\mu=0.5\).
\item
  Explain why reducing the significance level while holding \(n\) fixed reduces power at every fixed alternative \(\mu>0\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\chapter{Linear regressions}\label{ChapterLS}

\textbf{Prerequisites.} Conditional expectations, covariance matrices, matrix algebra, statistical tests, and large-sample approximations.

\textbf{After working through this chapter, you should be able to:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Derive the OLS estimator and state the assumptions behind its main properties.
\item
  Construct confidence intervals and tests for individual and joint restrictions.
\item
  Distinguish confidence intervals for a conditional mean from prediction intervals for a new outcome.
\item
  Diagnose multicollinearity, omitted-variable bias, and irrelevant regressors.
\item
  Explain identification and estimation with instrumental variables.
\item
  Select covariance estimators under heteroskedasticity, serial correlation, and clustering.
\item
  Describe the purpose of shrinkage methods.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Roadmap.} We introduce the linear model and OLS before turning to inference, common specification problems, and the distinction between confidence and prediction intervals. Instrumental variables address endogenous regressors. The general regression model then accommodates non-spherical disturbances, and the final section introduces shrinkage. Section \ref{regressions-exercises} collects exercises spanning these topics.

The empirical applications address several questions. \textbf{Accidents:} how is daily rainfall associated with injury-accident counts in Paris, and how precisely can we estimate the mean count? Zurich monthly counts also illustrate seasonality. \textbf{Electricity:} how do housing differences affect an equipment comparison? \textbf{Credit cards:} how do correlated balances share explanatory information? \textbf{Votations:} how does dependence across cantons and voting days affect inference? \textbf{Hydropower:} how can we estimate a production response when price is endogenous? We return to these models as the chapter develops inference and prediction. Appendix \ref{data-appendix} describes the data; short simulations isolate mechanisms whose true parameters are known.

\begin{definition}
\protect\hypertarget{def:essai}{}\label{def:essai}Linear regression organizes the conditional relationship between an outcome and a set of explanatory variables. Its basic specification is
\begin{equation}
y_i = \boldsymbol\beta'\mathbf{x}_{i} + \varepsilon_i,\label{eq:linearspecif}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathbf{x}_{i}=[x_{i,1},\dots,x_{i,K}]'\) is a vector of dimension \(K \times 1\).
\end{definition}

For entity \(i\), the \(x_{i,k}\)'s, for \(k \in \{1,\dots,K\}\), are explanatory variables, regressors, or covariates. The variable of interest, \(y_i\), is often called dependent variable, or regressand. The last term of the specification, namely \(\varepsilon_i\), is called error, or disturbance.

The coefficients \(\beta_k\) summarize how the conditional mean of \(y_i\) varies with the regressors under the assumptions introduced below. We estimate them from a sample \(\{y_i,\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^n\), where \(n\) denotes the sample size.

To have an intercept in the specification \eqref{eq:linearspecif}, one has to set \(x_{i,1}=1\) for all \(i\); \(\beta_1\) then corresponds to the intercept.

\Needspace{8\baselineskip}

\begin{example}[Introducing daily accidents and rainfall]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:rain-introduction}{}\label{exm:rain-introduction}\textbf{Do rainy days have more road accidents?} The data in \texttt{AEC::FRaccidents\_daily} (Appendix \ref{data-paris-weather}) let us study this question in Paris, using recorded injury accidents and independently measured rainfall.

\textbf{One row describes one day in Paris.} The outcome is the number of injury accidents recorded during that day, not the number of people injured. Rainfall is measured in millimetres at the Paris-Montsouris weather station. To match the station's rainfall measurement, each day runs from 06:00 UTC to 06:00 UTC the next morning; the date labels the start of that interval.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{\label{tab:paris-rain-rows}Six consecutive observation days in Paris.}
\centering
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}[t]{llrr}
\toprule
Date & Weekday & Injury accidents & Rainfall (mm)\\
\midrule
2011-01-01 & Saturday & 3 & 0.0\\
2011-01-02 & Sunday & 4 & 0.0\\
2011-01-03 & Monday & 9 & 0.0\\
2011-01-04 & Tuesday & 12 & 0.0\\
2011-01-05 & Wednesday & 20 & 14.7\\
2011-01-06 & Thursday & 11 & 5.8\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

For example, the day labelled 5 January 2011 has 20 recorded injury accidents and 14.7 mm of rain. The complete dataset contains \textbf{3287 consecutive days from 2011 to 2019}, including days with no recorded injury accident. The rainfall series comes from Météo-France; it is observed independently of whether an accident occurs.

We will first fit a line relating the accident count to rainfall, then ask how the comparison changes when we account for the calendar. The outcome is a count, rather than accident risk per journey: rainy weather can change both travel conditions and the amount of traffic.
\end{example}

\section{Hypotheses}\label{linearHyp}

In this section, we introduce different assumptions regarding the covariates and/or the errors. The properties of the estimators used by the researcher depend on which of these assumptions are satisfied.

\begin{hypothesis}[Full rank]
\protect\hypertarget{hyp:fullrank}{}\label{hyp:fullrank}The columns of the sample design matrix \(\mathbf{X}\) are linearly independent: \(\operatorname{rank}(\mathbf{X})=K\). In particular, this requires \(n\ge K\).
\end{hypothesis}

Intuitively, when Hypothesis \ref{hyp:fullrank} is violated, then the estimation of the model parameters is unfeasible since, for any value of \(\boldsymbol\beta\), some changes in the explanatory variables will be exactly compensated by other changes in another set of explanatory variables, preventing the identification of these effects.

Let us denote by \(\mathbf{X}\) the matrix containing all explanatory variables, of dimension \(n \times K\). (That is, row \(i\) of \(\mathbf{X}\) is \(\mathbf{x}_i'\).) The following hypothesis concerns the relationship between the errors (gathered in \(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\), a \(n\)-dimensional vector) and the explanatory variables \(\mathbf{X}\):

\begin{hypothesis}[Conditional mean-zero assumption]
\protect\hypertarget{hyp:exogeneity}{}\label{hyp:exogeneity}\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X}) = 0.
\end{equation}
\end{hypothesis}

Hypothesis \ref{hyp:exogeneity} has important implications:

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:implicationExog}{}\label{prp:implicationExog}

Under Hypothesis \ref{hyp:exogeneity}:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\roman{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i})=0\);
\item
  The \(x_{ij}\)'s and the \(\varepsilon_{i}\)'s have zero covariance, i.e.~\(\forall i,\,j \quad \mathbb{C}ov(x_{ij},\varepsilon_{i})=0\). Their correlation is therefore zero whenever both variances are finite and non-zero.
\end{enumerate}

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}

Let us prove (i) and (ii):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\roman{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  By the law of iterated expectations:
  \[
  \mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon)=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X}))=\mathbb{E}(0)=0.
  \]
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(x_{ij}\varepsilon_i)=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(x_{ij}\varepsilon_i|\mathbf{X}))=\mathbb{E}(x_{ij}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i|\mathbf{X})}_{=0})=0\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{proof}

The next two hypotheses (\ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} and \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}) concern the stochastic properties of the errors \(\varepsilon_i\):

\begin{hypothesis}[Homoskedasticity]
\protect\hypertarget{hyp:homoskedasticity}{}\label{hyp:homoskedasticity}\[
\forall i, \quad \mathbb{V}ar(\varepsilon_i|\mathbf{X}) = \sigma^2.
\]
\end{hypothesis}

Figure \ref{fig:heteroskedasticity} compares two situations: Panel (a) corresponds to homoskedasticity, and Panel (b) corresponds to heteroskedasticity. More specifically, in both plots, \(X_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)\) and \(\varepsilon^*_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)\). In Panel (a) (homoskedasticity):
\[
Y_i = 2 + 2X_i + \varepsilon^*_i.
\]
In Panel (b) (heteroskedasticity):
\[
Y_i = 2 + 2X_i + \left(2\mathbb{I}_{\{X_i<0\}}+0.2\mathbb{I}_{\{X_i\ge0\}}\right)\varepsilon^*_i\].

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/heteroskedasticity-1} 

}

\caption{The same conditional mean with different error variances. Both panels use the same regressor and underlying standard-normal errors; the line is the conditional mean. Only the error scale changes.}\label{fig:heteroskedasticity}
\end{figure}

\Needspace{40\baselineskip}

\begin{example}[Different dispersion across residential meters]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:electricity-heteroskedasticity}{}\label{exm:electricity-heteroskedasticity}The annual electricity data in \texttt{AEC::CHelectricity\_annual} (Appendix \ref{data-electricity}) provide an empirical illustration. We retain \textbf{2023 meters with all 365 daily import totals observed}, so each row describes one meter over one complete year. Let \(Y_i\) be its average daily electricity drawn from the grid, in kWh/day, and let \(D_i\) equal one for a single-family house and zero for an apartment.

With this binary regressor, the conditional-mean model \(\mathbb{E}(Y_i\mid D_i)=\alpha+\beta D_i\) allows a separate mean for each group. Homoskedasticity would additionally require the \textbf{same variance around those two means}. The two group means need not be equal for the errors to be homoskedastic.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Meter type & Meters & Mean (kWh/day) & Within-group SD (kWh/day)\\
\midrule
Apartment & 909 & 7.38 & 5.82\\
Single-family house & 325 & 32.00 & 21.77\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The within-group standard deviation is \textbf{5.82 kWh/day for apartments} and \textbf{21.77 for houses}. Subtracting each group's mean leaves these standard deviations unchanged. Figure \ref{fig:electricity-heterosk-residuals} displays those centred observations: both groups are centred at zero, but their spreads differ substantially.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/electricity-heterosk-residuals-1} 

}

\caption{Electricity imports relative to the meter-type mean, 2023. Each point is one meter with a complete year of readings; horizontal jitter separates overlapping points. The orange bars extend one within-group standard deviation above and below zero. The vertical scale is common to both groups.}\label{fig:electricity-heterosk-residuals}
\end{figure}

Differences in dwelling size, equipment and occupancy can contribute to the greater spread among houses. The point here is descriptive: allowing different conditional means does not automatically make the error variance constant. Grid imports measure electricity drawn from the grid; with on-site generation, they do not measure total electricity consumption.
\end{example}

The next assumption concerns the correlation of errors across observations. In the turnout application, this would rule out common unexplained influences across cantons or objects on the same voting day.

\begin{hypothesis}[Uncorrelated errors]
\protect\hypertarget{hyp:noncorrelResid}{}\label{hyp:noncorrelResid}\[
\forall i \ne j, \quad \mathbb{C}ov(\varepsilon_i,\varepsilon_j|\mathbf{X})=0.
\]
\end{hypothesis}

We will often need the conditional covariance matrix of the errors. Proposition \ref{prp:Sigma} gives its form when both Hypotheses \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} and \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid} hold.

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:Sigma}{}\label{prp:Sigma}If Hypotheses \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} and \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid} hold, then:
\[
\mathbb{V}ar(\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X})= \sigma^2 \mathbf{Id},
\]
where \(\mathbf{Id}\) is the \(n \times n\) identity matrix.
\end{proposition}

We will sometimes assume that errors are Gaussian---or normal. We will then invoke Hypothesis \ref{hyp:normality}:

\begin{hypothesis}[Normal distribution]
\protect\hypertarget{hyp:normality}{}\label{hyp:normality}\[
\boldsymbol\varepsilon\mid\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\sigma^2\mathbf{Id}_n).
\]
\end{hypothesis}

\section{Least-squares estimation}\label{LSquares}

\subsection{Derivation of the OLS formula}\label{derivation-of-the-ols-formula}

With the assumptions and notation in place, we now turn to estimation. \textbf{Ordinary Least Squares (OLS)} chooses the coefficient vector \(\mathbf{b}\) that minimizes the sum of squared residuals. Residuals are the sample counterparts of the unobserved errors \(\varepsilon_i\).

For a given vector of coefficients \(\mathbf{b}=[b_1,\dots,b_K]'\), the sum of squared residuals is:
\[
f(\mathbf{b}) =\sum_{i=1}^n \left(y_i - \sum_{j=1}^K x_{i,j} b_j \right)^2 = \sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \mathbf{x}_i' \mathbf{b})^2.
\]
Minimizing this sum amounts to minimizing:
\[
f(\mathbf{b}) = (\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b})'(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}).
\]

Since:\footnote{see Proposition \ref{prp:partial}.}
\[
\frac{\partial f}{\partial \mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{b}) = - 2 \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{y} + 2 \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b},
\]
it comes that a necessary first-order condition (FOC) is:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b} = \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{y}.\label{eq:OLSFOC}
\end{equation}
Under Assumption \ref{hyp:fullrank}, \(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\) is invertible. Hence:
\[
\boxed{\mathbf{b} = (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{y}.}
\]
Vector \(\mathbf{b}\) minimizes the sum of squared residuals. (\(f\) is a non-negative quadratic function, it therefore admits a minimum.)

We have:
\[
\mathbf{y} = \underbrace{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}}_{\mbox{fitted values } (\hat{\mathbf{y}})} + \underbrace{\mathbf{e}}_{\mbox{residuals}}
\]

The estimated residuals are:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{e} = \mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X} (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}' \mathbf{y} = \mathbf{M} \mathbf{y},\label{eq:Mres}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathbf{M} := \mathbf{Id} - \mathbf{X} (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\) is called the \textbf{residual maker} matrix.

Moreover, the fitted values \(\hat{\mathbf{y}}\) are given by:
\begin{equation}
\hat{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{X} (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}' \mathbf{y} = \mathbf{P} \mathbf{y},\label{eq:Proj}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{X} (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\) is a \textbf{projection matrix}.

These matrices \(\mathbf{M}\) and \(\mathbf{P}\) are such that:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathbf{M} \mathbf{X} = \mathbf{0}\): if one regresses one of the explanatory variables on \(\mathbf{X}\), the residuals are null.
\item
  \(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) (because \(\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta + \boldsymbol\varepsilon\) and \(\mathbf{M} \mathbf{X} = \mathbf{0}\)).
\end{itemize}

Here are some additional properties of \(\mathbf{M}\) and \(\mathbf{P}\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathbf{M}\) is symmetric (\(\mathbf{M} = \mathbf{M}'\)) and idempotent (\(\mathbf{M} = \mathbf{M}^2 = \mathbf{M}^k\) for \(k>0\)).
\item
  \(\mathbf{P}\) is symmetric and idempotent.
\item
  \(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{X} = \mathbf{X}\).
\item
  \(\mathbf{P} \mathbf{M} = \mathbf{M} \mathbf{P} = 0\).
\item
  \(\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{P}\mathbf{y} + \mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}\) (decomposition of \(\mathbf{y}\) into two orthogonal parts).
\end{itemize}

It is easily checked that \(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{e}=0\). Each column of \(\mathbf{X}\) is therefore orthogonal to \(\mathbf{e}\). In particular, if an intercept is included in the regression (\(x_{i,1} \equiv 1\) for all \(i\)'s, i.e., the first column of \(\mathbf{X}\) is filled with ones), the average of the residuals is null.

\begin{example}[Bivariate case]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:bivar}{}\label{exm:bivar}Consider a bivariate situation, where we regress \(y_i\) on a constant and an explanatory variable \(w_i\). We have \(K=2\), and \(\mathbf{X}\) is a \(n \times 2\) matrix whose \(i^{th}\) row is \([x_{i,1},x_{i,2}]\), with \(x_{i,1}=1\) (to account for the intercept) and with \(w_i = x_{i,2}\) (say).

We have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X} &=&
\left[\begin{array}{cc}
n & \sum_i w_i \\
\sum_i w_i & \sum_i w_i^2
\end{array}
\right],\\
(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} &=&
\frac{1}{n\sum_i w_i^2-(\sum_i w_i)^2}
\left[\begin{array}{cc}
\sum_i w_i^2 & -\sum_i w_i \\
-\sum_i w_i & n
\end{array}
\right],\\
(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{y} &=&
\frac{1}{n\sum_i w_i^2-(\sum_i w_i)^2}
\left[\begin{array}{c}
\sum_i w_i^2\sum_i y_i -\sum_i w_i \sum_i w_iy_i \\
-\sum_i w_i \sum_i y_i + n \sum_i w_i y_i
\end{array}
\right]\\
&=& \frac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_i(w_i - \bar{w})^2}
\left[\begin{array}{c}
\frac{\bar{y}}{n}\sum_i w_i^2 -\frac{\bar{w}}{n}\sum_i w_iy_i \\
\frac{1}{n}\sum_i (w_i-\bar{w})(y_i-\bar{y})
\end{array}
\right].
\end{eqnarray*}

It can be seen that the second element of \(\mathbf{b}=(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{y}\) is:
\[
b_2 = \frac{\overline{\mathbb{C}ov(W,Y)}}{\overline{\mathbb{V}ar(W)}},
\]
where \(\overline{\mathbb{C}ov(W,Y)}\) and \(\overline{\mathbb{V}ar(W)}\) are sample estimates.

Since there is a constant in the regression, we have \(b_1 = \bar{y} - b_2 \bar{w}\).
\end{example}

\subsection{Properties of the OLS estimate (small sample)}\label{properties-of-the-ols-estimate-small-sample}

The OLS properties stated in Proposition \ref{prp:propOLS} are valid for any sample size \(n\):

\begin{proposition}[Properties of the OLS estimator]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:propOLS}{}\label{prp:propOLS}

We have:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\roman{enumi}.}
\item
  Under Assumptions \ref{hyp:fullrank} and \ref{hyp:exogeneity}, the OLS estimator is linear and unbiased.
\item
  Under Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, the conditional covariance matrix of \(\mathbf{b}\) is: \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X}) = \sigma^2 (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}

Under Hypothesis \ref{hyp:fullrank}, \(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\) can be inverted. We have:
\[
\mathbf{b} = (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{y} = \boldsymbol\beta + (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}' {\boldsymbol\varepsilon}.
\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\roman{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Let us consider the expectation of the last term, i.e.~\(\mathbb{E}((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}' {\boldsymbol\varepsilon})\). Using the law of iterated expectations, we obtain:
  \[
  \mathbb{E}((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}' {\boldsymbol\varepsilon}) = \mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}' {\boldsymbol\varepsilon}|\mathbf{X}]) = \mathbb{E}((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol\varepsilon}|\mathbf{X}]).
  \]
  By Hypothesis \ref{hyp:exogeneity}, we have \(\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol\varepsilon}|\mathbf{X}]=0\). Hence \(\mathbb{E}((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}' {\boldsymbol\varepsilon}) =0\) and result (i) follows.
\item
  \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X}) = (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}' \mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\boldsymbol\varepsilon'|\mathbf{X}) \mathbf{X} (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\).
  By Prop. \ref{prp:Sigma}, if \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} and \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid} hold, then we have \(\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\boldsymbol\varepsilon'|\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{V}ar(\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X})=\sigma^2 \mathbf{Id}\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{proof}

Together, Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid} form the so-called Gauss-Markov set of assumptions. Under these assumptions, the OLS estimator feature the lowest possible variance within the family of linear unbiased estimates of \(\boldsymbol\beta\):

\begin{theorem}[Gauss-Markov Theorem]
\protect\hypertarget{thm:GaussMarkov}{}\label{thm:GaussMarkov}Under Assumptions \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, for any vector \(w\), the minimum-variance linear unbiased estimator of \(w' \boldsymbol\beta\) is \(w' \mathbf{b}\), where \(\mathbf{b}\) is the least squares estimator. (BLUE: Best Linear Unbiased Estimator.)
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Consider \(\mathbf{b}^* = C \mathbf{y}\), another linear unbiased estimator of \(\boldsymbol\beta\). Since it is unbiased, we must have \(\mathbb{E}(C\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{X}) = \mathbb{E}(C\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta + C\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X}) = \boldsymbol\beta\). We have \(\mathbb{E}(C\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X})=C\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X})=0\) (by \ref{hyp:exogeneity}). Therefore, conditional unbiasedness for every \(\boldsymbol\beta\) requires \(C\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{Id}\). (The matrix \(C\) may depend on the observed design matrix \(\mathbf{X}\).) Let us compute \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b^*}|\mathbf{X})\). For this, we introduce \(D = C - (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\), which is such that \(D\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{b}^*-\mathbf{b}\). The fact that \(C\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{Id}\) implies that \(D\mathbf{X} = \mathbf{0}\). We have \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b^*}|\mathbf{X}) = \mathbb{V}ar(C \mathbf{y}|\mathbf{X}) =\mathbb{V}ar(C \boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X}) = \sigma^2CC'\) (by Assumptions \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} and \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, see Prop. \ref{prp:Sigma}). Using \(C=D+(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\) and exploiting the fact that \(D\mathbf{X} = \mathbf{0}\) leads to:
\[
\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b^*}|\mathbf{X}) =\sigma^2\left[(D+(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}')(D+(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}')'\right] = \mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X}) + \sigma^2 \mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}'.
\]
Therefore, we have
\begin{eqnarray*}
&&\mathbb{V}ar(w'\mathbf{b^*}|\mathbf{X})=w'\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X})w + \sigma^2 w'\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}'w\\
&\ge& w'\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X})w=\mathbb{V}ar(w'\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X}).
\end{eqnarray*}
\end{proof}

The Frisch-Waugh theorem (Theorem \ref{thm:FW}) reveals the relationship between the OLS estimator and the notion of partial correlation coefficient. Consider the linear least square regression of \(\mathbf{y}\) on \(\mathbf{X}\). We introduce the notations:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathbf{b}^{\mathbf{y}/\mathbf{X}}\): OLS estimates of \(\boldsymbol\beta\),
\item
  \(\mathbf{M}^{\mathbf{X}}\): residual-maker matrix of any regression on \(\mathbf{X}\) (given by \(\mathbf{Id} - \mathbf{X} (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\)),
\item
  \(\mathbf{P}^{\mathbf{X}}\): projection matrix of any regression on \(\mathbf{X}\) (given by \(\mathbf{X} (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\)).
\end{itemize}

Let us split the set of explanatory variables into two: \(\mathbf{X} = [\mathbf{X}_1,\mathbf{X}_2]\). With obvious notations: \(\mathbf{b}^{\mathbf{y}/\mathbf{X}}=[\mathbf{b}_1',\mathbf{b}_2']'\).

\begin{theorem}[Frisch-Waugh Theorem]
\protect\hypertarget{thm:FW}{}\label{thm:FW}We have:
\[
\mathbf{b}_2 = \mathbf{b}^{\mathbf{M^{\mathbf{X}_1}y}/\mathbf{M^{\mathbf{X}_1}\mathbf{X}_2}}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The minimization of the least squares leads to (these are first-order conditions, see Eq. \eqref{eq:OLSFOC}):
\[
\left[ \begin{array}{cc} \mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_1 & \mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_2 \\ \mathbf{X}_2'\mathbf{X}_1 & \mathbf{X}_2'\mathbf{X}_2\end{array}\right]
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf{b}_1 \\ \mathbf{b}_2\end{array}\right] =
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf{X}_1' \mathbf{y} \\ \mathbf{X}_2' \mathbf{y} \end{array}\right].
\]
Use the first-row block of equations to solve for \(\mathbf{b}_1\) first; it comes as a function of \(\mathbf{b}_2\). Then use the second set of equations to solve for \(\mathbf{b}_2\), which leads to:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbf{b}_2 &=& [\mathbf{X}_2'\mathbf{X}_2 - \mathbf{X}_2'\mathbf{X}_1(\mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_2]^{-1}\mathbf{X}_2'(\mathbf{Id} - \mathbf{X}_1(\mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1')\mathbf{y}\\
&=& [\mathbf{X}_2' \mathbf{M}^{\mathbf{X}_1}\mathbf{X}_2]^{-1}\mathbf{X}_2'\mathbf{M}^{\mathbf{X}_1}\mathbf{y}.
\end{eqnarray*}
Using the fact that \(\mathbf{M}^{\mathbf{X}_1}\) is idempotent and symmetric leads to the result.
\end{proof}

This suggests a second way of estimating \(\mathbf{b}_2\):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Regress \(Y\) on \(X_1\), regress \(X_2\) on \(X_1\).
\item
  Regress the residuals associated with the former regression on those associated with the latter regressions.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{example}[A trend after removing seasonality]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:accident-seasonality}{}\label{exm:accident-seasonality}Has the monthly number of injury accidents in Zurich changed over time? Accident counts can also differ systematically between winter and summer. To separate these two patterns, regress the monthly count on a time trend and calendar-month indicators, using the 180 months in \texttt{AEC::CHaccidents\_monthly} (Appendix \ref{data-accidents}). The trend is measured in years since January 2011.

The partial-regression calculation has three steps: remove month-of-year means from accident counts; remove them from the trend; then regress the first set of residuals on the second. The theorem says that this gives exactly the trend coefficient from the full regression.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Method & Trend coefficient (accidents per year)\\
\midrule
Full regression & 1.5128\\
Partial regression & 1.5128\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The coefficients agree. A one-year increase in the trend is associated with a change of 1.51 in the fitted \textbf{monthly} injury-accident count, holding the calendar month fixed. This is a trend in counts, not accident risk per journey. Section \ref{HAC} examines residual dependence and its consequences for inference.
\end{example}

When \(b_2\) is scalar (and then \(\mathbf{X}_2\) is of dimension \(n \times 1\)), Theorem \ref{thm:FW} gives the expression of the \textbf{partial regression coefficient} \(b_2\):
\[
b_2 = \frac{\mathbf{X}_2'M^{\mathbf{X}_1}\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{X}_2'M^{\mathbf{X}_1}\mathbf{X}_2}.
\]

\subsection{Goodness of fit}\label{goodness-of-fit}

The preceding results concern the sampling properties of the coefficient estimates. We now ask a different question: how closely do the fitted values reproduce the observed outcomes within the sample?

Define the total variation in \(y\) as the sum of squared deviations (from the sample mean):
\[
TSS = \sum_{i=1}^{n} (y_i - \bar{y})^2.
\]
We have:
\[
\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b} + \mathbf{e} = \hat{\mathbf{y}} + \mathbf{e}
\]
In the following, we assume that the regression includes a constant (i.e.~for all \(i\), \(x_{i,1}=1\)). Denote by \(\mathbf{M}^0\) the matrix that transforms observations into deviations from sample means. Using that \(\mathbf{M}^0 \mathbf{e} = \mathbf{e}\) and that \(\mathbf{X}' \mathbf{e}=0\), we have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\underbrace{\mathbf{y}'\mathbf{M}^0\mathbf{y}}_{\mbox{Total sum of sq.}} &=& (\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b} + \mathbf{e})' \mathbf{M}^0 (\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b} + \mathbf{e})\\
&=& \underbrace{\mathbf{b}' \mathbf{X}' \mathbf{M}^0 \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}}_{\mbox{"Explained" sum of sq.}} + \underbrace{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}}_{\mbox{Sum of sq. residuals}}\\
TSS &=& Expl.SS + SSR.
\end{eqnarray*}

We can now define the coefficient of determination:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mbox{Coefficient of determination} = \frac{Expl.SS}{TSS} = 1 - \frac{SSR}{TSS} = 1 - \frac{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}}{\mathbf{y}'\mathbf{M}^0\mathbf{y}}.}\label{eq:RR2}
\end{equation}

It can be shown (\citet{Greene2003Econometric}, Section 3.5) that:
\[
\mbox{Coefficient of determination} = \frac{[\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i - \bar{y})(\hat{y_i} - \bar{y})]^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i - \bar{y})^2 \sum_{i=1}^n(\hat{y_i} - \bar{y})^2}.
\]
That is, the \(R^2\) is the sample squared correlation between \(y\) and the (regression-implied) \(y\)'s predictions.

The higher the \(R^2\), the better the in-sample fit of a model. One has to be cautious with \(R^2\), however, because adding explanatory variables cannot reduce it. As stated by Proposition \ref{prp:chgeInR2}, it may increase even when the new variable has no population relationship with the dependent variable. In the limit, \(n\) linearly independent regressors span \(\mathbb{R}^n\) and fit any \(n\)-observation outcome vector exactly, resulting in \(R^2=1\) when the usual definition is applicable.

\begin{proposition}[Change in SSR when a variable is added]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:chgeR2}{}\label{prp:chgeR2}We have:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{u}'\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e} - c^2(\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{z^*}) \qquad (\le \mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}) \label{eq:uu}
\end{equation}
where (i) \(\mathbf{u}\) and \(\mathbf{e}\) are the residuals in the regressions of \(\mathbf{y}\) on \([\mathbf{X},\mathbf{z}]\) and of \(\mathbf{y}\) on \(\mathbf{X}\), respectively, (ii) \(c\) is the regression coefficient on \(\mathbf{z}\) in the former regression and where \(\mathbf{z}^*\) are the residuals in the regression of \(\mathbf{z}\) on \(\mathbf{X}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The OLS estimates \([\mathbf{d}',\mathbf{c}]'\) in the regression of \(\mathbf{y}\) on \([\mathbf{X},\mathbf{z}]\) satisfies (first-order cond., Eq. \eqref{eq:OLSFOC}):
\[
\left[ \begin{array}{cc} \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X} & \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{z} \\ \mathbf{z}'\mathbf{X} & \mathbf{z}'\mathbf{z}\end{array}\right]
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf{d} \\ c\end{array}\right] =
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf{X}' \mathbf{y} \\ \mathbf{z}' \mathbf{y} \end{array}\right].
\]
Hence, in particular \(\mathbf{d} = \mathbf{b} - (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{z}c\), where \(\mathbf{b}\) is the OLS of \(\mathbf{y}\) on \(\mathbf{X}\). Substituting in \(\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{d} - \mathbf{z}c\), we get \(\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{e} - \mathbf{z}^*c\). We therefore have:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{u}'\mathbf{u} = (\mathbf{e} - \mathbf{z}^*c)'(\mathbf{e} - \mathbf{z}^*c)= \mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e} + c^2(\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{z^*}) - 2 c\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{e}.\label{eq:uuu}
\end{equation}
Now \(\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{e} = \mathbf{z^*}'(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}) = \mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{y}\) because \(\mathbf{z}^*\) are the residuals in an OLS regression on \(\mathbf{X}\). Since \(c = (\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{z^*})^{-1}\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{y^*}\) (by an application of Theorem \ref{thm:FW}), we have \((\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{z^*})c = \mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{y^*}\) and, therefore, \(\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{e} = (\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{z^*})c\). Substituting this expression into Eq. \eqref{eq:uuu} proves the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Change in the coefficient of determination when a variable is added]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:chgeInR2}{}\label{prp:chgeInR2}Denoting by \(R_W^2\) the coefficient of determination in the regression of \(\mathbf{y}\) on some variable \(\mathbf{W}\), we have:
\[
R_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{z}}^2 = R_{\mathbf{X}}^2 + (1-R_{\mathbf{X}}^2)(r_{yz}^\mathbf{X})^2,
\]
where \(r_{yz}^\mathbf{X}\) is the coefficient of partial correlation (see Definition \ref{def:partialcorrel}).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Using the notation of Proposition \ref{prp:chgeR2}, Theorem \ref{thm:FW} implies that \(c = (\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{z^*})^{-1}\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{y^*}\). Substitution into Eq. \eqref{eq:uu} gives \(\mathbf{u}'\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e} - (\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{y^*})^2/(\mathbf{z^*}'\mathbf{z^*})\). By the definition of partial correlation in Eq. \eqref{eq:pc}, \(\mathbf{u}'\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}\left(1 - (r_{yz}^\mathbf{X})^2\right)\). Dividing both sides by \(\mathbf{y}'\mathbf{M}_0\mathbf{y}\) proves the result.
\end{proof}

Figure \ref{fig:R2issue}, below, illustrates the fact that one can obtain an \(R^2\) of one by regressing a sample of length \(n\) on any set of \(n\) linearly-independent variables.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/R2issue-1} 

}

\caption{In-sample fit with irrelevant regressors: 30 observations and independent noise predictors. Ordinary R-squared cannot decrease when predictors are added. The final model has 29 slopes plus an intercept and fits exactly; its adjusted R-squared is undefined.}\label{fig:R2issue}
\end{figure}

In order to address the risk of adding irrelevant explanatory variables, measures of \textbf{adjusted \(R^2\)} have been proposed. Compared to the standard \(R^2\), these measures add penalties that depend on the number of covariates employed in the regression. A common adjusted \(R^2\) measure, denoted by \(\bar{R}^2\), is the following:
\begin{equation*}
\boxed{\bar{R}^2 = 1 - \frac{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}/(n-K)}{\mathbf{y}'\mathbf{M}^0\mathbf{y}/(n-1)} = 1 - \frac{n-1}{n-K}(1-R^2).}
\end{equation*}

\subsection{Inference and confidence intervals (in small sample)}\label{inference-and-confidence-intervals-in-small-sample}

Good fit does not by itself imply precise coefficient estimates. Inference requires the sampling distribution of \(\mathbf{b}\), which is available exactly under the Gaussian assumptions stated above.

Under the normality assumption (Assumption \ref{hyp:normality}), we know the distribution of \(\mathbf{b}\) (conditional on \(\mathbf{X}\)). Indeed, \(\mathbf{b} = \boldsymbol\beta + (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\). Therefore, conditional on \(\mathbf{X}\), vector \(\mathbf{b}\) is an affine combination of Gaussian variables---the components of \(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\). As a result, it is also Gaussian. Its distribution is therefore completely characterized by its mean and variance, and we have:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X} \sim \mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol\beta,\sigma^2(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}).\label{eq:distriBcondi}
\end{equation}

Eq. \eqref{eq:distriBcondi} can be used to conduct inference and tests. However, in practice, we do not know \(\sigma^2\) (which is a population parameter). The following proposition gives an unbiased estimate of \(\sigma^2\). The variance formulas and exact t and F tests below require \(n>K\).

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:expects2}{}\label{prp:expects2}Under \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, an unbiased estimate of \(\sigma^2\) is given by:
\begin{equation}
s^2 = \frac{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}}{n-K}.\label{eq:s2}
\end{equation}
(It is sometimes denoted by \(\sigma^2_{OLS}\).)
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}|\mathbf{X})&=&\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}'\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}|\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{E}(\mbox{Tr}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}'\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})|\mathbf{X}))\\
&=&\mbox{Tr}(\mathbf{M}\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}'|\mathbf{X}))=\sigma^2 \mbox{Tr}(\mathbf{M}).
\end{eqnarray*}
(Note that we have \(\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}'|\mathbf{X})=\sigma^2\mathbf{Id}\) by Assumptions \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} and \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, see Prop. \ref{prp:Sigma}.) Moreover:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mbox{Tr}(\mathbf{M})&=&n-\mbox{Tr}(\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}')\\
&=&n-\mbox{Tr}((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})=n-\mbox{Tr}(\mathbf{Id}_{K \times K}),
\end{eqnarray*}
which leads to the result.
\end{proof}

Two results will prove important to produce inference:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\roman{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  We know the conditional distribution of \(s^2\) (Prop. \ref{prp:s2distri}).
\item
  Conditional on \(\mathbf{X}\), \(s^2\) and \(\mathbf{b}\) are independent random variables (Prop. \ref{prp:indeps2b}).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:s2distri}{}\label{prp:s2distri}Under \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:normality}, we have: \(\dfrac{s^2}{\sigma^2} | \mathbf{X} \sim \frac{1}{n-K}\chi^2(n-K)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We have \(\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}=\boldsymbol\varepsilon'\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol\varepsilon\). \(\mathbf{M}\) is an idempotent symmetric matrix. Therefore it can be decomposed as \(PDP'\) where \(D\) is a diagonal matrix and \(P\) is an orthogonal matrix. As a result \(\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e} = (P'\boldsymbol\varepsilon)'D(P'\boldsymbol\varepsilon)\), i.e.~\(\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}\) is a weighted sum of independent squared Gaussian variables (the entries of \(P'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) are independent because they are Gaussian ---under \ref{hyp:normality}--- and uncorrelated). The variance of each of these i.i.d. Gaussian variable is \(\sigma^2\). Because \(\mathbf{M}\) is an idempotent symmetric matrix, its eigenvalues are either 0 or 1, and its rank equals its trace (see Propositions \ref{prp:rootsidempotent} and \ref{prp:chi2idempotent}). Further, its trace is equal to \(n-K\) (see proof of Eq. \eqref{eq:s2}). Therefore \(D\) has \(n-K\) entries equal to 1 and \(K\) equal to 0. Hence, \(\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e} = (P'\boldsymbol\varepsilon)'D(P'\boldsymbol\varepsilon)\) is a sum of \(n-K\) squared independent Gaussian variables of variance \(\sigma^2\). Therefore \(\frac{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}}{\sigma^2} = (n-K)\frac{s^2}{\sigma^2}\) is a sum of \(n-K\) squared i.i.d. standard normal variables. The result follows by the definition of the chi-square distribution (see Def. \ref{def:chi2}).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:indeps2b}{}\label{prp:indeps2b}Under Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:normality}, \(\mathbf{b}\) and \(s^2\) are independent conditional on \(\mathbf{X}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We have \(\mathbf{b}=\boldsymbol\beta + [\mathbf{X}'{\mathbf{X}}]^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) and \(s^2 = \boldsymbol\varepsilon' \mathbf{M} \boldsymbol\varepsilon/(n-K)\). Hence \(\mathbf{b}\) is an affine combination of \(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) and \(s^2\) is a quadratic combination of the same Gaussian shocks. One can write \(s^2\) as \(s^2 = (\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol\varepsilon)' \mathbf{M} \boldsymbol\varepsilon/(n-K)\) and \(\mathbf{b}\) as \(\boldsymbol\beta + \mathbf{T}\boldsymbol\varepsilon\). Conditional on \(\mathbf{X}\), \(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{M}=0\) implies that \(\mathbf{T}\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) and \(\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) are independent (because two uncorrelated Gaussian variables are independent), therefore \(\mathbf{b}\) and \(s^2\) are conditionally independent.
\end{proof}

Consistently with Eq. \eqref{eq:distriBcondi}, under Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:normality}, the \(k^{th}\) entry of \(\mathbf{b}\) satisfies:
\[
b_k | \mathbf{X} \sim \mathcal{N}(\beta_k,\sigma^2 v_k),
\]
where \(v_k\) is the k\(^{th}\) component of the diagonal of \((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\).

Moreover, we have (Prop. \ref{prp:s2distri}):
\[
\frac{(n-K)s^2}{\sigma^2} | \mathbf{X} \sim \chi ^2 (n-K).
\]

As a result (using Propositions \ref{prp:s2distri} and \ref{prp:indeps2b}), we have:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{t_k = \frac{\frac{b_k - \beta_k}{\sqrt{\sigma^2 v_k}}}{\sqrt{\frac{(n-K)s^2}{\sigma^2(n-K)}}} = \frac{b_k - \beta_k}{\sqrt{s^2v_k}} \sim t(n-K),}\label{eq:resultstudentt}
\end{equation}
where \(t(n-K)\) denotes a Student \(t\) distribution with \(n-K\) degrees of freedom (see Def. \ref{def:tStudent}).\footnote{We have \(\frac{b_k - \beta_k}{\sqrt{\sigma^2 v_k}} | \mathbf{X} \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)\) and \(\frac{(n-K)s^2}{\sigma^2} | \mathbf{X} \sim \chi ^2 (n-K)\). These two distributions do not depend on \(\mathbf{X}\) \(\Rightarrow\) the \emph{marginal distribution} of \(t_k\) is also \(t\).}

Note that \(s^2 v_k\) is not exactly the conditional variance of \(b_k\): The variance of \(b_k\) conditional on \(\mathbf{X}\) is \(\sigma^2 v_k\). However \(s^2 v_k\) is an unbiased estimate of \(\sigma^2 v_k\) (by Prop. \ref{prp:expects2}).

The previous result (Eq. \eqref{eq:resultstudentt}) can be extended to any linear combinations of elements of \(\mathbf{b}\). (Eq. \eqref{eq:resultstudentt} is for its \(k^{th}\) component only.) Let us consider \(\boldsymbol\alpha'\mathbf{b}\), the OLS estimate of \(\boldsymbol\alpha'\boldsymbol\beta\). From Eq. \eqref{eq:distriBcondi}, we have:
\[
\boldsymbol\alpha'\mathbf{b} | \mathbf{X} \sim \mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol\alpha'\boldsymbol\beta,\sigma^2 \boldsymbol\alpha'(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol\alpha).
\]
Therefore:
\[
\frac{\boldsymbol\alpha'\mathbf{b} - \boldsymbol\alpha'\boldsymbol\beta}{\sqrt{\sigma^2 \boldsymbol\alpha'(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol\alpha}} | \mathbf{X} \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1).
\]
Using the same approach as the one used to derive Eq. \eqref{eq:resultstudentt}, one can show that Props. \ref{prp:s2distri} and \ref{prp:indeps2b} also imply that:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\frac{\boldsymbol\alpha'\mathbf{b} - \boldsymbol\alpha'\boldsymbol\beta}{\sqrt{s^2\boldsymbol\alpha'(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\boldsymbol\alpha}} \sim t(n-K).}\label{eq:resultstudentt2}
\end{equation}

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/chartStudent-1} 

}

\caption{Student distributions approach the standard normal as the degrees of freedom increase. Small degrees of freedom imply heavier tails and larger critical values.}\label{fig:chartStudent}
\end{figure}

Equation \eqref{eq:resultstudentt} turns the distance between \(b_k\) and \(\beta_k\) into a probability statement and therefore supports confidence intervals and tests. For example, if \(\sqrt{v_k s^2}=1\) and \(n-K=10\), the probability that \(b_k\) lies below \(\beta_k-\) 4.587 \(\times \sqrt{v_k s^2}\) or above \(\beta_k+\) 4.587 \(\times \sqrt{v_k s^2}\) is 0.1\%.

That means for instance that, under the assumption that \(\beta_k=0\), it would be extremely unlikely to have obtained \(b_k/\sqrt{v_k s^2}\) smaller than -4.587 or larger than 4.587. More generally, this shows that the \textbf{t-statistic}, i.e., the ratio \(b_k/\sqrt{v_k s^2}\), is the test statistic associated with the null hypothesis:
\[
H_0: \beta_k=0.
\]
Under the Gaussian model and the null hypothesis, the test statistic follows a Student-t distribution with \(n-K\) degrees of freedom. Regression outputs routinely report an estimate divided by its standard error. For empirical applications, the appropriate standard error depends on the variance and dependence assumptions; Section \ref{HAC} develops alternatives to the conventional formula.

\begin{example}[Rainfall and daily accidents]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:rainOLS}{}\label{exm:rainOLS}Return to the Paris observations in Example \ref{exm:rain-introduction}. We start with a simple regression:
\[
\text{Accidents}_d=\alpha+\beta\frac{\text{Rainfall}_d}{10}+\varepsilon_d.
\]
The division by ten makes \(\beta\) the difference in fitted daily accident counts associated with \textbf{10 additional millimetres of rain}.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/paris-rain-scatter-1} 

}

\caption{Rainfall and daily injury accidents in Paris, 2011--2019. Each point is one day; overlapping points appear darker. The line is fitted to all 3,287 days.}\label{fig:paris-rain-scatter}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Term & Estimate\\
\midrule
Intercept & 17.292\\
Rainfall (per 10 mm) & 0.743\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The intercept, \textbf{17.29}, is the fitted number of accidents on a day with no rain. The rainfall slope is \textbf{0.74 accidents per 10 mm}. Thus the fitted difference between a day with 20 mm and a day with 10 mm is this slope. The points remain widely dispersed around the line: rainfall alone explains little of the variation across days.

Here we focus on fitting and interpreting the line. Daily observations can be dependent, so we postpone standard errors, tests and confidence intervals for this regression to Example \ref{exm:HCheterAC}.

This first line makes no calendar adjustment. Section \ref{Omitted} explains the role of controls using a separate electricity application; Example \ref{exm:HCheterAC} also considers calendar controls for these daily accident counts.
\end{example}

Returning to the Gaussian model considered above, the size-\(\alpha\) critical region for the exact Student test is:
\[
\left]-\infty,-\Phi^{-1}_{t(n-K)}\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\right] \cup \left[\Phi^{-1}_{t(n-K)}\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right),+\infty\right[.
\]

We recall that the \textbf{p-value} is defined as the probability that \(|Z| > |t|\), where \(t\) is the (computed) t-statistics and where \(Z \sim t(n-K)\). That is, in the context of the t-test, the p-value is given by \(2(1 - \Phi_{t(n-K)}(|t_k|))\). See \href{https://jrenne.shinyapps.io/tests/}{this webpage} for details regarding the link between critical regions, p-value, and test outcomes.

Now, suppose we want to compute a (symmetrical) \emph{confidence interval} \([I_{d,1-\alpha},I_{u,1-\alpha}]\) that is such that \(\mathbb{P}(\beta_k \in [I_{d,1-\alpha},I_{u,1-\alpha}])=1-\alpha\). That is, we want to have: \(\mathbb{P}(\beta_k < I_{d,1-\alpha})=\frac{\alpha}{2}\) and \(\mathbb{P}(\beta_k > I_{u,1-\alpha})=\frac{\alpha}{2}\). Let us focus on \(I_{d,1-\alpha}\) to start with. Using Eq. \eqref{eq:resultstudentt}, i.e., \(t_k = \frac{b_k - \beta_k}{\sqrt{s^2v_k}} \sim t(n-K)\), we have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{P}(\beta_k < I_{d,1-\alpha})=\frac{\alpha}{2} &\Leftrightarrow& \\
\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{b_k - \beta_k}{\sqrt{s^2v_k}} > \frac{b_k - I_{d,1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{s^2v_k}}\right)=\frac{\alpha}{2} &\Leftrightarrow& \mathbb{P}\left(t_k > \frac{b_k - I_{d,1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{s^2v_k}}\right)=\frac{\alpha}{2} \Leftrightarrow\\
1 - \mathbb{P}\left(t_k \le \frac{b_k - I_{d,1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{s^2v_k}}\right)=\frac{\alpha}{2} &\Leftrightarrow& \frac{b_k - I_{d,1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{s^2v_k}} = \Phi^{-1}_{t(n-K)}\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right),
\end{eqnarray*}
where \(\Phi_{t(n-K)}(\alpha)\) is the c.d.f. of the \(t(n-K)\) distribution (Table \ref{tab:Student}).

Doing the same for \(I_{u,1-\alpha}\), we obtain:
\begin{eqnarray*}
&&[I_{d,1-\alpha},I_{u,1-\alpha}] =\\
&&\left[b_k - \Phi^{-1}_{t(n-K)}\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\sqrt{s^2v_k},b_k + \Phi^{-1}_{t(n-K)}\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\sqrt{s^2v_k}\right].
\end{eqnarray*}

\subsection{Testing a set of linear restrictions}\label{Ftest}

A t-test concerns one coefficient or one linear combination. Many economic hypotheses impose several restrictions at once, so we next test whether \(J\) linear restrictions are \emph{jointly} consistent with the data:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:restrictions}
\begin{array}{ccc}
r_{1,1} \beta_1 + \dots + r_{1,K} \beta_K &=& q_1\\
\vdots && \vdots\\
r_{J,1} \beta_1 + \dots + r_{J,K} \beta_K &=& q_J.
\end{array}
\end{equation}
Assume that the \(J\) restrictions are linearly independent. In matrix form, we get:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{R}\boldsymbol\beta = \mathbf{q}.
\end{equation}

Define the \emph{discrepancy vector} \(\mathbf{m} = \mathbf{R}\mathbf{b} - \mathbf{q}\). Under the null hypothesis:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{m}|\mathbf{X}) &=& \mathbf{R}\boldsymbol\beta - \mathbf{q} = 0 \quad \mbox{and} \\
\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{m}|\mathbf{X}) &=& \mathbf{R} \mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X}) \mathbf{R}'.
\end{eqnarray*}

With these notations, the assumption to test is:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{H_0: \mathbf{R}\boldsymbol\beta - \mathbf{q} = 0 \mbox{ against } H_1: \mathbf{R}\boldsymbol\beta - \mathbf{q} \ne 0.}\label{eq:H0Ftest}
\end{equation}

Under Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, we have \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{m}|\mathbf{X}) = \sigma^2 \mathbf{R} (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{R}'\) (see Prop. \ref{prp:propOLS}). If we add the normality assumption (Hypothesis \ref{hyp:normality}), we have:
\begin{equation}
W = \mathbf{m}'\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{m}|\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{m} \sim \chi^2(J). \label{eq:W1}
\end{equation}

If \(\sigma^2\) was known, we could then conduct a \emph{Wald test} (directly exploiting Eq. \eqref{eq:W1}). But this is not the case in practice and we cannot compute \(W\). We can, however, approximate it be replacing \(\sigma^2\) by \(s^2\) (given in Eq. \eqref{eq:s2}). The distribution of this new statistic is not \(\chi^2(J)\) any more; it is an \emph{\(\mathcal{F}\) distribution} (whose quantiles are shown in Table \ref{tab:Fstat}), and the test is called \emph{\(F\) test}.

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:Ftest1}{}\label{prp:Ftest1}Under Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:normality} and if Eq. \eqref{eq:H0Ftest} holds, we have:
\begin{equation}
F = \frac{W}{J}\frac{\sigma^2}{s^2} = \frac{\mathbf{m}'(\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{R}')^{-1}\mathbf{m}}{s^2J} \sim \mathcal{F}(J,n-K),\label{eq:defFstatistics}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{F}\) is the distribution of the F-statistic (see Def. \ref{def:fstatistics}).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
According to Eq. \eqref{eq:W1}, \(W/J \sim \chi^2(J)/J\). Moreover, the denominator satisfies \(s^2/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2(n-K)/(n-K)\). Therefore, \(F\) is the ratio of a r.v. distributed as \(\chi^2(J)/J\) and another distributed as \(\chi^2(n-K)/(n-K)\). It remains to verify that these r.v. are independent. Under \(H_0\), we have \(\mathbf{m} =  \mathbf{R}(\mathbf{b}-\boldsymbol\beta) = \mathbf{R}(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\).
Therefore \(\mathbf{m}'(\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{R}')^{-1}\mathbf{m}\) is of the form \(\boldsymbol\varepsilon'\mathbf{T}\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) with \(\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{D}'\mathbf{C}\mathbf{D}\) where \(\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\) and \(\mathbf{C}=(\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{R}')^{-1}\). Under Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, the covariance between \(\mathbf{T}\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) and \(\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) is \(\sigma^2\mathbf{T}\mathbf{M} = \mathbf{0}\). Therefore, under \ref{hyp:normality}, these variables are Gaussian variables with 0 covariance. Hence they are independent.
\end{proof}

For large \(n-K\), the \(\mathcal{F}_{J,n-K}\) distribution converges to \(\mathcal{F}_{J,\infty}=\chi^2(J)/J\). Thus \(JF\) has an approximate \(\chi^2(J)\) distribution in large samples; \(F\) itself is approximated by \(\chi^2(J)/J\). In other words, one can approximately employ Eq. \eqref{eq:W1} to perform a Wald test (one just has to replace \(\sigma^2\) with \(s^2\) when computing \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{m}|\mathbf{X})\)).

The following proposition proposes another equivalent computation of the F-statistic, based on the \(R^2\) of the restricted and unrestricted linear models.

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:Ftest}{}\label{prp:Ftest}The F-statistic defined by Eq. \eqref{eq:defFstatistics} is also equal to:
\begin{equation}
F = \frac{(R^2-R_*^2)/J}{(1-R^2)/(n-K)} =  \frac{(SSR_{restr}-SSR_{unrestr})/J}{SSR_{unrestr}/(n-K)},\label{eq:defFstatistics2}
\end{equation}
where \(R_*^2\) is the coef. of determination (Eq. \eqref{eq:RR2}) of the ``restricted regression'' \emph{(SSR: sum of squared residuals.)}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let's denote by \(\mathbf{e}_*=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}_*\) the vector of residuals associated to the \emph{restricted regression} (i.e.~\(\mathbf{R}\mathbf{b}_*=\mathbf{q}\)).
We have \(\mathbf{e}_*=\mathbf{e} - \mathbf{X}(\mathbf{b}_*-\mathbf{b})\). Using \(\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{X}=0\), we get \(\mathbf{e}_*'\mathbf{e}_*=\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e} + (\mathbf{b}_*-\mathbf{b})'\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{b}_*-\mathbf{b}) \ge \mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}\).

By Proposition \ref{prp:constrainedLS} (in Appendix \ref{LinAlgebra}), we have: \(\mathbf{b}_*-\mathbf{b}=-(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} \mathbf{R}'\{\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{R}'\}^{-1}(\mathbf{R}\mathbf{b} - \mathbf{q})\). Therefore:
\[
\mathbf{e}_*'\mathbf{e}_* - \mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e} = (\mathbf{R}\mathbf{b} - \mathbf{q})'[\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{R}']^{-1}(\mathbf{R}\mathbf{b} - \mathbf{q}).
\]
This implies that the F statistic defined in Prop. \ref{prp:Ftest1} is also equal to:
\[
\frac{(\mathbf{e}_*'\mathbf{e}_* - \mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e})/J}{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}/(n-K)},
\]
which leads to the result.
\end{proof}

The null hypothesis \(H_0\) (Eq. \eqref{eq:H0Ftest}) of the F-test is rejected if \(F\) ---defined by Eq. \eqref{eq:defFstatistics} or \eqref{eq:defFstatistics2}--- is higher than \(\mathcal{F}_{1-\alpha}(J,n-K)\). (Hence, this test is a one-sided test.)

\subsection{Large-sample properties}\label{largeSample}

Exact t and F distributions rely on Gaussian errors. When normality is relaxed, large-sample approximations provide a second route to inference.

To begin with, we proceed under Hypothesis \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}. (We will see, later on, how to deal with ---partial--- relaxations of Hypothesis \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} and \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}.)

Under regularity assumptions, and under Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, even if the residuals are not normally-distributed, the least square estimators can be \emph{asymptotically normal} and inference can be performed in the same way as in small samples when Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:normality} hold. This derives from Prop. \ref{prp:asymptOLS} (below). The F-test (Prop. \ref{prp:Ftest}) and the t-test (Eq. \eqref{eq:resultstudentt}) can then be performed.

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:asymptOLS}{}\label{prp:asymptOLS}Under Assumptions \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}, suppose that the \((\mathbf{x}_i,\varepsilon_i)\) are i.i.d. with the finite moments required below and that:
\begin{equation}
Q = \mbox{plim}_{n \rightarrow \infty} \frac{\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}}{n},\label{eq:Qasympt}
\end{equation}
where \(Q\) is positive definite. Then:
\begin{equation}
\sqrt{n}(\mathbf{b} - \boldsymbol\beta)\overset{d} {\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^2Q^{-1}\right).\label{eq:convgceOLS}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(\mathbf{b} = \boldsymbol\beta + \left( \frac{\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}}{n}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon}{n}\right)\), we have: \(\sqrt{n}(\mathbf{b} - \boldsymbol\beta) = \left( \frac{\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}}{n}\right)^{-1} \left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\). Since \(f:A \rightarrow A^{-1}\) is continuous at every nonsingular matrix, \(\mbox{plim}_{n \rightarrow \infty} \left(\frac{\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}}{n}\right)^{-1} = \mathbf{Q}^{-1}\) (see Prop. \ref{prp:Slutsky}). Let us denote by \(V_i\) the vector \(\mathbf{x}_i \varepsilon_i\). Because the \((\mathbf{x}_i,\varepsilon_i)\)'s are independent, the \(V_i\)'s are independent as well. Their covariance matrix is \(\sigma^2\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{x}_i \mathbf{x}_i')=\sigma^2Q\). Applying the multivariate central limit theorem on vectors \(V_i\) gives \(\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{x}_i \varepsilon_i\right) = \left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2Q)\). An application of Slutsky's theorem (Prop. \ref{prp:Slutsky}) then leads to the results.
\end{proof}

In practice, \(\sigma^2\) is approximated by \(s^2=\frac{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}}{n-K}\) (Eq. \eqref{eq:s2}) and \(\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\) by \(\left(\frac{\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}}{n}\right)^{-1}\). That is, the covariance matrix of the estimator is approximated by:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\widehat{\mathbb{V}ar}(\mathbf{b}) = s^2 (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}.}\label{eq:sXX}
\end{equation}

Eqs. \eqref{eq:Qasympt} and \eqref{eq:convgceOLS} respectively correspond to convergences in probability and in distribution (see Definitions \ref{def:convergenceproba} and \ref{def:cvgceDistri}, respectively).

\section{Common pitfalls in linear regressions}\label{CommonPitfalls}

The preceding theory clarifies what OLS delivers when its assumptions hold. The next three cases show how the quality of estimation changes when regressors contain little independent variation, relevant variables are omitted, or irrelevant variables are added.

\subsection{Multicollinearity}\label{multicollinearity}

Consider the model: \(y_i = \beta_1 x_{i,1} + \beta_2 x_{i,2} + \varepsilon_i\), where all variables are zero-mean and \(\mathbb{V}ar(\varepsilon_i)=\sigma^2\). We have:
\[
\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X} = \left[ \begin{array}{cc}
\sum_i x_{i,1}^2 & \sum_i x_{i,1} x_{i,2} \\
\sum_i x_{i,1} x_{i,2} & \sum_i x_{i,2}^2
\end{array}\right],
\]
therefore:
\begin{eqnarray*}
(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} &=& \frac{1}{\sum_i x_{i,1}^2\sum_i x_{i,2}^2 - (\sum_i x_{i,1} x_{i,2})^2} \left[ \begin{array}{cc}
\sum_i x_{i,2}^2 & -\sum_i x_{i,1} x_{i,2} \\
-\sum_i x_{i,1} x_{i,2} & \sum_i x_{i,1}^2
\end{array}\right].
\end{eqnarray*}
The reciprocal of the upper-left entry of \((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\) is:
\begin{equation}
\sum_i x_{i,1}^2 - \frac{(\sum_i x_{i,1} x_{i,2})^2}{\sum_i x_{i,2}^2} = \sum_i x_{i,1}^2(1 - correl_{1,2}^2),\label{eq:multicollin}
\end{equation}
where \(correl_{1,2}\) is the sample correlation between \(\mathbf{x}_{1}\) and \(\mathbf{x}_{2}\).

Considering the whole matrix, we have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} &=& \frac{1}{1 - correl_{1,2}^2}\left[ \begin{array}{cc}
\frac{1}{\sum_i x_{i,1}^2}& - \frac{\sum_i x_{i,1} x_{i,2}}{\sum_i x_{i,1}^2\sum_i x_{i,2}^2}\\
- \frac{\sum_i x_{i,1} x_{i,2}}{\sum_i x_{i,1}^2\sum_i x_{i,2}^2} & \frac{1}{\sum_i x_{i,2}^2}
\end{array}\right].
\end{eqnarray*}

Hence, the closer to one \(|correl_{1,2}|\), the higher the variance of \(b_1\) (recall that the variance of \(b_1\) is the upper-left component of \(\sigma^2(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\)). That is, if some of our regressors are close to a linear combination of the other ones, then the confidence intervals will tend to be wide, which typically reduces the power of the t-test (we more often fail to reject the null hypothesis that a coefficient is zero).

\textbf{Coefficients as communicating vessels.} Suppose two regressors move almost together, so that \(x_{i,2}\approx x_{i,1}\). Then
\[
\beta_1x_{i,1}+\beta_2x_{i,2}\approx(\beta_1+\beta_2)x_{i,1}.
\]
The data can tell us about their combined contribution while providing much less information about how to divide it between the two coefficients. Increasing \(\beta_1\) by \(c\) and decreasing \(\beta_2\) by \(c\) changes the fitted value by only \(c(x_{i,1}-x_{i,2})\), which is small when the regressors are close. The two coefficients can compensate for one another, like \emph{communicating vessels} (\emph{vases communicants}). With exact equality of the regressors, their separate coefficients cannot be identified at all.

This also explains the sign of their sampling covariance. Under the homoskedastic, uncorrelated-error assumptions used above, for these two centred regressors,
\[
\operatorname{Corr}(b_1,b_2\mid\mathbf{X})=-correl_{1,2}.
\]
Thus highly \textbf{positively} correlated regressors produce highly \textbf{negatively} correlated coefficient estimates: in repeated samples, an upward estimation error for one coefficient tends to accompany a downward error for the other. With a fixed design, the coefficients themselves are fixed unknown parameters; it is their estimators that are correlated. The negative covariance can keep the estimated sum relatively precise even when each coefficient has a wide confidence interval:
\[
\operatorname{Var}(b_1+b_2\mid\mathbf{X})=
\operatorname{Var}(b_1\mid\mathbf{X})+\operatorname{Var}(b_2\mid\mathbf{X})
+2\operatorname{Cov}(b_1,b_2\mid\mathbf{X}).
\]
