The identity \(-correl_{1,2}\) need not hold for a heteroskedasticity-robust covariance matrix, but the compensating-coefficient mechanism remains useful. For negatively correlated regressors, the compensating changes instead tend to have the same sign.

\Needspace{43\baselineskip}

\begin{example}[Two monthly balances carrying similar information]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:credit-collinearity}{}\label{exm:credit-collinearity}The credit-card data (Appendix \ref{data-credit}) provide a real example. Each of the 30,000 clients has July and August statement balances and a recorded September payment amount. We relate that payment to the two preceding balances. All amounts are expressed in \textbf{thousands of NTD}, so a slope is the change in the fitted September payment for an additional thousand NTD in a regressor.

The July and August balances have correlation \textbf{0.928}. A client with a large balance in one month usually also has a large balance in the other. Either regressor alone therefore captures much of the same information about the client's balance level.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Specification / coefficient & Estimate & SE & Lower 95\% & Upper 95\%\\
\midrule
August alone & 0.065 & 0.003 & 0.059 & 0.072\\
July alone & 0.058 & 0.004 & 0.051 & 0.065\\
Both: August & 0.090 & 0.013 & 0.065 & 0.115\\
Both: July & -0.028 & 0.013 & -0.053 & -0.002\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

When only one balance is included, its slope partly represents the information shared by the two months. Including both asks a more demanding question: how does September's fitted payment change with August's balance \textbf{holding July's balance fixed}, or vice versa? The separate slopes change substantially, and both standard errors increase almost fourfold. The July slope even changes sign. The coefficients now divide information that either regressor could previously proxy for alone.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/credit-collinearity-geometry-1} 

}

\caption{Correlated balances and compensating coefficients. Left: 1,000 clients selected at evenly spaced positions in the source file for display; the correlation and all regressions use all 30,000 clients. Right: approximate joint 95\% confidence region for the two slopes, with the point estimate marked. The downward tilt shows the negative covariance of the coefficient estimates.}\label{fig:credit-collinearity-geometry}
\end{figure}

The estimated correlation between the coefficient estimators is \textbf{-0.966}: a higher August estimate can be offset by a lower July estimate. The large-sample intervals and ellipse allow unequal error variances (Section \ref{HAC}). They describe payment associations, rather than causal effects of changing a statement balance.
\end{example}

\subsection{Omitted variables}\label{Omitted}

A regression coefficient depends on \textbf{what else is held fixed}. A simple regression of \(Y\) on \(x\) compares observations with different values of \(x\), even when they also differ in other characteristics that matter for \(Y\). If those characteristics systematically vary with \(x\), the slope combines several relationships. \textbf{Controls} are additional regressors introduced to separate these relationships and make the comparison of interest more informative.

For example, meters with electric water heaters may draw more electricity partly because they are more often associated with houses than with apartments. Housing type matters for imports through floor area, occupancy and other equipment. A regression on the water-heater indicator alone can therefore attribute some housing-type differences to that indicator. Controlling for housing type lets us compare equipped and unequipped meters within the same housing category.

\textbf{Why omitting a variable changes the slope.} Consider a conditional-mean model with one regressor of interest \(x_i\) and one potential control \(z_i\):
\[
Y_i=\alpha+\beta x_i+\gamma z_i+\varepsilon_i,
\qquad \mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i\mid x_i,z_i)=0.
\]
The coefficient \(\beta\) describes the change in the conditional mean associated with \(x_i\), holding \(z_i\) fixed. If we omit \(z_i\), the population slope of the simple regression becomes
\begin{equation}
\beta_{\mathrm{short}}=\beta+\gamma
\frac{\mathbb{C}ov(x_i,z_i)}{\mathbb{V}ar(x_i)}.\label{eq:omitted-scalar}
\end{equation}
Thus omitting \(z_i\) distorts the target slope when \textbf{both} conditions hold: \(z_i\) contributes to the conditional mean (\(\gamma\ne0\)), and it is correlated with \(x_i\). The sign of the distortion depends on both terms. It can exaggerate, attenuate or even reverse the slope. A larger sample estimates \(\beta_{\mathrm{short}}\) more precisely; it does not remove its difference from \(\beta\).

The same mechanism applies to several omitted variables. Write the full model as
\[
\mathbf{y} = \underbrace{\mathbf{X}_1}_{n \times K_1}\underbrace{\boldsymbol\beta_1}_{K_1 \times 1} + \underbrace{\mathbf{X}_2}_{n\times K_2}\underbrace{\boldsymbol\beta_2}_{K_2 \times 1} + \boldsymbol\varepsilon,
\qquad \mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\mid\mathbf{X}_1,\mathbf{X}_2)=\mathbf{0}.
\]
Here \(\mathbf{X}_1\) contains the retained regressors (including an intercept), and \(\mathbf{X}_2\) contains the omitted controls. Regressing \(\mathbf{y}\) on \(\mathbf{X}_1\) alone gives
\[
\mathbf{b}_1 = \boldsymbol\beta_1 + (\mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2 +
(\mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1'\boldsymbol\varepsilon.
\]
Consequently, with \(\mathbf{X}=[\mathbf{X}_1,\mathbf{X}_2]\),
\[
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{b}_1\mid\mathbf{X}) = \boldsymbol\beta_1 + \underbrace{(\mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1'\mathbf{X}_2}_{K_1 \times K_2}\boldsymbol\beta_2.
\]
Each column of the matrix multiplying \(\boldsymbol\beta_2\) contains the coefficients from regressing an omitted variable on the retained regressors. The bias therefore combines how the omitted variables vary with the retained regressors and how they contribute to the outcome. It vanishes when the resulting product is zero; with several omitted variables, their contributions can also offset one another.

\textbf{What a control actually does.} The Frisch--Waugh theorem (Theorem \ref{thm:FW}) gives a precise interpretation. Remove the linear associations with the controls from both \(Y\) and the regressor of interest, then regress the remaining part of \(Y\) on the remaining part of \(x\). The slope equals that in the full regression. Controls therefore change the variation used to estimate the slope: we compare observations after accounting for their measured differences in the control variables. This does not require finding observations with exactly identical control values; the adjustment relies on the specified regression model.

Controls should be chosen to match the question and the mechanisms linking the variables, rather than simply to increase \(R^2\) or obtain a desired p-value. For a causal interpretation, we need controls that make the conditional mean-zero assumption credible. Including a variable that is itself a consequence of the regressor can change the effect being estimated; adding many variables is not a substitute for deciding what should be held fixed.

\Needspace{8\baselineskip}

\begin{example}[Electric water heaters: the role of housing type]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:electricity-controls}{}\label{exm:electricity-controls}Do meters with an electric water heater draw more electricity from the grid? We use \texttt{AEC::CHelectricity\_annual} (Appendix \ref{data-electricity}), keeping \textbf{2023 meters with all 365 daily import totals observed and no reported equipment changes}.

\textbf{One row is one meter over one year} (1229 meters). The outcome is mean daily grid imports (kWh/day). Let \(H_i\) indicate a water heater and \(D_i\) a house rather than an apartment. Compare
\[
Y_i=a+bH_i+u_i
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
Y_i=\alpha+\beta H_i+\gamma D_i+\varepsilon_i.
\]
The first slope pools housing types. The second allows a separate house baseline and estimates the equipment difference \textbf{holding housing type fixed}.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Specification & Water-heater coefficient (kWh/day)\\
\midrule
Water-heater indicator only & 18.58\\
Controlling for housing type & 7.07\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The estimated difference falls from \textbf{18.58 to 7.07 kWh/day}. Why? Houses have higher average imports for other reasons, and electric water heaters are much more common among houses: \textbf{67.2\%}, compared with \textbf{11.6\%} of apartments. The uncontrolled water-heater indicator partly proxies for housing type.

The within-type comparisons reveal what the control changes:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{\label{tab:electricity-controls-within}Mean grid imports in kWh/day; numbers of meters in parentheses.}
\centering
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}[t]{lllr}
\toprule
Meter type & Without: mean (n) & With: mean (n) & Difference\\
\midrule
Apartment & 6.63 (804) & 13.08 (105) & 6.45\\
Single-family house & 26.70 (105) & 34.59 (215) & 7.89\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

Within apartments, the difference is \textbf{6.45 kWh/day}; within houses it is \textbf{7.89}. Both are much smaller than the pooled difference. The adjusted regression imposes a common water-heater slope across housing types; its estimate lies between these two within-type differences. The weighting reflects both group size and variation in equipment status within each group.

This is also a redistribution of explanatory roles: housing differences previously attributed partly to the equipment indicator now have their own coefficient. The water-heater estimate still reflects other differences in size, occupancy and equipment; it is an adjusted association, rather than a causal installation effect. The sample excludes five changing-equipment house meters from Example \ref{exm:electricity-heteroskedasticity}.
\end{example}

\subsection{Irrelevant variable}\label{irrelevant}

Consider the \emph{true model}:
\[
\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}_1\boldsymbol\beta_1 + \boldsymbol\varepsilon,
\]
while the \emph{estimated model} is:
\[
\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}_1\boldsymbol\beta_1 + \mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2 + \boldsymbol\varepsilon
\]

The estimates are unbiased. However, adding irrelevant explanatory variables increases the variance of the estimate of \(\boldsymbol\beta_1\) (compared to the case where one uses the correct explanatory variables). This is the case unless the correlation between \(\mathbf{X}_1\) and \(\mathbf{X}_2\) is null, see Eq. \eqref{eq:multicollin}.

In other words, the estimator is \emph{inefficient}, i.e., there exists an alternative consistent estimator whose variance is lower. The inefficiency problem can have serious consequences when testing hypotheses such as \(H_0: \beta_1 = 0\). Due to the loss of power, we might wrongly infer that the \(\mathbf{X}_1\) variables are not ``relevant'' (\emph{Type-II error, False Negative}).

\section{Confidence and prediction intervals}\label{regression-prediction-intervals}

A fitted regression can answer two different questions. What is the \textbf{mean outcome} at given regressor values? And what outcome might we observe for \textbf{one new observation} at those same values? The distinction determines which interval we need.

Let \(\mathbf{x}_0\) be a specified vector of regressor values, including the intercept. Under the linear conditional-mean model,
\[
m_0 := \mathbb{E}(Y_0\mid\mathbf{x}_0)=\mathbf{x}_0'\boldsymbol\beta,
\qquad \widehat m_0=\mathbf{x}_0'\mathbf{b}.
\]
Here the subscript 0 denotes a new observation, rather than an observation used to fit the model.

\textbf{A confidence interval for the conditional mean.} The target \(m_0\) is an unknown mean. The interval reflects uncertainty about the estimated coefficients. If \(\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_b\) estimates their covariance matrix, the estimated standard error of \(\widehat m_0\) is
\begin{equation}
\widehat{\operatorname{se}}(\widehat m_0)
=\sqrt{\mathbf{x}_0'\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_b\mathbf{x}_0}.\label{eq:mean-prediction-se}
\end{equation}
A large-sample 95\% confidence interval is therefore
\[
\widehat m_0\ \pm\ 1.96\sqrt{\mathbf{x}_0'\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_b\mathbf{x}_0}.
\]
Under the classical Gaussian model, using \(\widehat{\mathbf{V}}_b=s^2(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\) and replacing 1.96 by \(\Phi^{-1}_{t(n-K)}(.975)\) gives an exact conditional interval. With a suitable covariance estimator, the large-sample construction also applies beyond that model. Both constructions require the conditional-mean specification to be correct.

\textbf{A prediction interval for a new outcome.} Now the target is the realised value \(Y_0=m_0+\varepsilon_0\). Even if we knew the coefficients exactly, the new disturbance would remain unknown. Under the classical Gaussian model, with the new disturbance independent of the estimation sample and having the same variance \(\sigma^2\), the prediction-error variance is
\[
\operatorname{Var}(Y_0-\widehat m_0\mid\mathbf{X},\mathbf{x}_0)
=\underbrace{\sigma^2}_{\text{new disturbance}}
+\underbrace{\sigma^2\mathbf{x}_0'(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_0}_{\text{coefficient uncertainty}}.
\]
An exact 95\% prediction interval under these assumptions is
\begin{equation}
\widehat m_0\ \pm\ \Phi^{-1}_{t(n-K)}(.975)\,s
\sqrt{1+\mathbf{x}_0'(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{x}_0}.\label{eq:regression-prediction-interval}
\end{equation}
Compared with the confidence interval for the mean, the additional \textbf{1 inside the square root} accounts for the new disturbance. As the estimation sample grows, uncertainty about the mean can shrink, while the dispersion of a new outcome around that mean remains. Thus a narrow confidence interval for the mean does not imply accurate prediction of an individual outcome.

Outside the Gaussian independent-error model, a prediction interval needs additional information about the distribution of the new disturbance and its dependence on the sample. A covariance estimate for the coefficients alone is not sufficient.

\begin{example}[Mean accident counts at a given rainfall level]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:rain-mean-interval}{}\label{exm:rain-mean-interval}Return to the simple Paris regression in Example \ref{exm:rainOLS}. At rainfall \(r\) mm, the regressor vector is \(\mathbf{x}_0=(1,r/10)'\). We estimate the \textbf{mean daily accident count at that rainfall level}, using the fitted line as a linear approximation to the conditional mean.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/paris-rain-mean-band-1} 

}

\caption{Daily injury accidents and rainfall in Paris. The line estimates the conditional mean under the linear specification; the shaded region gives pointwise 95\% confidence intervals for that mean. It is not a prediction band for individual days.}\label{fig:paris-rain-mean-band}
\end{figure}

The band is narrow where the data provide precise information about the line and widens at high rainfall levels, where observations are scarce. Many daily counts lie outside it: this is expected, because the band concerns the \textbf{mean}, not individual days. The covariance estimate allows for unequal error variances and dependence between successive days; its construction is deferred to Section \ref{HAC}.

These are \textbf{pointwise} 95\% intervals: the coverage statement applies at each fixed rainfall level, not jointly to the entire curve. The band quantifies uncertainty about the fitted linear mean; it does not account for a possible failure of the linear specification. We do not draw the classical prediction interval for these count data, since its Gaussian independent-error assumptions are not justified here.
\end{example}

\section{Instrumental Variables}\label{IV}

Omitted variables are one reason why a regressor may be correlated with the error. Simultaneity and measurement error can create the same problem. When the conditional mean-zero assumption \(\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X})=0\) is implausible, OLS generally fails to estimate the parameter consistently. Instrumental variables recover identifying variation from variables that are related to the endogenous regressors but orthogonal to the structural error.

Consider the following model:
\begin{equation}
y_i = \mathbf{x_i}'\boldsymbol\beta + \varepsilon_i, \quad \mbox{where } \mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i)=0  \mbox{ and } \mathbf{x_i}\not\perp \varepsilon_i.\label{eq:modelIV}
\end{equation}

Let us illustrate how this situation may result in biased OLS estimate. Consider for instance the situation where:
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i)=0 \quad \mbox{and} \quad \mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i \mathbf{x_i})=\boldsymbol\gamma,\label{eq:exmIV}
\end{equation}
in which case we have \(\mathbf{x}_i\not\perp \varepsilon_i\) (consistently with Eq. \eqref{eq:modelIV}).

By the law of large numbers, \(\mbox{plim}_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon / n = \boldsymbol\gamma\). If \(\mathbf{Q}_{xx} := \mbox{plim } \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}/n\), the OLS estimator is not consistent because
\[
\mathbf{b} = \boldsymbol\beta + (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon \overset{p}{\rightarrow} \boldsymbol\beta + \mathbf{Q}_{xx}^{-1}\boldsymbol\gamma \ne \boldsymbol\beta.
\]

Let us now introduce the notion of instruments.

\begin{definition}[Instrumental variables]
\protect\hypertarget{def:instruments}{}\label{def:instruments}The \(L\)-dimensional vector \(\mathbf{z}_i\) is a valid instrument vector for the linear model if:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\operatorname{rank}\mathbb E(\mathbf{z}_i\mathbf{x}_i')=K\) (\textbf{relevance});
\item
  \(\mathbb E(\mathbf{z}_i\varepsilon_i)=\mathbf{0}\) (\textbf{exogeneity});
\item
  \(\mathbb E(\mathbf{z}_i\mathbf{z}_i')\) is finite and positive definite.
\end{enumerate}

Included exogenous regressors also enter the instrument vector. The stronger condition \(\mathbb E(\varepsilon_i\mid\mathbf{z}_i)=0\) is sufficient for (b), but the linear IV moment condition itself does not require it.
\end{definition}

Point (a) implies in particular that the dimension of \(\mathbf{z}_i\) has to be at least as large as that of \(\mathbf{x}_i\). If \(\mathbf{z}_i\) is a valid set of instruments, we have:
\[
\mbox{plim}\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{y}}{n} \right) =\mbox{plim}\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'(\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta + \boldsymbol\varepsilon)}{n} \right) = \mbox{plim}\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}}{n} \right)\boldsymbol\beta.
\]
Indeed, by the law of large numbers, \(\frac{\mathbf{Z}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon}{n} \overset{p}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{z}_i\varepsilon_i)=0\).

If \(L = K\), the matrix \(\frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}}{n}\) is of dimension \(K \times K\) and we have:
\[
\left[\mbox{plim }\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}}{n} \right)\right]^{-1}\mbox{plim }\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{y}}{n} \right) = \boldsymbol\beta.
\]
By continuity of matrix inversion at a nonsingular limit: \(\left[\mbox{plim }\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}}{n} \right)\right]^{-1}=\mbox{plim }\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}}{n} \right)^{-1}\).
The Slutsky Theorem (Prop. \ref{prp:Slutsky}) further implies that:
\[
\mbox{plim }\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}}{n} \right)^{-1} \mbox{plim }\left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{y}}{n} \right)  = \mbox{plim }\left( \left( \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}}{n} \right)^{-1} \frac{\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{y}}{n} \right).
\]
Hence \(\mathbf{b}_{iv}\) is consistent if it is defined by:
\[
\boxed{\mathbf{b}_{iv} = (\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{y}.}
\]

\begin{proposition}[Asymptotic distribution of the IV estimator]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:IV}{}\label{prp:IV}Under valid instruments, the rank and central-limit conditions, and homoskedastic, uncorrelated structural errors with variance \(\sigma^2\), for \(L=K\) the limiting distribution is:
\[
\sqrt n(\mathbf{b}_{iv}-\boldsymbol\beta) \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0},\sigma^2\left[Q_{xz}Q_{zz}^{-1}Q_{zx}\right]^{-1}\right)
\]
where \(\mbox{plim } \mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{Z}/n =: \mathbf{Q}_{zz}\), \(\mbox{plim } \mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}/n =: \mathbf{Q}_{zx}\), \(\mbox{plim } \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{Z}/n =: \mathbf{Q}_{xz}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The proof is very similar to that of Prop. \ref{prp:asymptOLS}, the starting point being that \(\mathbf{b}_{iv} = \boldsymbol\beta + (\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\).
\end{proof}

When \(L=K\), we have:
\[
\left[Q_{xz}Q_{zz}^{-1}Q_{zx}\right]^{-1}=Q_{zx}^{-1}Q_{zz}Q_{xz}^{-1}.
\]
In practice, to estimate \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}_{iv}) = \frac{\sigma^2}{n}Q_{zx}^{-1}Q_{zz}Q_{xz}^{-1}\), we replace \(\sigma^2\) by:
\[
s_{iv}^2 = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} (y_i - \mathbf{x}_i'\mathbf{b}_{iv})^2.
\]

What about when \(L > K\)? In this case, we proceed as follows:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Regress \(\mathbf{X}\) on the space spanned by \(\mathbf{Z}\) and
\item
  Regress \(\mathbf{y}\) on the fitted values \(\hat{\mathbf{X}}:=\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}\).
\end{enumerate}

This two-step approach is called \textbf{Two-Stage Least Squares (2SLS)}. It results in:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mathbf{b}_{iv} = [\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{X}]^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{Y}.} \label{eq:IV}
\end{equation}

Under the same covariance and regularity assumptions, Prop. \ref{prp:IV} also gives the normal approximation for the 2SLS estimator in Eq. \eqref{eq:IV}. Robust inference is discussed in Section \ref{regression-grm}.

If the instruments do not properly satisfy Condition (a) in Def. \ref{def:instruments} (i.e.~if \(\mathbf{x}_i\) and \(\mathbf{z}_i\) are only loosely related), the instruments are said to be \textbf{weak} (see, e.g., \citet{stock_yogo_2005}, available \href{http://scholar.harvard.edu/files/stock/files/testing_for_weak_instruments_in_linear_iv_regression.pdf}{here} or \citet{Andrews_Stock_Sun_2019}). With one endogenous regressor, a first-stage joint test assesses whether the excluded instruments predict it after the included controls. A large statistic provides evidence of relevance, but a small p-value alone does not ensure reliable IV inference. The appropriate diagnostic and its reference values depend on the error structure and number of instruments. Instrument relevance and the exclusion restriction are separate questions.

The Durbin-Wu-Hausman test (\citet{Durbin_1954}, \citet{Wu_1973}, \citet{Hausman_1978}) can be used to test if IV necessary. (IV techniques are required if \(\mbox{plim}_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon / n \ne 0\).) \href{http://www.jstor.org/stable/1913827?seq=1\#page_scan_tab_contents}{Hausman (1978)} proposes a test of the efficiency of estimators. Under the null hypothesis two estimators, \(\mathbf{b}_0\) and \(\mathbf{b}_1\), are consistent but \(\mathbf{b}_0\) is (asymptotically) efficient relative to \(\mathbf{b}_1\). Under the alternative hypothesis, \(\mathbf{b}_1\) (IV in the present case) remains consistent but not \(\mathbf{b}_0\) (OLS in the present case). Under the maintained validity of the instruments and the test's covariance assumptions, rejection provides evidence against the consistency of OLS. It does not validate the instruments; failure to reject does not establish exogeneity.

The test statistic is:
\[
H = (\mathbf{b}_1 - \mathbf{b}_0)' MPI(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}_1) - \mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}_0))(\mathbf{b}_1 - \mathbf{b}_0),
\]
where \(MPI\) is the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse. Under the null hypothesis, \(H \sim \chi^2(q)\), where \(q\) is the rank of \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}_1) - \mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}_0)\).

\begin{example}[Using supply shifts to estimate demand]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:priceElasticity}{}\label{exm:priceElasticity}Suppose a market clears where supply equals demand. Let \(p_t\) and \(q_t\) denote log price and log quantity, \(w_t\) an income variable, and \(z_t\) a favourable supply shifter:
\[
 q_t^d=1-p_t+w_t+u_t,\qquad q_t^s=-1+p_t+z_t+v_t.
\]
The four shocks \(w_t,z_t,u_t,v_t\) are independent standard normal variables. The demand elasticity is therefore \(-1\). Equilibrium gives
\[
 p_t=\frac{2+w_t-z_t+u_t-v_t}{2}.
\]
Price contains the demand shock \(u_t\), so regressing quantity on price and income violates exogeneity. By contrast, \(z_t\) shifts supply without entering demand or sharing its unobserved shocks. It is a valid excluded instrument by construction. Figure \ref{fig:figureIV} shows why this distinction matters.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/figureIV-1} 

}

\caption{Equilibrium movements and identification. Left: exogenous supply shifts trace a fixed demand curve. Right: demand shocks move both price and quantity along a fixed supply curve. Orange dots mark equilibria. Price therefore responds to demand shocks as well as to excluded supply shifters.}\label{fig:figureIV}
\end{figure}

In 5,000 simulated market equilibria, the two estimators give:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Method & Price coefficient & True elasticity\\
\midrule
OLS & -0.333 & -1\\
IV & -0.978 & -1\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

OLS mixes supply and demand movements: its price coefficient converges to \(-1+2/3=-1/3\). IV uses the supply-induced price variation and converges to the demand elasticity \(-1\). The simulation makes the identifying assumption transparent because we specified the independent shocks ourselves. With observed data, that assumption needs an economic argument.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[How does Swiss hydropower respond to price?]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:IVhydro}{}\label{exm:IVhydro}A reservoir operator can generate electricity today or keep water for later. A higher current price should encourage generation, other opportunities held fixed. But price and production are jointly determined: abundant water can increase Swiss production and lower the price. OLS can therefore mix a response to price with shifts in supply.

We use 3,288 days of observed Swiss electricity-market data, 2016--2024, from \texttt{AEC::CHhydro} (Appendix \ref{data-hydro}). The outcome \(Q_t\) is the \textbf{average power generated by reservoir plants that day}, in MW. The explanatory variable \(P_t\) is the Swiss wholesale price, measured in units of 10 EUR/MWh. The coefficient \(\beta\) in
\[
 Q_t=\alpha+\beta P_t+\boldsymbol\gamma'\mathbf{W}_t+u_t
\]
therefore measures the change in generation, in MW, associated with a 10 EUR/MWh increase in price. This is a production decision, distinct from household electricity consumption or electricity used for pumping.

\textbf{The instrument.} Let \(Z_t\) be German wind generation, in GW. More wind adds supply outside Switzerland. Through the interconnected market it can lower the price available to Swiss producers and the residual demand they face. In the diagram of Example \ref{exm:priceElasticity}, supply shifts identified demand; here an outside-market shift helps trace \textbf{Swiss supply}. The proposed chain is
\[
 \text{more German wind}\quad\longrightarrow\quad
 \text{lower Swiss price}\quad\longrightarrow\quad
 \text{less Swiss reservoir generation}.
\]

Both stages include the same controls \(\mathbf{W}_t\): month-by-year indicators, weekday indicators, and temperatures and their squares at Sion and Güttingen. They account for market conditions, calendar patterns and temperature-related electricity needs. The two stations are weather proxies; full details are in the appendix. We keep all days, including the six with negative average prices, and estimate the model in levels.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Method & Generation change for +10 EUR/MWh (MW)\\
\midrule
OLS & 47.7\\
IV & 344.7\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The IV estimate is approximately \textbf{345 MW per 10 EUR/MWh}, compared with 48 MW by OLS. To see where it comes from, consider the two regressions on wind. After the controls, an additional GW of German wind predicts a Swiss price change of -0.61 EUR/MWh and a generation change of -21.1 MW. Both are negative, so their ratio gives a positive production response to price.

\begin{figure}
\centering
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/figure-hydro-iv-1.pdf}}
\caption{\label{fig:figure-hydro-iv}The two comparisons behind the Swiss hydropower IV estimate. Both panels remove the calendar and temperature controls. Grey points are daily observations; coloured points are means within 20 wind bins of approximately equal size. Lines use all 3,288 days. The first-stage and reduced-form slopes are both negative.}
\end{figure}

\textbf{What must we assume?} Conditional on the controls, German wind must affect Swiss reservoir generation through the Swiss price, rather than through other determinants of the production decision. Common weather and water availability can challenge this assumption. Transmission constraints, wind curtailment and expected future prices can matter too: an operator's choice to store water depends on future opportunities. The observed slopes support the proposed mechanism but do not prove exclusion.

The table reports point estimates. Example \ref{exm:hydro-inference}, after the discussion of serial dependence, adds appropriate uncertainty estimates and sensitivity checks. Those checks also show why we should interpret the coefficient as a short-run dispatch response, rather than a universal technological elasticity.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Measurement error and a second measurement]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:IVmeasurement}{}\label{exm:IVmeasurement}A measured regressor can be endogenous even without simultaneous decisions. Suppose the true relationship is \(y_i=1+2x_i^*+\varepsilon_i\), but we observe a noisy measurement \(x_i=x_i^*+v_i\). Substitution gives \(y_i=1+2x_i+(\varepsilon_i-2v_i)\): the observed regressor and the new error share \(v_i\).

A second measurement \(z_i=x_i^*+u_i\) supplies an instrument if it shares the true signal but not the measurement error. For this simulation, assume \(x_i^*,\varepsilon_i,v_i,u_i\) are mutually independent standard normal variables. We compare OLS with IV, then verify the two-stage coefficient directly.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Method & Slope\\
\midrule
OLS & 0.989\\
Manual two-stage coefficient & 2.003\\
2SLS & 2.003\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

Since the latent variable and the measurement noise have unit variance, the OLS slope converges to 1 instead of the true value 2. IV corrects the attenuation under the stated assumptions. The manual second regression reproduces the 2SLS coefficient; its ordinary OLS standard error would be inappropriate. Use the IV covariance estimator for inference.
\end{example}

\section{General Regression Model (GRM) and robust covariance matrices}\label{regression-grm}

The OLS and IV sections focused primarily on whether coefficients are identified and consistently estimated. Inference introduces a separate issue: the familiar covariance formula assumes homoskedastic, mutually uncorrelated errors. The general regression model allows a richer error covariance structure and leads to appropriate standard errors when those assumptions fail.

\subsection{Presentation of the General Regression Model (GRM)}\label{presentation-of-the-general-regression-model-grm}

It will prove useful to introduce the following notation:
\begin{eqnarray}
\mathbb{V}ar(\boldsymbol\varepsilon | \mathbf{X}) = \mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon \boldsymbol\varepsilon'| \mathbf{X}) &=& \boldsymbol\Sigma. \label{eq:assumGLS2}
\end{eqnarray}

Note that Eq. \eqref{eq:assumGLS2} is more general than Hypothesis \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} and \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid} because the diagonal entries of \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) may be different (as opposed to under Hypothesis \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity}), and the non-diagonal entries of \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) can be non-null (as opposed to under Hypothesis \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}).

\begin{definition}[General Regression Model (GRM)]
\protect\hypertarget{def:GRM}{}\label{def:GRM}Hypothesis \ref{hyp:fullrank} and \ref{hyp:exogeneity}, together with Eq. \eqref{eq:assumGLS2}, form the General Regression Model (GRM) framework.
\end{definition}

Naturally, a regression model where Hypotheses \ref{hyp:fullrank} to \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid} hold is a specific case of the GRM framework.

The GRM context notably encompasses situations of heteroskedasticity and autocorrelation:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Heteroskedasticity:
  \begin{equation}
  \boldsymbol\Sigma = \left[  \begin{array}{cccc}
  \sigma_1^2 & 0 & \dots & 0 \\
  0 & \sigma_2^2 &  & 0 \\
  \vdots && \ddots& \vdots \\
  0 & \dots & 0 & \sigma_n^2
  \end{array} \right]. \label{eq:heteroskedasticity}
  \end{equation}
\item
  Autocorrelation:
  \begin{equation}
  \boldsymbol\Sigma = \sigma^2 \left[ \begin{array}{cccc}
  1 & \rho_{2,1} & \dots & \rho_{n,1} \\
  \rho_{2,1} & 1 &  & \vdots \\
  \vdots && \ddots& \rho_{n,n-1} \\
  \rho_{n,1} & \rho_{n,2} & \dots & 1
  \end{array} \right]. \label{eq:autocorrelation}
  \end{equation}
\end{itemize}

\begin{example}[Auto-regressive processes]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:autocorrelaaa}{}\label{exm:autocorrelaaa}Autocorrelation is common in time-series contexts (see Section \ref{TS}). In a time-series context, subscript \(i\) refers to a date.

Assume for instance that:
\begin{equation}
y_i = \mathbf{x}_i' \boldsymbol\beta + \varepsilon_i \label{eq:usual}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\varepsilon_i = \rho \varepsilon_{i-1} + v_i, \quad v_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_v^2).\label{eq:usual2}
\end{equation}
If \(|\rho|<1\) and the errors start in their stationary distribution, their covariance matrix is:
\begin{equation}
\boldsymbol\Sigma =\frac{ \sigma_v^2}{1 - \rho^2} \left[    \begin{array}{cccc}
1 & \rho & \dots & \rho^{n-1} \\
\rho & 1 &  & \vdots \\
\vdots && \ddots& \rho \\
\rho^{n-1} & \rho^{n-2} & \dots & 1
\end{array} \right].\label{eq:SigmaAutocorrel}
\end{equation}
\end{example}

In some cases ---in particular when one assumes a parametric formulation for \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\)--- one can determine a better (more accurate) estimator than the OLS one. This approach is called Generalized Least Squares (GLS), which we present below.

\subsection{Generalized Least Squares}\label{GLS}

First suppose \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) is known. Because \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) is symmetric positive, it admits a spectral decomposition of the form \(\boldsymbol\Sigma = \mathbf{C} \boldsymbol\Lambda \mathbf{C}'\), where \(\mathbf{C}\) is an orthogonal matrix (i.e.~\(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}'=\mathbf{Id}\)) and \(\boldsymbol\Lambda\) is a diagonal matrix (the diagonal entries are the eigenvalues of \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\)).

We have \(\boldsymbol\Sigma = (\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}')^{-1}\) with \(\mathbf{P} = \mathbf{C}\boldsymbol\Lambda^{-1/2}\). Consider the transformed model:
\[
\mathbf{P}'\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{P}'\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta + \mathbf{P}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon \quad \mbox{or} \quad \mathbf{y}^* = \mathbf{X}^*\boldsymbol\beta + \boldsymbol\varepsilon^*.
\]
The variance of \(\boldsymbol\varepsilon^*\) is the identity matrix \(\mathbf{Id}\). In the transformed model, OLS is BLUE (Gauss-Markov Theorem \ref{thm:GaussMarkov}).

The \textbf{Generalized least squares} estimator of \(\boldsymbol\beta\) is:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mathbf{b}_{GLS} = (\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{y}.}\label{eq:betaGLS}
\end{equation}
We have:
\[
\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}_{GLS}|\mathbf{X}) = (\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{X})^{-1}.
\]

However, in general, \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) is unknown. The GLS estimator is then said to be \emph{infeasible}. Some structure is required. Assume \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) admits a parametric form \(\boldsymbol\Sigma(\theta)\). The estimation becomes \emph{feasible} (FGLS) if one replaces \(\boldsymbol\Sigma(\theta)\) by \(\boldsymbol\Sigma(\hat\theta)\), where \(\hat\theta\) is a consistent estimator of \(\theta\). In that case, the FGLS is asymptotically efficient (see Example \ref{exm:autocorrelaaa2}).

When we do not specify a parametric model for \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\), we can retain OLS, or IV when valid instruments are needed, and estimate its covariance robustly. The coefficient estimator and the covariance estimator answer different questions: the former determines the fitted relationship; the latter determines the reported uncertainty. Robust standard errors do not repair endogeneity.

\begin{example}[GLS in the auto-correlation case]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:autocorrelaaa2}{}\label{exm:autocorrelaaa2}Return to the stationary AR(1) errors in Example \ref{exm:autocorrelaaa}. Fit the original regression, then regress its residuals on their one-period lags to estimate \(\rho\). Under the model's regularity conditions, the residual regression also estimates the innovation variance. Substituting these estimates into Eq. \eqref{eq:SigmaAutocorrel} gives a feasible GLS procedure. Its efficiency gain depends on the AR(1) covariance specification being appropriate (\citet{Cochrane_Orcutt_1949}).
\end{example}

\subsection{Asymptotic properties of the OLS estimator in the GRM framework}\label{asymptotic-properties-of-the-ols-estimator-in-the-grm-framework}

Since \(\mathbf{b} = \boldsymbol\beta + \left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1} \mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) and \(\mathbb{V}ar(\boldsymbol\varepsilon|\mathbf{X})=\boldsymbol\Sigma\), we have:
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b}|\mathbf{X}) = \frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma\mathbf{X}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}.\label{eq:xsx}
\end{equation}

Therefore, the conditional covariance matrix of the OLS estimator is not \(\sigma^2 (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\) any longer, and using \(s^2 (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\) for inference may be misleading. Below, we will see how to construct appropriate estimates of the covariance matrix of \(\mathbf{b}\). Before that, let us prove that the OLS estimator remains consistent in the GRM framework.

\begin{proposition}[Consistency of the OLS estimator in the GRM framework]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:ols-grm-consistency}{}\label{prp:ols-grm-consistency}Under the conditional mean-zero assumption, suppose \(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}/n\) converges in probability to a finite positive definite matrix and \(\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma\mathbf{X}/n\) converges in probability to a finite matrix. Then \(\mathbf{b}\overset p\longrightarrow\boldsymbol\beta\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{eq:xsx} gives a conditional covariance matrix converging to zero in probability. Conditional Chebyshev bounds the probability of any fixed estimation error by a term converging to zero, capped at one. Taking expectations of this bounded term proves convergence in probability. Mean-square convergence would additionally require control of the corresponding unconditional second moments.
\end{proof}

Prop. \ref{prp:AsymptGRM} gives the asymptotic distribution of the OLS estimator in the GRM framework.

\begin{proposition}[Asymptotic distribution of the OLS estimator in the GRM framework]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:AsymptGRM}{}\label{prp:AsymptGRM}In addition to the preceding moment limits, assume a score central limit theorem: \(n^{-1/2}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\overset d\longrightarrow\mathcal N(0,Q_{x\boldsymbol\Sigma x})\). If \(Q_{xx}=\mbox{plim }(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}/n)\) and \(Q_{x\boldsymbol\Sigma x}=\mbox{plim }(\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma\mathbf{X}/n)\) are finite positive definite matrices, then:
\[
\sqrt{n}(\mathbf{b}-\boldsymbol\beta) \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}(0,Q_{xx}^{-1}Q_{x\boldsymbol\Sigma x}Q_{xx}^{-1}).
\]
\end{proposition}

The IV estimator also features a normal asymptotic distribution:

\begin{proposition}[Asymptotic distribution of the IV estimator in the GRM framework]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:AsymptIVGRM}{}\label{prp:AsymptIVGRM}Assume valid instruments, the stated rank and moment limits, and a central limit theorem for \(n^{-1/2}\mathbf{Z}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon\). Then the large-sample approximation is:
\[
\mathbf{b}_{iv} \overset{a}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol\beta,\mathbf{V}_{iv}),
\]
where
\[
\mathbf{V}_{iv} = \frac{1}{n}(\mathbf{Q}^*)\mbox{ plim }\left(\frac{1}{n} \mathbf{Z}'\boldsymbol\Sigma \mathbf{Z}\right)(\mathbf{Q}^*)',
\]
with
\[
\mathbf{Q}^* = [\mathbf{Q}_{xz}\mathbf{Q}_{zz}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{zx}]^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{xz}\mathbf{Q}_{zz}^{-1}.
\]
\end{proposition}

For practical purposes, one needs to have estimates of \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) in Props. \ref{prp:AsymptGRM} or \ref{prp:AsymptIVGRM}. The complication comes from the fact that \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) is of dimension \(n \times n\), and its estimation ---based on a sample of length \(n\)--- is therefore infeasible in the general case. Notwithstanding, looking at Eq. \eqref{eq:xsx}, it appears that one can focus on the estimation of \(Q_{x\boldsymbol\Sigma x}=\mbox{plim }(\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma\mathbf{X}/n)\) (or \(\mbox{plim }\left(\frac{1}{n} \mathbf{Z}'\boldsymbol\Sigma \mathbf{Z}\right)\) in the IV case). This matrix being of dimension \(K \times K\), its estimation is easier.

We have:
\begin{equation}
\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma\mathbf{X} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}'_j. \label{eq:GeneralXSigmaX}
\end{equation}

The so-called \textbf{robust covariance matrices} are estimates of the previous matrix. Their computation is based on the fact that if \(\mathbf{b}\) is consistent, then the \(e_i\)'s are consistent estimators of the \(\varepsilon_i\)'s.

In the following sections (\ref{HAC} and \ref{Clusters}), we present two types of robust covariance matrices.

\subsection{HAC-robust covariance matrices}\label{HAC}

When only heteroskedasticity prevails, i.e., when matrix \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) is as in Eq. \eqref{eq:heteroskedasticity}, then one can use the formula proposed by \citet{White_1980} to estimate \(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma\mathbf{X}\) (see Example \ref{exm:HCheteroskedasticity}). When the residuals feature both heteroskedasticity and auto-correlation, then one can use the \citet{Newey_West_1987} approach (see Example \ref{exm:HCheterAC}).

\begin{example}[Heteroskedasticity]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:HCheteroskedasticity}{}\label{exm:HCheteroskedasticity}This is the case of Eq. \eqref{eq:heteroskedasticity}. We have \(\sigma_{i,j}=0\) for \(i \ne j\). Hence, in this case, we then need to estimate \(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^2\mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}'_i\). \citet{White_1980} has shown that, under general conditions:
\begin{equation}
\mbox{plim}\left( \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^2\mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}'_i \right) =
\mbox{plim}\left( \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^2\mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}'_i \right). \label{eq:white}
\end{equation}
The estimator of \(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma\mathbf{X}\) therefore is:
\begin{equation}
M_{HC0} = \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}'
\left[
\begin{array}{cccc}
e_1^2 & 0 & \dots & 0 \\
0 & e_2^2 &  \\
\vdots & & \ddots&0 \\
0 & \dots & 0 & e_n^2
\end{array}
\right]
\mathbf{X}.\label{eq:White}
\end{equation}
where the \(e_i\) are the OLS residuals of the regression. The previous estimator is often called \textbf{HC0}. The \textbf{HC1} estimator, due to \citet{MacKinnon_White_1985}, is obtained by applying an adjustment factor \(n/(n-K)\) for the number of degrees of freedom (as in Prop. \ref{prp:expects2}). That is:
\begin{equation}
M_{HC1} = \frac{n}{n-K}M_{HC0}.\label{eq:WhiteHC1}
\end{equation}

We can illustrate the influence of heteroskedasticity using simulations. Consider the following model:
\[
y_i = x_i + \varepsilon_i, \quad \varepsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,x_i^2),
\]
where the \(x_i\)'s are i.i.d. \(t(6)\).

Here is a simulated sample (\(n=200\)) of this model:

\begin{figure}

\includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/simulHeterosk-1} \hfill{}

\caption{Situation of heteroskedasticity. The model is $y_i = x_i + \varepsilon_i, \quad \varepsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,x_i^2)$, where the $x_i$'s are i.i.d. $t(6)$.}\label{fig:simulHeterosk}
\end{figure}

We simulate 500 independent samples of the same model with \(n=200\). For each sample, we compute the OLS estimate of \(\beta\) (\(=1\)). For each of the 500 OLS estimations, we employ (a) the standard OLS variance formula (\(s^2 (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\)) and (b) the White formula to estimate the variance of \(b\). We compare the average reported standard error with the empirical standard deviation of the 500 slope estimates.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Quantity & Value\\
\midrule
Empirical SD across samples & 0.151\\
Mean conventional SE & 0.070\\
Mean HC0 SE & 0.143\\
Mean HC1 SE & 0.143\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The White formula yields, on average, an estimated standard deviation much closer to the simulation benchmark than the conventional OLS formula, which underestimates the standard deviation of \(b\).
\end{example}

For the Paris rainfall regression in Example \ref{exm:rainOLS}, the following table compares conventional standard errors with HC0 and HC1 adjustments. The coefficient remains unchanged. HC adjustments allow unequal variances but do not account for dependence between successive days; the HAC calculation in Example \ref{exm:HCheterAC} addresses both.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Term & Estimate & Conventional SE & HC0 SE & HC1 SE\\
\midrule
Intercept & 17.292 & 0.129 & 0.130 & 0.130\\
Rainfall (per 10 mm) & 0.743 & 0.279 & 0.322 & 0.322\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

\begin{example}[Heteroskedasticity and Autocorrelation (HAC)]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:HCheterAC}{}\label{exm:HCheterAC}

\citet{Newey_West_1987} have proposed a formula to address both heteroskedasticity and auto-correlation of the residuals (Eqs. \eqref{eq:heteroskedasticity} and \eqref{eq:autocorrelation}). They show that, if the correlation between terms \(i\) and \(j\) gets sufficiently small when \(|i-j|\) increases:
\begin{eqnarray}
&&\mbox{plim} \left( \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}'_j \right) \approx  \\
&&\mbox{plim} \left( \frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}e_{t}^2\mathbf{x}_t\mathbf{x}'_t +
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{t=\ell+1}^{n}w_\ell e_{t}e_{t-\ell}(\mathbf{x}_t\mathbf{x}'_{t-\ell} + \mathbf{x}_{t-\ell}\mathbf{x}'_{t})
\right), \nonumber \label{eq:NW}
\end{eqnarray}
where \(w_\ell = 1 - \ell/(L+1)\). A fixed \(L\) gives a truncated approximation. Consistency under general weak serial dependence requires \(L\) to increase suitably slowly with sample size; Chapter \ref{TS} develops the long-run-variance argument.

Let us illustrate the influence of autocorrelation using simulations. We consider the following model:
\begin{equation}
y_i = x_i + \varepsilon_i,\label{eq:simul11}
\end{equation}
where the \(x_i\)'s and the \(\varepsilon_i\)'s are such that:
\begin{equation}
x_i = 0.8 x_{i-1} + u_i \quad and \quad \varepsilon_i = 0.8 \varepsilon_{i-1} + v_i, \label{eq:simul22}
\end{equation}
where the \(u_i\)'s and the \(v_i\)'s are mutually independent i.i.d. \(\mathcal{N}(0,1)\) innovations. Thus both \(x_i\) and \(\varepsilon_i\) are serially correlated, but they are independent of one another; this simulation is designed to illustrate autocorrelation rather than heteroskedasticity.

We simulate 500 samples of the same model with \(n=200\). For each sample, we compute the OLS estimate of \(\beta\) (=1). For each of the 500 OLS estimations, we employ (a) the standard OLS variance formula (\(s^2 (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\)), (b) the White formula, and (c) the Newey-West formula to estimate the variance of \(b\). For each formula, we compute the average of the 500 resulting standard deviations and compare these with the standard deviation of the 500 OLS estimate of \(\beta\).

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
Simulation SD & Mean OLS SE & Mean HC1 SE & Mean HAC SE\\
\midrule
0.149 & 0.072 & 0.071 & 0.121\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The empirical SD measures how much the slope varies across independent samples. Compare each reported SE with that benchmark. HC1 permits unequal error variances but ignores serial dependence; the HAC calculation also uses eight lags. In a sample of 200 observations, a HAC estimate need not reproduce the benchmark exactly.

Return to the Paris rainfall regression in Example \ref{exm:rainOLS}. For comparison, we also fit a specification with weekday, month and year indicators, allowing for systematic calendar differences in accident counts. Both specifications use the same consecutive days. We compare conventional, HC1 and Newey--West standard errors for the rainfall slope; the HAC calculation uses seven daily lags, Bartlett weights and no prewhitening.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Specification & Accidents per 10 mm & Conventional SE & HC1 SE & HAC SE\\
\midrule
Rainfall only & 0.743 & 0.279 & 0.322 & 0.350\\
Calendar controls & 0.834 & 0.216 & 0.244 & 0.267\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The fitted slopes do not depend on the covariance estimator. Choosing seven lags includes a week of serial covariances; it does not assert that all dependence beyond a week is zero. Longer lags provide a useful sensitivity check.

We can now complete the inference postponed in Example \ref{exm:rainOLS}. The table uses HAC standard errors and a large-sample standard-normal reference distribution. Normality of the individual errors is not required, but suitable weak-dependence conditions are. The final columns give 95\% intervals, \(b_k\pm1.96\,\widehat{\operatorname{se}}_{HAC}(b_k)\).

\begin{example}[Returning to Swiss hydropower: uncertainty and sensitivity]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:hydro-inference}{}\label{exm:hydro-inference}The observations in Example \ref{exm:IVhydro} are successive days. Water conditions and electricity-market shocks can persist, so conventional independent-error standard errors are inappropriate. We keep the same model and use a Newey--West covariance estimate with seven daily lags, allowing a week of serial dependence.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Specification & Days & Response (MW) & HAC SE (MW)\\
\midrule
Baseline & 3288 & 344.7 & 61.0\\
Rain and hydrology controls & 3278 & 357.1 & 60.6\\
Offshore wind only & 3288 & 383.0 & 74.1\\
Without 2022 & 2923 & 469.6 & 53.3\\
2016-2020 & 1827 & 717.4 & 105.7\\
\addlinespace
2021-2024 & 1461 & 264.4 & 55.0\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

All responses are in MW per 10 EUR/MWh. For the baseline, the approximate 95\% interval is {[}225, 464{]} MW. The first-stage wind coefficient has a squared HAC \(t\)-statistic of 30.4, giving evidence of relevance after the controls; this is a one-restriction robust Wald statistic, not a homoskedastic first-stage \(F\)-statistic.

The additional hydrological controls are Swiss run-of-river and nuclear generation, current rainfall and preceding-seven-day rainfall. Ten incomplete river observations are omitted in that row only. Offshore wind provides a geographically more distant instrument; removing 2022 checks whether the energy crisis alone drives the estimate. The final rows estimate the same specification in two periods.

The positive sign survives these checks, but its magnitude changes considerably between periods. The baseline's interval describes sampling uncertainty \textbf{within the specified model}; it does not cover uncertainty about exclusion or stability across regimes. In the row with incomplete river data, the seven HAC lags count successive observed days rather than exactly seven elapsed calendar days. The baseline is exactly identified, so an overidentification test is unavailable.
\end{example}

The preceding examples adjust inference for serial dependence. Residual diagnostics provide a complementary check on the covariance assumptions. The Durbin-Watson test, proposed by \citet{Durbin_Watson_1950} and \citet{Durbin_Watson_1951}, uses the statistic
\[
DW = \frac{\sum_{i=2}^{n}(e_i - e_{i-1})^2}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_i^2}= 2(1 - r) - \underbrace{\frac{e_1^2 + e_n^2}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_i^2}}_{\overset{p}{\rightarrow} 0},
\]
where \(r\) is the lag-one sample residual autocorrelation, with the full residual sum of squares in its denominator:
\[
r = \frac{\sum_{i=2}^{n}e_i e_{i-1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_i^2}.
\]
(\(r\) is a consistent estimator of \(\mathbb{C}or(\varepsilon_i,\varepsilon_{i-1})\), i.e.~\(\rho\) in Eq. \eqref{eq:usual2}.)

The one-sided test for \(H_0\): \(\rho=0\) against \(H_1\): \(\rho>0\) is carried out by comparing \(DW\) to values \(d_L(T, K)\) and \(d_U(T, K)\):
\[
\left\{
\begin{array}{ll}
\mbox{If $DW < d_L$,}&\mbox{ the null hypothesis is rejected;}\\
\mbox{if $DW > d_U$,}&\mbox{ the hypothesis is not rejected;}\\
\mbox{If $d_L \le DW \le d_U$,} &\mbox{ no conclusion is drawn.}
\end{array}
\right.
\]

\begin{example}[Serial correlation in monthly accident counts]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:DurbinWats}{}\label{exm:DurbinWats}Figure \ref{fig:accidents-residuals} displays the same Zurich model and the autocorrelation of its residuals. The calendar-month indicators absorb regular seasonality; they do not guarantee that the remaining errors are uncorrelated over time.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/accidents-residuals-1} 

}

\caption{Monthly injury accidents in Zurich, 2011–2025. Left: recorded counts and fitted values from the trend-plus-month-indicators model. Right: residual autocorrelations; the zero-lag correlation is omitted. The dashed bounds are the usual approximate white-noise reference bounds.}\label{fig:accidents-residuals}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{rr}
\toprule
DW & p.value\\
\midrule
1.4476 & <0.001\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The one-sided test rejects zero residual autocorrelation in favour of positive autocorrelation. This supports revisiting the conventional covariance assumption; it does not by itself select a lag length for HAC estimation or establish that the mean specification is complete.
\end{example}

\subsection{Cluster-robust covariance matrices}\label{Clusters}

HAC estimators exploit an ordering of observations. In many cross-sectional and panel applications, dependence is instead organized by groups. Cluster-robust inference allows arbitrary dependence within a cluster while assuming independence across clusters. A cluster may gather entities from the same geographical area, industry, or cohort. This section follows \citet{MACKINNON2022}; another useful reference is \citet{Cameron_Miller_2014}.

The OLS estimator satisfies:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{b} = \boldsymbol\beta + (\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\varepsilon.\label{eq:BBB}
\end{equation}
Consider a set \(\{n_1,n_2,\dots,n_G\}\) s.t. \(n=\sum_g n_g\), on which is based the following decomposition of \(\mathbf{X}\):
\[
\mathbf{X} = \left[
\begin{array}{c}
\mathbf{X}_1 \\
\mathbf{X}_2 \\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{X}_G
\end{array}
\right].
\]
With these notations, Eq. \eqref{eq:BBB} rewrites:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{b} - \boldsymbol\beta = \left(\sum_{g=1}^G \mathbf{X}_g'\mathbf{X}_g\right)^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^G \mathbf{s}_g,\label{eq:cluster1}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{s}_g = \mathbf{X}_g'\boldsymbol\varepsilon_g \label{eq:definiS}
\end{equation}
denotes the score vector (of dimension \(K \times 1\)) associated with the \(g^{th}\) cluster.

If the model is correctly specified then \(\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{s}_g)=0\) for all clusters \(g\). Note that Eq. \eqref{eq:cluster1} is valid for any partition of \(\{1,\dots,n\}\). Dividing the sample into clusters becomes meaningful if we further assume that the following hypothesis holds:

\begin{hypothesis}[Clusters]
\protect\hypertarget{hyp:cluster}{}\label{hyp:cluster}We have:
\[
(i)\; \mathbb{E}(\mathbf{s}_g\mathbf{s}_g')=\Sigma_g,\quad (ii)\; \mathbb{E}(\mathbf{s}_g\mathbf{s}_q')=0,\;g \ne q,
\]
where \(s_g\) is defined in Eq. \eqref{eq:definiS}.
\end{hypothesis}

The real assumption here is \((ii)\); the first one simply gives a notation for the covariance matrix of the score associated with the \(g^{th}\) cluster. Remark that these covariance matrices can differ across clusters. That is, \emph{cluster-based inference is robust against both heteroskedasticity and intra-cluster dependence without imposing any restrictions on the (unknown) form of either of them}.

Naturally, matrix \(\Sigma_g\) depends on the covariance structure of the \(\varepsilon\)'s. In particular, if \(\Omega_g = \mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon_g\boldsymbol\varepsilon_g'|\mathbf{X}_g)\), then we have \(\Sigma_g = \mathbb{E}(\mathbf{X}_g'\Omega_g\mathbf{X}_g)\).

Under Hypothesis \ref{hyp:cluster}, it comes that the conditional covariance matrix of \(\mathbf{b}\) is:
\begin{equation}
\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{g=1}^G \Sigma_g\right)\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\label{eq:cluster2}
\end{equation}

Let us denote by \(\varepsilon_{g,i}\) the error associated with the \(i^{th}\) component of vector \(\boldsymbol\varepsilon_g\). Consider the special case where \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{g,i} \varepsilon_{g,j}|\mathbf{X}_g)=\sigma^2\mathbb{I}_{\{i=j\}}\), then Eq. \eqref{eq:cluster2} gives the standard expression \(\sigma^2\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\) (see Eq. \eqref{eq:distriBcondi}).

If we have \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{gi} \varepsilon_{gj}|\mathbf{X}_g)=\sigma_{gi}^2\mathbb{I}_{\{i=j\}}\), then we fall in the case addressed by the White formula (see Example \ref{exm:HCheteroskedasticity}). That is, in this case, the conditional covariance matrix of \(\mathbf{b}\) is:
\[
\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{X}'\left[  \begin{array}{cccc}
\sigma_1^2 & 0 & \dots & 0 \\
0 & \sigma_2^2 &  & 0 \\
\vdots && \ddots& \vdots \\
0 & \dots & 0 & \sigma_n^2
\end{array} \right]\mathbf{X}\right)\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}.
\]
As in \citet{White_1980}, the natural way to approach the conditional covariance given in Eq. \eqref{eq:cluster2} consists in replacing the \(\Sigma_g\) matrices by their sample equivalent, i.e.~\(\widehat{\Sigma}_g=\mathbf{X}_g'\mathbf{e}_g\mathbf{e}_g'\mathbf{X}_g\). Adding corrections for the number of degrees of freedom, this leads to the following estimate of the covariance matrix of \(\mathbf{b}\):
\begin{equation}
\frac{G(n-1)}{(G-1)(n-K)}\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{g=1}^G\widehat{\Sigma}_g\right) \left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}. \label{eq:AsymptCL}
\end{equation}
The previous estimate is CRCV1 in \citet{MACKINNON2022}. Note that we indeed find the White-MacKinnon estimator (Eq. \eqref{eq:WhiteHC1}) when \(G=n\).

Remark that if there was only one cluster (\(G=1\)), and neglecting the degree-of-freedom correction, we would have:
\[
\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{X}\right) \left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1} = 0
\]
because \(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{e}=0\). Hence, having large clusters does not necessarily increase variance.

Often, when working with panel data (see Chapter \ref{Panel}), we want to cluster in different dimensions. A typical case is when the data are indexed by both individuals (\(i\)) and time (\(t\)). In that case, we may indeed suspect that: (a) the residuals are correlated across clusters of dates (e.g., with monthly data, a cluster may be one year) and (b) the residuals are correlated across clusters of individuals (e.g., with data at the county level a cluster may be a state). In this case, one can employ \textbf{two-way clustering}.

Formally, consider two distinct partitions of the data: one through index \(g\), with \(g \in \{1,\dots,G\}\), and the other through index \(h\), with \(h \in \{1,\dots,H\}\). Accordingly, we denote by \(\mathbf{X}_{g,h}\) the submatrix of \(\mathbf{X}\) that contains the explanatory variables corresponding to clusters \(g\) and \(h\) (e.g., the firms of a given country \(g\) at a given date \(h\)). We also denote by \(\mathbf{X}_{g,\bullet}\) (respectively \(\mathbf{X}_{\bullet,h}\)) the submatrix of \(\mathbf{X}\) containing all explanatory variables pertaining to cluster \(g\), for all possible values of \(h\) (resp. to cluster \(h\), for all possible values of \(g\)).

We will make the following assumption:

\begin{hypothesis}[Two-way clusters]
\protect\hypertarget{hyp:twowaycluster}{}\label{hyp:twowaycluster}We have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
&&\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{s}_{g,\bullet}\mathbf{s}_{g,\bullet}')=\Sigma_g,\quad \mathbb{E}(\mathbf{s}_{\bullet,h}\mathbf{s}_{\bullet,h}')=\Sigma^*_h,\quad \mathbb{E}(\mathbf{s}_{g,h}\mathbf{s}_{g,h}')=\Sigma_{g,h},\\ &&\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{s}_{g,h}\mathbf{s}_{q,k}')=0\;\mbox{if }g\neq q\mbox{ and }h \ne k.
\end{eqnarray*}
\end{hypothesis}

\begin{proposition}[Covariance of scores in the two-way-cluster setup]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:twoWayCov}{}\label{prp:twoWayCov}Under this assumption, the matrix of covariance of the scores is given by:
\[
\Sigma = \sum_{g=1}^G \Sigma_{g} + \sum_{h=1}^H \Sigma^*_{h} - \sum_{g=1}^G\sum_{h=1}^H \Sigma_{g,h}.
\]
(The last term on the right-hand side must be subtracted in order to avoid double counting.)
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Scores have mean zero under exogeneity. In the covariance of \(\sum_{g,h}\mathbf{s}_{g,h}\), pairs of cells sharing neither cluster have zero cross-covariance. Summing the remaining pairs gives
\[
\Sigma=\sum_g\mathbb E(\mathbf{s}_{g,\bullet}\mathbf{s}_{g,\bullet}')+
\sum_h\mathbb E(\mathbf{s}_{\bullet,h}\mathbf{s}_{\bullet,h}')-
\sum_{g,h}\mathbb E(\mathbf{s}_{g,h}\mathbf{s}_{g,h}').
\]
The subtraction removes cell contributions counted in both cluster dimensions, yielding the stated formula.
\end{proof}

The asymptotic theory can be based on two different approaches: (i) large number of clusters (common case), and (ii) fixed number of clusters but large number of observations in each cluster (see Subsections 4.1 and 4.2 in \citet{MACKINNON2022}). The more variable the \(N_g\)'s (clusters' sizes are heterogeneous in terms of size), the less reliable asymptotic inference based on Eq. \eqref{eq:AsymptCL}, especially when a very few clusters are unusually large, or when the distribution of the data is heavy-tailed. These issues are somehow mitigated when the clusters have an approximate factor structure.

In practice, \(\Sigma\) is estimated by:
\[
\widehat{\Sigma} = \sum_{g=1}^G \widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{g,\bullet}\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{g,\bullet}' + \sum_{h=1}^H \widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\bullet,h}\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{\bullet,h}' - \sum_{g=1}^G\sum_{h=1}^H \widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{g,h}\widehat{\mathbf{s}}_{g,h}',
\]
and we use:
\[
\widehat{\mathbb{V}ar}(\mathbf{b}) = \left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}\widehat{\Sigma}\left(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}\right)^{-1}.
\]

As an alternative to an asymptotic approximation, one can use the bootstrap methods discussed in Section 5 of \citet{MACKINNON2022}. In R, the package \texttt{fwildclusterboot} implements such procedures.\footnote{See, e.g., \href{https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fwildclusterboot/vignettes/fwildclusterboot.html}{this tutorial by Alexander Fischer}.}

\begin{example}[Turnout and two-way clustering]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:votes-cluster-example}{}\label{exm:votes-cluster-example}Swissvotes (Appendix \ref{data-votes}) provides a setting with two clustering dimensions. \textbf{One row is one federal voting object in one canton.} Several objects may share a voting day, and every canton appears repeatedly. We relate turnout (in percent of eligible voters) to the number of proposals on the day, with canton indicators, procedure indicators and a linear time trend.

Objects submitted on the same day can share turnout shocks; observations in the same canton can share persistent local influences. Canton indicators allow different mean turnout levels but do not eliminate dependence within cantons. The proposal count varies by voting day, not independently across all rows. Its source definition groups an initiative with its counterproposal and tie-break question as one proposal.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Covariance estimator & Proposal-count coefficient & SE\\
\midrule
Conventional & 0.476 & 0.053\\
HC1 & 0.476 & 0.051\\
Voting-day clusters & 0.476 & 0.322\\
Canton and day clusters & 0.476 & 0.318\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

All four rows use the same 11807 observations and the same coefficient: only the dependence assumption changes. There are 158 voting-day clusters and 26 cantons. The two-way calculation allows dependence among observations sharing either dimension. The limited number of cantons makes the cluster-count qualifications above relevant. The lesson is that changing the dependence assumption changes uncertainty, not the fitted relationship or its causal interpretation.
\end{example}

\section{Shrinkage methods}\label{shrinkage}

The chapter has so far treated the set of regressors as given. When many covariates are potentially relevant, selecting a specification becomes part of the estimation problem. Keeping all variables can make estimates imprecise (Section \ref{irrelevant}); shrinkage methods trade some bias for lower variance and, in the lasso case, variable selection. The two best-known approaches are \textbf{ridge regression} and the \textbf{lasso}.\footnote{See \citet{Tibshirani_2011} for a review of the lasso approach. See also Section 6.2 of \citet{James2013}.} Both modify the OLS minimization problem (Section \ref{LSquares}):
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{b} = \underset{\boldsymbol\beta}{\mbox{argmin}}\; \sum_{i=1}^n(y_i - \mathbf{x}_i'\boldsymbol\beta)^2
\end{equation}
is replaced with the following:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{b}_\lambda = \underset{\boldsymbol\beta}{\mbox{argmin}}\; \sum_{i=1}^n(y_i - \mathbf{x}_i'\boldsymbol\beta)^2 + \lambda f(\boldsymbol\beta),\label{eq:minLasso}
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda f(\boldsymbol\beta)\) is a penalty term that positively depends on the ``size'' of the components of \(\boldsymbol\beta\). This term is called the \emph{shrinkage penalty} term.

Specifically, assuming that the vector \(\mathbf{x}_i\) of potential covariates is of dimension \(K \times 1\), we have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
f(\boldsymbol\beta) & = & \sum_{j=1}^K \beta_j^2 \quad \mbox{in the ridge case ($\ell_2$ norm)},\\
f(\boldsymbol\beta) & = & \sum_{j=1}^K |\beta_j| \quad \mbox{in the lasso case ($\ell_1$ norm)}.
\end{eqnarray*}

In most cases, we do not want to involve the intercept in the set of parameters to shrink, and the preceding equations are respectively replaced with:
\begin{eqnarray*}
f(\boldsymbol\beta) & = & \sum_{j=2}^K \beta_j^2 \quad \mbox{(ridge)},\\
f(\boldsymbol\beta) & = & \sum_{j=2}^K |\beta_j| \quad \mbox{(lasso)}.
\end{eqnarray*}

The \(\ell_1\) and \(\ell_2\) penalties affect estimates differently as the tuning parameter \(\lambda\) grows. Ridge coefficients move continuously towards zero. Under the lasso penalty, some coefficients become exactly zero. Ridge therefore performs shrinkage, whereas the lasso performs both shrinkage and variable selection.

Parameter \(\lambda\) has to be determined separately from the minimization problem of Eq. \eqref{eq:minLasso}. One can combine standard criteria (e.g., BIC or Akaike) for this purpose.

\begin{example}[Predicting credit defaults with many regressors]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:credit-shrinkage}{}\label{exm:credit-shrinkage}Can a flexible regression help predict which clients carry the next-month default indicator? We return to \texttt{AEC::UCIcredit} (Appendix \ref{data-credit}), with \textbf{one row per client} and a binary outcome: one for a recorded default and zero otherwise. All predictors come from the client characteristics and April--September histories observed before that outcome.

We use age, the credit limit, six statement balances and six payment amounts, together with indicators for sex, education, marital status and the six repayment-status variables. Monetary predictors are transformed to reduce their skewness, preserving negative statement balances. Starting from these 14 numeric predictors, we compare three systematic specifications: a quadratic expansion, that expansion augmented with interactions with commonly observed repayment-status categories, and \textbf{all numeric polynomial terms up to degree three}. The cubic terms include cubes, squared-by-linear products and products of three different predictors. They allow curvature and interactions, rather than adding new information about the clients.

Here ridge and lasso minimise the squared-error criterion introduced above, even though the outcome is binary. They produce \textbf{linear default scores}. Chapter \ref{binary-choice-models} introduces logit and probit models that constrain fitted probabilities to lie between zero and one.

We use \textbf{21,000 training clients and 9,000 evaluation clients}. Five-fold cross-validation uses only the training clients, with identical folds for all methods. The following training-only comparison records all three specifications considered; a lower mean squared error is better.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Specification & Predictors & OLS CV MSE & Ridge CV MSE & Lasso CV MSE\\
\midrule
Quadratic & 186 & 0.1370 & 0.1356 & 0.1354\\
Quadratic + status interactions & 480 & 0.1395 & 0.1360 & 0.1354\\
Cubic & 746 & 0.1467 & 0.1359 & 0.1352\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The cubic specification has the lowest lasso validation error, but its advantage over the quadratic specification is small. Its main lesson is the cost of fitting a rich model without regularisation: cubic OLS has substantially higher validation error. We use the \textbf{746-predictor cubic design} for the common OLS/ridge/lasso comparison and retain the smaller quadratic OLS model as a benchmark. Thus we do not judge shrinkage solely against an unnecessarily flexible competitor.

Constant or exactly redundant columns are removed using training predictors only. Standardisation is fitted within each training fold. Neither the richer specification nor its penalties is chosen using the 9,000 evaluation outcomes.

Figure \ref{fig:credit-shrink-paths} shows how increasing the penalty shrinks coefficients. For lasso, some coefficients become exactly zero.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/credit-shrink-paths-1} 

}

\caption{Coefficient paths for credit-default prediction. Coefficients are expressed per training-sample standard deviation of each predictor for display. Dotted vertical lines mark the minimum-error cross-validation choices. The unpenalised intercept is omitted.}\label{fig:credit-shrink-paths}
\end{figure}

We choose the penalty separately for ridge and lasso by minimising the mean validation squared error in Figure \ref{fig:credit-shrink-cv}. The error bars show one estimated standard error across the validation folds. A larger penalty can reduce estimation noise, but excessive shrinkage loses useful predictive information.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/credit-shrink-cv-1} 

}

\caption{Five-fold cross-validation on the 21,000 training clients. Orange dashed lines mark the minimum-error penalties; grey dotted lines mark the largest penalties within one estimated standard error of the minimum. The 9,000 held-out clients do not enter these curves.}\label{fig:credit-shrink-cv}
\end{figure}

Finally, we evaluate the selected models on the reserved clients. Cubic OLS, ridge and lasso use \textbf{the same predictors}. The table also retains the default-share benchmark and the smaller quadratic OLS specification. The primary comparison uses the original squared-error criterion; the next column reports a common diagnostic that clips each score to \([0,1]\) before evaluation.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Method & Held-out MSE & MSE after clipping & Nonzero slopes\\
\midrule
Training default share & 0.1718 & 0.1718 & 0\\
Quadratic OLS & 0.1358 & 0.1356 & 186\\
Cubic OLS & 0.1418 & 0.1379 & 746\\
Cubic ridge & 0.1361 & 0.1359 & 746\\
Cubic lasso & 0.1359 & 0.1358 & 98\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

Lasso retains \textbf{98 of the 746 cubic-design slopes}. Its primary held-out mean squared error is \textbf{4.2\% lower than cubic OLS}. The improvement is smaller after clipping, because part of the gain comes from avoiding extreme linear scores: with clipping the reduction is \textbf{1.5\%}. No penalties or features are retuned on these evaluation results.

The smaller quadratic OLS model remains competitive and slightly outperforms the penalised cubic models on these clients. The conclusion is therefore specific: \textbf{shrinkage protects against the cost of fitting a rich specification; it does not establish that greater complexity improves prediction}. The score comparison concerns other clients from the same collection, rather than outcomes in future calendar months. Chapter \ref{binary-choice-models} returns to the data with models designed for binary probabilities.
\end{example}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\section{Chapter recap}\label{regressions-recap}

\begin{recapbox}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Section \ref{linearHyp} states the assumptions that determine the finite- and large-sample properties of regression estimators.
\item
  Section \ref{LSquares} derives OLS, and Theorem \ref{thm:GaussMarkov} identifies its efficiency among linear unbiased estimators under the Gauss-Markov assumptions.
\item
  Proposition \ref{prp:Ftest} shows how jointly imposed linear restrictions can be tested by comparing restricted and unrestricted fit.
\item
  Section \ref{CommonPitfalls} separates multicollinearity, omitted-variable bias, and the loss of precision caused by irrelevant regressors.
\item
  Section \ref{regression-prediction-intervals} distinguishes uncertainty about the conditional mean from the additional dispersion of a new outcome.
\item
  Definition \ref{def:instruments} states the relevance and exogeneity requirements for instrumental variables.
\item
  Definition \ref{def:GRM} allows heteroskedastic and correlated disturbances; Sections \ref{HAC} and \ref{Clusters} develop corresponding covariance estimators.
\item
  Section \ref{shrinkage} explains how ridge and lasso estimation trade fit against coefficient magnitude when many covariates are available.
\end{itemize}

\end{recapbox}

\clearpage

\section{Exercises}\label{regressions-exercises}

Attempt each question and explain your reasoning, including for true-or-false statements. \textbf{Foundation} exercises apply a result directly; \textbf{Standard} exercises require several steps or a short proof; \textbf{Advanced} exercises combine several results or require a longer derivation. Worked solutions and exercise-specific hints are reserved for teaching sessions and are not included in this student edition.

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[OLS inference in a three-regressor model]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-ols-inference}{}\label{exr:regressions-ols-inference}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{LSquares}.

Consider the following linear regression: \[y_i = \beta_1 x_{i,1} + \beta_2 x_{i,2} + \beta_3 x_{i,3} + \varepsilon_i,\] where \(x_{i,1} = 1\) for all \(i\). We observe \((y_i, x_{i,2}, x_{i,3})\), for \(i \in \{1, \dots, n\}\), with \(n = 200\).

In matrix notation, the linear regression writes: \[\mathbf{y} = \boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}.\] The OLS estimator of \(\boldsymbol{\beta} = [\beta_1, \beta_2, \beta_3]'\) is denoted by \(\mathbf{b}\).\\
We suppose that the standard full-rank, conditional-mean-zero, homoskedasticity, and no-cross-correlation assumptions hold. We additionally assume normally distributed errors.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  The OLS estimates of the disturbances \(\varepsilon_i\) are denoted by \(e_i\). Show that \(\bar{e}_n = 0\).
\item
  Show that if \(\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} | \boldsymbol{X}) = 0\), the OLS estimator of \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) is unbiased.
\item
  The correlation between \(x_{i,2}\) and \(x_{i,3}\) is 0.98. What does it imply for the \(\mathbf{b}\) estimator? (No computation is required here, just a brief explanation.)\\
  In the following, we assume that \(\beta_3 = 0\) and that the Gauss--Markov assumptions are satisfied. In the formulas below, \(\boldsymbol{X}\) denotes the \(n\times2\) design matrix of the reduced model \(y_i = \beta_1 + \beta_2 x_{i,2} + \varepsilon_i\). Its OLS estimation gives \(b_2 = 1.2\), a residual sum of squares of 181, and \[(\boldsymbol{X}'\boldsymbol{X})^{-1} =
  \begin{bmatrix}
  1.1 & 0.4 \\
  0.4 & 0.25
  \end{bmatrix}.\]
\item
  Give an estimate of \(\mathbb{V}ar(b_2|\boldsymbol{X})\).
\item
  Compute an approximate 95\% confidence interval for \(\beta_2\). You will also briefly explain why you can compute such an interval in the present case.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[The fitted hyperplane and sample averages]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-fitted-hyperplane}{}\label{exr:regressions-fitted-hyperplane}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Proof}\\
