Review Section \ref{LSquares}.

Consider the linear model: \[y_i = \beta_1 x_{i,1} + \beta_2 x_{i,2} + \cdots + \beta_K x_{i,K} + \varepsilon_i = \mathbf{x}_i'\boldsymbol{\beta} + \varepsilon_i, \quad i = 1, \dots, n.\] We assume that Assumptions 4.1 (full rank), 4.2 (conditional mean zero), 4.3 (homoskedasticity), 4.4 (non-correlated residuals) of the course hold. We further assume that \(x_{i,1} = 1\) for all \(i\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Show that the hyperplane of the regression passes through the average of the data, i.e., \[\bar{y} = \bar{\mathbf{x}}'\mathbf{b},\] where \[\bar{y} = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n y_i, \quad \bar{\mathbf{x}} = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{x}_i,\] and where \(\mathbf{b}\) is the OLS estimator of \(\boldsymbol{\beta} = [\beta_1, \dots, \beta_K]'\).
\item
  Show that the mean of the fitted values (\(= \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}\)) is equal to the mean of the actual values, i.e., \[\bar{y} = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \hat{y}_i,\] where \(\hat{y}_i = \mathbf{b}'\mathbf{x}_i\).
\item
  The \(R^2\) coefficient is defined by \[R^2 = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n (\hat{y}_i - \bar{y})^2}{\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \bar{y})^2}.\] Show that \(\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \bar{y})^2 = \sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2 + \sum_{i=1}^n (\hat{y}_i - \bar{y})^2\), and deduce that \(R^2\) is between 0 and 1.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Unbiased estimation of the error variance]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-variance-estimator}{}\label{exr:regressions-variance-estimator}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Proof}\\
Review Section \ref{LSquares}.

Consider the linear model: \[y_i = \beta_1 x_{i,1} + \beta_2 x_{i,2} + \cdots + \beta_K x_{i,K} + \varepsilon_i = \mathbf{x}_i'\boldsymbol{\beta} + \varepsilon_i, \quad i = 1, \dots, n.\] We assume that Assumptions 4.1 (full rank), 4.2 (conditional mean zero), 4.3 (homoskedasticity), and 4.4 (non-correlated residuals) hold.

Let \(\mathbf{b}\) be the estimator of \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) and let \(s^2\) be the OLS estimator of \(\sigma^2\). The estimator \(s^2\) is defined by: \[s^2 = \frac{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}}{n - K}, \quad \text{where } \mathbf{e} = \mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}.\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Write the model above in matrix form: \[\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\] clarifying how \(\mathbf{y}\), \(\mathbf{X}\), \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\), and \(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\) are defined and their dimensions.
\item
  Show that the estimated residuals are orthogonal to the explanatory variables.
\item
  Show that if the model contains an intercept, the estimated residuals have mean zero.
\item
  Show that if the columns of \(\mathbf{X}\) are orthogonal, the estimator of \(\beta_j\) in the model above coincides with that of \(\beta_j\) in \(y_i = \beta_j x_{i,j} + u_i, \quad i = 1, \dots, n\).
\item
  Show that \(s^2\) is conditionally unbiased for \(\sigma^2\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[OLS assumptions, confidence intervals, and tests]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-ols-properties}{}\label{exr:regressions-ols-properties}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{LSquares}.

\emph{Verbeek (2004), exercise 2.1 (slightly modified)}

Consider the linear model \[y_i = \beta_1 + \beta_2 x_{i,2} + \beta_3 x_{i,3} + \varepsilon_i = \mathbf{x}_i' \boldsymbol{\beta} + \varepsilon_i, \quad i = 1, \dots, n,\]

Let \(\mathbf{b}\) be the estimator of \(\boldsymbol{\beta} = [\beta_1, \beta_2, \beta_3]'\) and let \(s^2\) be the OLS estimator of \(\sigma^2\). Assume that \(n\) is large.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Express \(\mathbf{b}\).
\item
  Which assumptions are needed to make \(\mathbf{b}\) an unbiased estimator for \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\)?
\item
  Explain how a confidence interval for \(\beta_2\) can be constructed. Which additional assumptions are needed?
\item
  Explain how one can test the hypothesis that \(\beta_3 = 1\).
\item
  Explain how one can test the joint hypothesis that \(\beta_2 + \beta_3 = 0\) and \(\beta_1 = 2\).
\item
  Explain how one can test the hypothesis that \(\beta_2 = \beta_3 = 0\).
\item
  Suppose that \(\lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{1}{n} \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X} = \mathbf{Q}\) and that all \((y_i, x_{i,2}, x_{i,3})\) are independent across \(i\). Which assumptions are needed to make \(\mathbf{b}\) a consistent estimator for \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\)?
\item
  Suppose that \(x_{i,2} = 2 + 3x_{i,3}\). What will happen if you try to estimate the linear model above?
\item
  Suppose that the model is estimated with \(x_{i,2}^* = 2x_{i,2} - 2\) included rather than \(x_{i,2}\). How are the coefficients in this model related to those in the original model above? And the \(R^2\)s?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Gauss-Markov results: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-gauss-markov-true-false}{}\label{exr:regressions-gauss-markov-true-false}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{CommonPitfalls}.

\emph{Verbeek (2004), exercise 2.4 (slightly modified)}

Carefully read the following statements. Are they True or False? Explain.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Under Gauss-Markov conditions, OLS can be shown to be BLUE. The phrase linear in this acronym refers to the fact that we are estimating a linear model.
\item
  The Gauss--Markov conditions alone are sufficient for the usual exact \emph{t}-test in small samples.
\item
  A regression of the OLS residual upon the regressors included in the model by construction yields an \(R^2\) of zero. (We suppose the regression includes a constant.)
\item
  The hypothesis that the OLS estimator is equal to zero can be tested by means of a \emph{t}-test.
\item
  If the absolute \emph{t}-value of a coefficient is smaller than 1.96, we reject the null hypothesis that the coefficient is zero, with 95\% confidence.
\item
  Because OLS provides the \emph{best} linear approximation of a variable \(y\) from a set of regressors, OLS also gives the \emph{best} linear unbiased estimators for the coefficients of the regressors.
\item
  If a variable in a model is significant at the 10\% level, it is also significant at the 5\% level.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[OLS matrices and residuals: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-ols-matrix-true-false}{}\label{exr:regressions-ols-matrix-true-false}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{LSquares}.

We assume that the Gauss--Markov assumptions (4.1 to 4.4 from the course) are satisfied in the model \[y_i=\mathbf{x}_i'\boldsymbol\beta+\varepsilon_i,
\qquad
\mathbb{V}ar(\varepsilon_i|\mathbf{X})=\sigma^2.\] The model includes a constant (the first component \(x_{i,1}=1\) for all \(i\)). The dimension of \(\mathbf{x}_i\) is \(K\times 1\), with \(K>1\).

Consider the OLS regression of \(y_i\) on \(\mathbf{x}_i\). Let \(\mathbf{b}\) denote the OLS estimator of \(\boldsymbol\beta\) and let \(\mathbf{e}\) denote the residual vector, \(\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}\), where \(\mathbf{X}\) is the \(n\times K\) matrix whose \(i\)th row is \(\mathbf{x}_i'\), and \(\mathbf{y}=[y_1,\dots,y_n]'\).

We assume that \(n\) is large, that \(\{\mathbf{x}_i,\varepsilon_i\}\) are i.i.d.~across individuals \(i\), and that \(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X}/n\) converges to an invertible matrix \(Q\).

We set \(s^2=\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}/(n-K)\) and denote by \(v_k\) the \(k\)th diagonal element of \((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1}\).

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \(s^2=\sigma^2\).
\item
  The sum of the OLS residuals \(e_i\) is equal to zero.
\item
  The distribution of the OLS estimator is approximately Gaussian only if the errors \(\varepsilon_i\) are Gaussian.
\item
  For \(k=1,\dots,K\), the distribution of \(b_k-\beta_k\) is approximately \(\mathcal{N}(0,s^2 v_k)\).
\item
  The coefficient of determination (\(R^2\)) is strictly larger than 0.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Endogeneity and instruments: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-instrumental-variables-true-false}{}\label{exr:regressions-instrumental-variables-true-false}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{IV}.

Consider four random variables \(v_i\), \(w_i\), \(z_i\), and \(\varepsilon_i\). Each of them is Gaussian with mean zero.

An additional random variable \(y_i\) is defined by \[y_i=\beta_1 v_i+\beta_2 w_i+\varepsilon_i.\]

The error term \(\varepsilon_i\) is uncorrelated with \(v_i\) and \(z_i\), but it is correlated with \(w_i\). Moreover, \(w_i\) is correlated with \(z_i\) but not with \(v_i\). This implies in particular that \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i|v_i)=0\), \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i|v_i,w_i)\neq 0\), \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i|w_i)\neq 0\), \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i|v_i,z_i)=0\), \(\mathbb{E}(w_i|v_i)=0\), and \(\mathbb{E}(v_i|w_i)=0\).

None of the four random variables can be obtained as an exact linear combination of the other three. Finally, the variables \((v_i,w_i,z_i,\varepsilon_i)\) are independent across entities \(i\).

We denote by \(\hat{w}_i\) the fitted value of \(w_i\) in the first-stage OLS regression of \(w_i\) on both exogenous variables, \(v_i\) and \(z_i\).

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  In the OLS regression of \(y_i\) on \((v_i,w_i)\), the OLS estimator of \(\boldsymbol\beta=(\beta_1,\beta_2)'\) is unbiased.
\item
  \(z_i\) is a valid instrument for \(w_i\).
\item
  In the OLS regression of \(y_i\) on \(v_i\) (alone), the OLS estimator of \(\beta_1\) is unbiased.
\item
  In the OLS regression of \(y_i\) on \(w_i\) (alone), the OLS estimator of \(\beta_2\) is unbiased.
\item
  Regressing \(y_i\) on \((v_i,\hat{w}_i)\) by OLS provides a consistent estimate of \((\beta_1,\beta_2)'\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Measurement error in income]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-measurement-error}{}\label{exr:regressions-measurement-error}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Application}\\
Review Section \ref{CommonPitfalls}.

We denote by \(c_i\) the logarithm of consumption by household \(i\) and by \(y_i^*\) the permanent income of the same household.\\
Consider the consumption function \[c_i = \beta y_i^* + \varepsilon_i, \quad \varepsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2),\] where \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i | y_i^*) = 0.\)\\
Permanent income \(y_i^*\) is not observed but we have data on \(y_i\), the current income of household \(i\). Assume that \(y_i = y_i^* + v_i\), where \(v_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, w^2)\), \(\mathbb{E}(v_i | y_i^*) = 0\) and \(\mathbb{E}(v_i | \varepsilon_i) = 0\).\\
We have a sample of size \(n\) and use the notation: \(\mathbf{y} = [y_1, \dots, y_n]' \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{u} = [u_1, \dots, u_n]'\). We assume further that the \((y_i^*, \varepsilon_i, v_i)\) are independent (across \(i\)).\\
Now consider the linear regression of \(c_i\) on \(y_i\): \(c_i = \beta y_i + u_i\). The residuals of this regression are denoted by \(u_i\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Show that \(u_i = \varepsilon_i - \beta v_i\).
\item
  Compute the covariance between \(y_i\) and \(u_i\).
\item
  We denote by \(\mathbf{b}\) the OLS estimator of \(\beta\) in the regression of \(c_i\) on \(y_i\) (with no constant term). After expressing \(\mathbf{b}\) as a function of \(\beta\), \(\mathbf{y}\) and \(\mathbf{u}\), give the limit of \(\mathbf{b}\) when \(n \to \infty\) (the formula involves, in particular, \(\mathbb{E}(y_i)\) and \(\mathbb{V}ar(y_i)\)).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Consumer sentiment and stock returns]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-sentiment-returns}{}\label{exr:regressions-sentiment-returns}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Application}\\
Review Section \ref{CommonPitfalls}.

We want to study the influence of consumer sentiment on stock returns (\(y_i\)). Some institutions construct (aggregated) consumer sentiment indices. These indices are usually based on surveys. We denote by \(\Delta s_i\) the change in the value of such an index between date \(i - 1\) and date \(i\).

We have a sample of \(n\) dates and we assume that the observations \((y_i, \Delta s_i)\) are independent across time (in other words they are "serially independent").

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Consider the model: \[        y_i = \beta \Delta s_i + \varepsilon_i,\] with \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i) = 0\).

  We also assume that: \[\varepsilon_i = \eta_i + \nu_i\] \[\Delta s_i = \delta \eta_i + \zeta_i\] where \(\eta_i\), \(\nu_i\) and \(\zeta_i\) are zero-mean variables that are mutually and serially independent and with respective variances \(\sigma_\eta^2\), \(\sigma_\nu^2\) and \(\sigma_\zeta^2\).

  Provide potential interpretations of \(\eta_i\) and compute \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i \Delta s_i)\).
\item
  What can be said about the OLS estimate of \(\beta\) (the model above)?
\item
  Let \(z_i\) be the value of a weather index at date \(i\). Interpret the condition \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i\mid z_i)=0\). What additional condition must \(z_i\) satisfy to be a valid instrument? Assuming validity, name the method that can be used to estimate \(\beta\).
\item
  Let \(\mathbf Z\) be the \(n\)-dimensional vector with \(i\)th element \(z_i\). Regress \(\Delta s_i\) on \(z_i\) by OLS and express the fitted vector \(\widehat{\Delta\mathbf s}\) as a function of \(\mathbf Z\).
\item
  Show that the OLS regression of \(y_i\) on the fitted values \(\widehat{\Delta s_i}\) provides a consistent estimate of \(\beta\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[A regression with MA(1) errors]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-serial-correlation}{}\label{exr:regressions-serial-correlation}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{ChapterLS}.

Consider the model:

\[y_i = \beta_1 x_{i,1} + \cdots + \beta_K x_{i,K} + \varepsilon_i,\]

where \(\varepsilon_i = u_i + \delta u_{i-1}\).

There is no collinear relationship among the explanatory variables \(x_{i,k}\). The \(u_i\) are i.i.d. \(\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)\). These disturbances \(u_i\) are independent from the \(\mathbf{x}_j = [x_{j,1},\dots,x_{j,K}]'\).

We denote by \(\mathbf{X}\) the matrix whose \((i,k)\) entry is \(x_{i,k}\) and by \(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\) the vector \([\varepsilon_1,\dots,\varepsilon_n]'\). The sample size is denoted by \(n\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Compute \(\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\) and \(\mathbb{C}ov(\varepsilon_i, \varepsilon_j)\). Consider three cases: (1) \(i = j\), (2) \(i = j+1\) (or \(i = j-1\)), and (3) \(i > j+1\) (or \(i < j-1\)). Express \(\mathbf{\Sigma} = \mathbb{C}ov(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\) as a function of \(\sigma^2\) and \(\delta\).
\item
  Compute \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf{b} | \mathbf{X})\) where \(\mathbf{b}\) is the OLS estimate of \(\boldsymbol{\beta} = [\beta_1, \dots, \beta_K]'\).
\item
  If we knew \(\mathbf{\Sigma}\), what would be the best linear unbiased estimator of \(\boldsymbol\beta\)?
\item
  Assuming that \(\mathbf{X'X}/n\) and \(\mathbf{X'\Sigma X}/n\) converge to positive definite matrices (when \(n\) goes to infinity), explain how to obtain a consistent estimate of \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Projection matrices and OLS residuals: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-projection-matrix-true-false}{}\label{exr:regressions-projection-matrix-true-false}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{LSquares}.

Consider the multiple linear regression model in matrix form: \[\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta} + \boldsymbol\varepsilon.\] \(\mathbf{y}\) is a \(n \times 1\) vector (dependent variable), \(\mathbf{X}\) is a \(n \times K\) matrix (the first column is a constant and the remaining \(K-1\) columns are explanatory variables), \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) is a \(K \times 1\) vector (coefficients), and \(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\) is a \(n \times 1\) vector (error term). We denote \(\mathbf{b}\) as the OLS estimator of \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\). The residuals are \[\mathbf{e} = \mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}.\] We denote \[\mathbf{P} = \mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X'X})^{-1}\mathbf{X'}\] as the projection matrix (which exists).

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  For \(\mathbf{b}\) to be unbiased we do not need homoskedasticity in the errors.
\item
  The OLS estimator is necessarily biased if the error term does not follow a normal distribution.
\item
  \(\mathbf{PPX} = \mathbf{PX}\).
\item
  The fact that \(\mathbf{X}\) is orthogonal to \(\mathbf{e}\) \((\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{e} = \mathbf{0})\) suggests that our OLS estimator is unbiased.
\item
  \(\boldsymbol\varepsilon = \mathbf{My}\), where \(\mathbf{M} = \mathbf{Id} - \mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X'X})^{-1}\mathbf{X'}\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[The covariance estimator changes inference]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-robust-inference}{}\label{exr:regressions-robust-inference}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Application}\\
Review Section \ref{Clusters}.

An OLS regression gives \(b_2=0.80\). Three estimated standard errors are available:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Covariance estimator & Standard error of \(b_2\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Homoskedastic & 0.25 \\
Heteroskedasticity-robust & 0.40 \\
Cluster-robust & 0.55 \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Explain why the coefficient estimate is the same in all three rows.
\item
  For each standard error, test \(H_0:\beta_2=0\) against \(H_1:\beta_2\ne0\) at the 5\% significance level using the large-sample critical value 1.96.
\item
  Which covariance estimator should be preferred if observations may be dependent within firms but firms are independent of one another? State the additional sampling requirement needed for its large-sample justification.
\item
  Explain why choosing a covariance estimator after comparing which one gives significance is invalid practice.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Ridge shrinkage in an orthogonal design]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:regressions-ridge-and-lasso}{}\label{exr:regressions-ridge-and-lasso}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{shrinkage}.

Consider the centered linear model \(\mathbf y=\mathbf X\boldsymbol\beta+\boldsymbol\varepsilon\), where \(\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\mid\mathbf X)=\mathbf0\), \(\mathbb{V}ar(\boldsymbol\varepsilon\mid\mathbf X)=\sigma^2\mathbf{Id}\), and \(\mathbf X'\mathbf X=n\mathbf{Id}_K\). The ridge estimator minimizes
\[
(\mathbf y-\mathbf X\mathbf b)'(\mathbf y-\mathbf X\mathbf b)+\lambda\mathbf b'\mathbf b,
\qquad \lambda\ge0.
\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Derive the ridge estimator and show that \(\mathbf b_R=\frac{n}{n+\lambda}\mathbf b_{OLS}\).
\item
  Compute \(\mathbb{E}(\mathbf b_R\mid\mathbf X)\) and its conditional bias.
\item
  Compute \(\mathbb{V}ar(\mathbf b_R\mid\mathbf X)\) and compare it with the conditional variance of OLS.
\item
  Explain the bias--variance trade-off created by increasing \(\lambda\).
\item
  Ridge generally does not set coefficients exactly to zero. Which feature of the lasso penalty can produce exact zeros?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\chapter{Panel regressions}\label{Panel}

\textbf{Prerequisites.} Linear regression, omitted-variable bias, generalized least squares, instrumental variables, and hypothesis testing.

\textbf{After working through this chapter, you should be able to:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Recognize the structure of a panel dataset and distinguish pooled, fixed-effects, and random-effects models.
\item
  Derive and interpret the within estimator.
\item
  Explain when random-effects estimation is consistent and how it differs from fixed effects.
\item
  Identify the source of bias in dynamic panel regressions and the role of internal instruments.
\item
  State the identifying assumption behind difference-in-differences.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Roadmap.} The chapter introduces panel notation and the main assumptions before presenting fixed-effects and random-effects estimators. It then studies dynamic panels and closes with programme-evaluation methods. The running application follows Swiss electricity meters: we compare weekend and weekday imports for the same installation. Simulations isolate the assumptions behind random effects and dynamic panels; a French speed-camera application illustrates difference-in-differences with later-treated comparison sites. Section \ref{panel-exercises} applies these ideas.

\section{Specification and notation}\label{specification-and-notation}

A panel dataset follows the same entities across time. Entities are indexed by \(i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\) and periods by \(t\in\{1,\dots,T\}\). This repeated-observation structure lets us distinguish variation across entities from variation within an entity over time.

The linear panel regression model is:
\begin{equation}
y_{i,t} = \mathbf{x}'_{i,t}\underbrace{\boldsymbol\beta}_{K \times 1} + \underbrace{\mathbf{z}'_{i}\boldsymbol\alpha}_{\mbox{Individual effects}} + \varepsilon_{i,t}.\label{eq:panel1}
\end{equation}

When running panel regressions, the usual objective is to estimate \(\boldsymbol\beta\).

Figure \ref{fig:simulPanel} illustrates why that distinction matters. Each entity is observed twice. In the simulation, \(y_{i,t}=\alpha_i+x_{i,t}+\varepsilon_{i,t}\), so the true slope is \textbf{one}. Entities with larger \(x\) tend to have lower \(\alpha_i\). Pooling the observations hides this heterogeneity and produces a negative slope; joining each entity's observations reveals the positive within-entity relationship.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/simulPanel-1} 

}

\caption{Between-entity differences can conceal a positive within-entity relationship. Both panels show the same simulated observations. Grey segments join each entity's two periods; the blue line in panel (a) is pooled OLS.}\label{fig:simulPanel}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Running data: electricity imports at Swiss meters.} \texttt{AEC::CHelectricity} records daily grid imports and exports in 2023--2024. We use the 30 apartment and 30 single-family-house meters in its compact panel and retain only complete daily import totals (Appendix \ref{data-electricity}). Our outcome is electricity \textbf{drawn from the grid}, in kWh per day. It is not total electricity use: on-site generation consumed directly is not measured by imports.

The question is simple: \textbf{does a given installation draw more or less electricity from the grid on weekends?} Meter effects absorb stable differences in equipment, building size and occupancy. Month--year effects absorb common seasonal and annual movements. Neither effect absorbs day-to-day weather or changes in occupancy, so we interpret the result as a calendar association.

There are \textbf{39,073 meter--day observations} from \textbf{60 meters}. A meter contributes between 85 and 731 days. Missing readings are not zero imports, and we do not fill them in. The resulting panel is \textbf{unbalanced}. The formulas below use a common \(T\) for clarity; for an unbalanced panel, means and observation counts use the available periods \(T_i\) for each entity. Selective missingness can still affect the estimates.

We will make use of the following notations:
\[
\mathbf{y}_i =
\underbrace{\left[
\begin{array}{c}
y_{i,1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{i,T}
\end{array}\right]}_{T \times 1}, \quad
\boldsymbol\varepsilon_i =
\underbrace{\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\varepsilon_{i,1}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{i,T}
\end{array}\right]}_{T \times 1}, \quad
\mathbf{x}_i =
\underbrace{\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\mathbf{x}_{i,1}'\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{x}_{i,T}'
\end{array}\right]}_{T \times K}, \quad
\mathbf{X} =
\underbrace{\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\mathbf{x}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{x}_{n}
\end{array}\right]}_{(nT) \times K}.
\]
\[
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_i =
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
y_{i,1} - \bar{y}_i\\
\vdots\\
y_{i,T} - \bar{y}_i
\end{array}\right], \quad
\tilde{\boldsymbol\varepsilon}_i =
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\varepsilon_{i,1} - \bar{\varepsilon}_i\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{i,T} - \bar{\varepsilon}_i
\end{array}\right],
\]
\[
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_i =
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\mathbf{x}_{i,1}' - \bar{\mathbf{x}}_i'\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{x}_{i,T}' - \bar{\mathbf{x}}_i'
\end{array}\right], \quad
\tilde{\mathbf{X}} =
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{n}
\end{array}\right], \quad
\tilde{\mathbf{y}} =
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{n}
\end{array}\right],
\]
where
\[
\bar{y}_i = \frac{1}{T} \sum_{t=1}^T y_{i,t}, \quad \bar{\varepsilon}_i = \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^T \varepsilon_{i,t} \quad \mbox{and} \quad \bar{\mathbf{x}}_i = \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^T \mathbf{x}_{i,t}.
\]

\section{Three standard cases}\label{panel-cases}

How the individual effect relates to the observed regressors determines the appropriate estimator. The following three cases separate pooled models, fixed effects, and random effects.

There are three typical situations:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Pooled regression}: \(\mathbf{z}_i \equiv 1\). This case amounts to the case studied in Chapter \ref{ChapterLS}.
\item
  \textbf{Fixed Effects} (Section \ref{FixedEffect}): \(\mathbf{z}_i\) is unobserved, but correlates with \(\mathbf{x}_i\) \(\Rightarrow\) \(\mathbf{b}\) is biased and inconsistent in the OLS regression of \(\mathbf{y}\) on \(\mathbf{X}\) (omitted variable, see Section \ref{Omitted}).
\item
  \textbf{Random Effects} (Section \ref{RandomEffect}): \(\mathbf{z}_i\) is unobserved, but uncorrelated with \(\mathbf{x}_i\). The model writes:
  \[
  y_{i,t} = \mathbf{x}'_{i,t}\boldsymbol\beta + \alpha +  \underbrace{{\color{blue}u_i + \varepsilon_{i,t}}}_{\mbox{compound error}},
  \]
  where \(\alpha = \mathbb{E}(\mathbf{z}'_{i}\boldsymbol\alpha)\) and \(u_i = \mathbf{z}'_{i}\boldsymbol\alpha - \mathbb{E}(\mathbf{z}'_{i}\boldsymbol\alpha)\), with \(\mathbb{E}(u_i\mid\mathbf{x}_i)=0\). In that case, the OLS is consistent, but not efficient. GLS can be used to gain efficiencies over OLS (see Section \ref{GLS} for a presentation of the GLS approach).
\end{itemize}

\section{Estimation of fixed-effects models}\label{FixedEffect}

When individual effects may be correlated with the regressors, the estimator must remove those effects rather than treat them as part of the disturbance. The fixed-effects approaches below do so through dummy variables, within transformations, or first differences.

\begin{hypothesis}[Fixed-effect model]
\protect\hypertarget{hyp:FE}{}\label{hyp:FE}

We assume that:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\roman{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The time-varying regressors retain full column rank after removing entity means; a common intercept and time-invariant regressors are absorbed by the entity effects.
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i,t}|\mathbf{X},\boldsymbol\alpha)=0\), for all \(i,t\) (strict exogeneity conditional on the entity effects).
\item
  We have:
  \[
  \mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i,t}\varepsilon_{j,s}|\mathbf{X},\boldsymbol\alpha) =
  \left\{
  \begin{array}{cl}
  \sigma^2 & \mbox{if $i=j$ and $s=t$},\\
  0 & \mbox{otherwise.}
  \end{array}\right.
  \]
\end{enumerate}

\end{hypothesis}

These assumptions are analogous to those introduced in the standard linear regression:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{(\roman{enumi})}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\leftrightarrow\) Hyp. \ref{hyp:fullrank}, (ii) \(\leftrightarrow\) Hyp. \ref{hyp:exogeneity}, (iii) \(\leftrightarrow\) Hyp. \ref{hyp:homoskedasticity} + \ref{hyp:noncorrelResid}.
\end{enumerate}

In matrix form, for a given \(i\), the model writes:
\[
\mathbf{y}_i = \mathbf{x}_i \boldsymbol\beta + \mathbf{1}_T\alpha_i + \boldsymbol\varepsilon_i,
\]
where \(\mathbf{1}_T\) is a \(T\)-dimensional vector of ones.

This is the \textbf{Least Square Dummy Variable (LSDV)} model:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y} = [\mathbf{X} \quad \mathbf{D}]
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\boldsymbol\beta\\
\boldsymbol\alpha
\end{array}
\right]
+ \boldsymbol\varepsilon, \label{eq:LSDV}
\end{equation}
with:
\[
\mathbf{D} = \underbrace{ \left[\begin{array}{cccc}
\mathbf{1}_T&\mathbf{0}&\dots&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{1}_T&\dots&\mathbf{0}\\
&&\vdots&\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\dots&\mathbf{1}_T\\
\end{array}\right]}_{(nT \times n)}.
\]

The linear regression (Eq. \eqref{eq:LSDV}) ---with the dummy variables--- satisfies the Gauss-Markov conditions (Theorem \ref{thm:GaussMarkov}). Hence, in this context, the OLS estimator is the \emph{best linear unbiased estimator} (BLUE).

Denoting by \(\mathbf{Z}\) the matrix \([\mathbf{X} \quad \mathbf{D}]\), and by \(\mathbf{b}\) and \(\mathbf{a}\) the respective OLS estimates of \(\boldsymbol\beta\) and of \(\boldsymbol\alpha\), we have:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{a}
\end{array}
\right]
= [\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{Z}]^{-1}\mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{y}.} \label{eq:bfixedeffects11}
\end{equation}

When the number of entities \(n\) grows while \(T\) is fixed, the dimension of \(\boldsymbol\alpha\) also grows. Consequently, the usual fixed-dimensional OLS limit theorem cannot be applied to the full vector \([\mathbf{b}',\mathbf{a}']'\). Under strict exogeneity, cross-sectional independence, and standard moment and rank conditions, the within estimator \(\mathbf{b}\) of the common slope vector \(\boldsymbol\beta\) is consistent and asymptotically normal as \(n\rightarrow\infty\) with fixed \(T\). By contrast, each individual effect \(\alpha_i\) is estimated from only \(T\) observations and is generally not consistent when \(T\) remains fixed.

In practice, an estimator of the covariance matrix of \([\mathbf{b}',\mathbf{a}']'\) is:
\[
s^2 \left( \mathbf{Z}'\mathbf{Z}\right)^{-1} \quad with \quad s^2 = \frac{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}}{nT - K - n},
\]
where \(\mathbf{e}\) is the \((nT) \times 1\) vector of OLS residuals.

There is an alternative way of expressing the LSDV estimators. It involves the residual-maker matrix \(\mathbf{M_D}=\mathbf{Id} - \mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}'\mathbf{D})^{-1}\mathbf{D}'\) (see Eq. \eqref{eq:Mres}), which acts as an operator that removes entity-specific means, i.e.:
\[
\tilde{\mathbf{y}} = \mathbf{M_D}\mathbf{y}, \quad \tilde{\mathbf{X}} = \mathbf{M_D}\mathbf{X} \quad and \quad \tilde{\boldsymbol\varepsilon} = \mathbf{M_D}\boldsymbol\varepsilon.
\]

With these notations, using the Frisch-Waugh theorem (Theorem \ref{thm:FW}), we get another expression for the estimator \(\mathbf{b}\) appearing in Eq. \eqref{eq:bfixedeffects11}:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mathbf{b} = [\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{M_D}\mathbf{X}]^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{M_D}\mathbf{y}.}\label{eq:bfixedeffects}
\end{equation}

This amounts to regressing the \(\tilde{y}_{i,t}\)'s (\(= y_{i,t} - \bar{y}_i\)) on the \(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i,t}\)'s (\(=\mathbf{x}_{i,t} - \bar{\mathbf{x}}_i\)). Each observation is compared with its own entity mean. Stable differences between entities disappear, including time-invariant observed characteristics. Strict exogeneity is still needed: demeaning does not remove time-varying confounding. Assumption (iii) gives the conventional covariance formula; clustered covariance estimates relax its error restrictions without changing the within coefficients.

The estimate of \(\boldsymbol\alpha\) is given by:
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\mathbf{a} = (\mathbf{D}'\mathbf{D})^{-1}\mathbf{D}'(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}),} \label{eq:a}
\end{equation}
which is obtained by developing the second row of
\[
\left[
\begin{array}{cc}
\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X} & \mathbf{X}'\mathbf{D}\\
\mathbf{D}'\mathbf{X} & \mathbf{D}'\mathbf{D}
\end{array}\right]
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{a}
\end{array}\right] =
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{y}\\
\mathbf{D}'\mathbf{y}
\end{array}\right],
\]
which are the first-order conditions resulting from the least squares problem (see Eq. \eqref{eq:OLSFOC}).

\textbf{First differences provide another way to remove entity effects.} Subtract the previous observation from the current one:
\[
\Delta y_{i,t}=(\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i,t})'\boldsymbol\beta+\Delta\varepsilon_{i,t}.
\]
The time-invariant \(\alpha_i\) cancels. With two periods, this gives the same slope as the within estimator; with more periods, the estimators generally differ. Even independent level errors generate dependence between adjacent differenced errors because they share an innovation. The covariance estimator must account for that transformation.

One can use different types of fixed effects in the same regression. Typically, one can have time and entity fixed effects. In that case, the model writes:
\[
y_{i,t} = \mathbf{x}_{i,t}'\boldsymbol\beta + \alpha_i + \gamma_t + \varepsilon_{i,t}.
\]

The LSDV approach (Eq. \eqref{eq:LSDV}) can still be resorted to. It suffices to extend the \(\mathbf{Z}\) matrix with additional columns (then called \emph{time dummies}):
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y} = [\mathbf{X} \quad \mathbf{D} \quad \mathbf{C}]
\left[
\begin{array}{c}
\boldsymbol\beta\\
\boldsymbol\alpha\\
\boldsymbol\gamma
\end{array}
\right]
+ \boldsymbol\varepsilon, \label{eq:LSDV2}
\end{equation}
with:
\[
\mathbf{C} = \left[\begin{array}{cccc}
\boldsymbol{\delta}_1&\boldsymbol{\delta}_2&\dots&\boldsymbol{\delta}_{T-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\delta}_1&\boldsymbol{\delta}_2&\dots&\boldsymbol{\delta}_{T-1}\\
\end{array}\right],
\]
where the \(T\)-dimensional vector \(\boldsymbol\delta_t\) (the \emph{time dummy}) is
\[
[0,\dots,0,\underbrace{1}_{\mbox{t$^{th}$ entry}},0,\dots,0]'.
\]

The interpretation of these effects is particularly concrete in our running application.

\begin{example}[Weekends at the same electricity meter]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:electricity-panel}{}\label{exm:electricity-panel}We estimate
\[
imports_{i,t}=\beta\,weekend_t+\alpha_i+\gamma_{m(t)}+\varepsilon_{i,t},
\]
where \(m(t)\) denotes the month--year containing day \(t\). The coefficient \(\beta\) measures the fitted weekend--weekday difference, in \textbf{kWh per day}, after allowing each meter its own baseline and each month--year a common shift.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Model & Weekend difference (kWh/day)\\
\midrule
Month--year effects & 0.597\\
Meter + month--year effects & 0.622\\
Partial regression & 0.622\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The fixed-effects estimate is \textbf{0.62 additional kWh per weekend day} for the same meter, after month--year adjustment. This is a difference in levels, not a percentage. The pooled and fixed-effects estimates are close: removing meter baselines need not change a slope substantially when nearly the same calendar variation is observed for each meter.

The last row verifies the within logic numerically. First remove the meter and month--year effects from both imports and the weekend indicator; then regress the residual imports on the residual indicator. The resulting slope exactly equals the dummy-variable estimate. With an unbalanced panel and two sets of effects, subtracting the meter mean and time mean once is not generally equivalent to this partial regression.

Figure \ref{fig:electricity-meter-differences} shows the unadjusted comparison for each meter. The spread of the points shows why the common coefficient should be read as a summary: individual installations have different weekend patterns.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/electricity-meter-differences-1} 

}

\caption{Weekend minus weekday mean grid imports for each of the 60 meters, in 2023--2024. Each point is one meter. These raw differences are descriptive; the regression also adjusts for month--year effects.}\label{fig:electricity-meter-differences}
\end{figure}

Meter effects cannot identify coefficients on characteristics that never change within a meter, such as its recorded installation type. Conversely, \textbf{daily} date effects would absorb the weekend indicator, which is common to all meters on a given date. Month--year effects leave weekend--weekday variation within each month available for estimation.

These are associations for the sampled installations, not a representative estimate for Swiss households. We return to uncertainty in Example \ref{exm:electricity-panel-inference}.
\end{example}

\section{Estimation of random effects models}\label{RandomEffect}

If the individual effect is uncorrelated with the regressors, it can be incorporated into the covariance structure of a composite error. This additional assumption permits random-effects estimation and can improve efficiency by using both within- and between-entity variation.

Here, the individual effects are assumed to be not correlated to other variables (the \(\mathbf{x}_i\)'s). In that context, the OLS estimator is consistent. However, it is not efficient. The GLS approach can be employed to gain efficiency.

\Needspace{18\baselineskip}

\textbf{Random-effect models} write:
\[
y_{i,t}=\mathbf{x}'_{it}\boldsymbol\beta + (\alpha + \underbrace{u_i}_{\substack{\text{Random}\\\text{heterogeneity}}}) + \varepsilon_{i,t},
\]
with
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i,t}|\mathbf{X})&=&\mathbb{E}(u_{i}|\mathbf{X}) =0,\\
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i,t}\varepsilon_{j,s}|\mathbf{X}) &=&
\left\{
\begin{array}{cl}
\sigma_\varepsilon^2 & \mbox{ if $i=j$ and $s=t$},\\
0 & \mbox{ otherwise.}
\end{array}
\right.\\
\mathbb{E}(u_{i}u_{j}|\mathbf{X}) &=&
\left\{
\begin{array}{cl}
\sigma_u^2 & \mbox{ if $i=j$},\\
0 & \mbox{otherwise.}
\end{array}
\right.\\
\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i,t}u_{j}|\mathbf{X})&=&0 \quad \text{for all $i$, $j$ and $t$}.
\end{eqnarray*}

Introducing the notations \(\eta_{i,t} = u_i + \varepsilon_{i,t}\) and \(\boldsymbol\eta_i = [\eta_{i,1},\dots,\eta_{i,T}]'\), we have \(\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol\eta_i |\mathbf{X}) = \mathbf{0}\) and \(\mathbb{V}ar(\boldsymbol\eta_i | \mathbf{X}) = \boldsymbol\Gamma\), where
\[
\boldsymbol\Gamma = \left[  \begin{array}{ccccc}
\sigma_\varepsilon^2+\sigma_u^2 & \sigma_u^2 & \sigma_u^2 & \dots & \sigma_u^2\\
\sigma_u^2 & \sigma_\varepsilon^2+\sigma_u^2 & \sigma_u^2 & \dots & \sigma_u^2\\
\vdots && \ddots && \vdots \\
\sigma_u^2 & \sigma_u^2 & \sigma_u^2 & \dots & \sigma_\varepsilon^2+\sigma_u^2\\
\end{array}
\right] = \sigma_\varepsilon^2\mathbf{Id}_T + \sigma_u^2\mathbf{1}_T\mathbf{1}_T',
\]
where \(\mathbf{Id}_T\) is the identity matrix of dimension \(T \times T\), and \(\mathbf{1}_T\) is a \(T\)-dimensional vector of ones.

Denoting by \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) the covariance matrix of \(\boldsymbol\eta = [\boldsymbol\eta_1',\dots,\boldsymbol\eta_n']'\), we have:
\[
\boldsymbol\Sigma = \mathbf{Id}_n \otimes \boldsymbol\Gamma.
\]

Here \(\mathbf{Id}_n\) is the identity matrix of dimension \(n \times n\). In the GLS formula below, the design matrix includes a column for the common intercept.

If we knew \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\), we would apply GLS (Eq. \eqref{eq:betaGLS}, in Section \ref{GLS}):
\[
\mathbf{b}_{GLS} = (\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}'\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{y}.
\]
(As explained in Section \ref{GLS}, this amounts to regressing \({\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1/2}}'\mathbf{y}\) on \({\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1/2}}'\mathbf{X}\).)

It can be checked that \(\boldsymbol\Sigma^{-1/2} = \mathbf{Id}_n \otimes (\boldsymbol\Gamma^{-1/2})\) where
\[
\boldsymbol\Gamma^{-1/2} = \frac{1}{\sigma_\varepsilon}\left( \mathbf{Id}_T - \frac{\theta}{T}\mathbf{1}_T\mathbf{1}_T'\right),\quad \mbox{with}\quad\theta = 1 - \frac{\sigma_\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\sigma_\varepsilon^2+T\sigma_u^2}}.
\]

Hence, if we knew \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\), we would transform the data as follows:
\[
\boldsymbol\Gamma^{-1/2}\mathbf{y}_i = \frac{1}{\sigma_\varepsilon}\left[\begin{array}{c}y_{i,1} - \theta\bar{y}_i\\y_{i,2} - \theta\bar{y}_i\\\vdots\\y_{i,T} - \theta\bar{y}_i\\\end{array}\right].
\]

What about when \(\boldsymbol\Sigma\) is unknown? One can take deviations from group means to remove heterogeneity:
\begin{equation}
y_{i,t} - \bar{y}_i = [\mathbf{x}_{i,t} - \bar{\mathbf{x}}_i]'\boldsymbol\beta + (\varepsilon_{i,t} - \bar{\varepsilon}_i).\label{eq:OLSRUM}
\end{equation}
The previous equation can be estimated consistently by OLS. Although its residuals are correlated across periods for a given entity, this dependence affects the covariance estimator rather than coefficient consistency; see Proposition \ref{prp:ols-grm-consistency}.

We have \(\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\varepsilon_{i,t}-\bar{\varepsilon}_i)^2\right] = (T-1)\sigma_{\varepsilon}^2\).

The \(\varepsilon_{i,t}\)'s are not observed but \(\mathbf{b}\), the OLS estimator of \(\boldsymbol\beta\) in Eq. \eqref{eq:OLSRUM}, is a consistent estimator of \(\boldsymbol\beta\). Using an adjustment for the degrees of freedom, we can approximate their variance with:
\[
\hat{\sigma}_\varepsilon^2 = \frac{1}{nT-n-K}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(e_{i,t} - \bar{e}_i)^2.
\]

What about \(\sigma_u^2\)? We can exploit the fact that OLS are consistent in the pooled regression:
\[
\mbox{plim }s^2_{pooled} = \mbox{plim }\frac{\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e}}{nT-K-1} = \sigma_u^2 + \sigma_\varepsilon^2,
\]
and therefore use \(\max(0,s^2_{pooled} - \hat{\sigma}_\varepsilon^2)\) as an approximation to \(\sigma_u^2\). Estimating these components and inserting them in \(\theta\) gives feasible GLS. Random effects quasi-demeans the data: \(y_{i,t}-\hat\theta\bar y_i\) is regressed on the similarly transformed regressors, including the transformed intercept. At \(\theta=0\) this is pooling; as \(\theta\) approaches one, it approaches the within transformation.

\begin{example}[When can random effects use between-entity information?]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:panel-random-simulation}{}\label{exm:panel-random-simulation}Consider \(y_{i,t}=2+x_{i,t}+u_i+\varepsilon_{i,t}\), with independent standard-normal \(u_i\), \(\varepsilon_{i,t}\) and \(v_{i,t}\). The true slope is one. We simulate 400 entities over six periods and change just one assumption: in the first case \(x_{i,t}=v_{i,t}\); in the second \(x_{i,t}=0.8u_i+v_{i,t}\). The second case makes persistent heterogeneity part of the regressor.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Case & Pooled & FE & RE & Hausman p-value\\
\midrule
Uncorrelated effects & 1.044 & 1.016 & 1.021 & 0.207\\
Correlated effects & 1.497 & 1.016 & 1.310 & <0.001\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

With uncorrelated effects, all three estimates are near one. Random effects can use between-entity information without importing a correlation between \(x\) and \(u\). With correlated effects, pooling and random effects are pulled away from one; fixed effects removes \(u_i\) and uses the same within-entity variation in both cases. These are individual simulated estimates, not a demonstration of relative efficiency.

The \citet{Hausman_1978} test compares the fixed- and random-effects slope estimates. Under the classical random-effects assumptions, both are consistent and random effects is efficient; systematic differences therefore count against those assumptions. A large p-value does not establish that the entity effect is uncorrelated with the regressors. The standard test used here relies on the simulated homoskedastic, independent-error structure; it should not be applied mechanically to dependent empirical panels.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Returning to electricity: effects and uncertainty]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:electricity-panel-inference}{}\label{exm:electricity-panel-inference}The meter and month--year effects specify the \textbf{conditional mean}. They do not make the remaining daily disturbances independent. Imports at one meter may be persistent; weather and other common shocks can affect several meters on the same day. We keep the fitted model from Example \ref{exm:electricity-panel} and change only its covariance estimator.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Uncertainty estimator & Weekend difference & Standard error\\
\midrule
Conventional & 0.622 & 0.198\\
Heteroskedasticity robust & 0.622 & 0.198\\
Cluster: meter & 0.622 & 0.254\\
Cluster: meter + day & 0.622 & 0.304\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The coefficient stays at \textbf{0.62 kWh/day} in every row. Conventional uncertainty assumes homoskedastic, uncorrelated errors. HC1 allows unequal variances; meter clustering also permits dependence through time within a meter. Two-way clustering permits both within-meter dependence and same-day dependence across meters (Section \ref{Clusters}). Its standard error is \textbf{0.30 kWh/day}.

Clustering changes our assessment of precision; it does not remove weather-related omitted variables. The two-way calculation also rules out dependence between different meters on different days. Persistent common weather shocks or meters belonging to the same building can violate that restriction. The data identify meters but not buildings, so the table illustrates the role of covariance assumptions rather than establishing a causal weekend effect. With only 60 meter clusters, finite-sample uncertainty also deserves care.
\end{example}

\section{Dynamic panel regressions}\label{DynPanel}

The preceding estimators assume that lagged outcomes do not appear among the regressors. Once they do, the transformed lag is mechanically correlated with the transformed error. Dynamic-panel methods address this endogeneity with internal instruments constructed from earlier observations.

In what precedes, it has been assumed that there is no correlation between the observations indexed by \((i,t)\) and those indexed by \((j,s)\) as long as \(j \ne i\) or \(t \ne s\). If one suspects that the errors \(\varepsilon_{i,t}\) are correlated (across entities \(i\) for a given date \(t\), or across dates for a given entity, or both), then one should employ a robust covariance matrix (see Section \ref{Clusters}).

In several cases, auto-correlation in the variable of interest may stem from an auto-regressive specification. That is, Eq. \eqref{eq:panel1} is then replaced by:
\begin{equation}
y_{i,t} = \rho y_{i,t-1} + \mathbf{x}'_{i,t}\underbrace{\boldsymbol\beta}_{K \times 1} + \underbrace{\alpha_i}_{\mbox{Individual effects}} + \varepsilon_{i,t}.\label{eq:paneldyn}
\end{equation}

In that case, even if the explanatory variables \(\mathbf{x}_{i,t}\) are uncorrelated to the errors \(\varepsilon_{i,t}\), we have that the additional \emph{explanatory variable} \(y_{i,t-1}\) correlates to the errors \(\varepsilon_{i,t-1},\varepsilon_{i,t-2},\dots,\varepsilon_{i,1}\). As a result, the LSDV estimate of the model parameters \(\{\rho,\boldsymbol\beta\}\) may be biased, even if \(n\) is large. To see this, notice that the LSDV regression amounts to regressing \(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}\) on \(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\) (see Eq. \eqref{eq:bfixedeffects}), where the elements of \(\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}\) are the explanatory variables from which we subtract their within-sample means. In particular, we have:
\[
\tilde{y}_{i,t-1} = y_{i,t-1} - \frac{1}{T} \sum_{s=1}^{T} y_{i,s-1},
\]
which correlates to the corresponding error, that is:
\[
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i,t} = \varepsilon_{i,t} - \frac{1}{T} \sum_{s=1}^{T} \varepsilon_{i,s}.
\]

The previous equation shows that the \emph{within-group} estimator (LSDV) introduces all realizations of the \(\varepsilon_{i,t}\) errors into the transformed error term (\(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i,t}\)). As a result, in large-\(n\) fixed-\(T\) panels, it is consistent only if all the right-hand-side variables of the regression are strictly exogenous (i.e., do not correlate to past, present, and future errors \(\varepsilon_{i,t}\)).\footnote{Although the bias may vanish for large \(T\)'s, it does not if \(n\) only goes to infinity.} This is not the case when there are lags of \(y_{i,t}\) on the right-hand side of the regression formula.

\begin{example}[Persistence in a short panel]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:panel-dynamic-simulation}{}\label{exm:panel-dynamic-simulation}Suppose \(y_{i,t}=\alpha_i+0.6y_{i,t-1}+\varepsilon_{i,t}\). We simulate 1,000 independent entities, eight transitions per entity, and independent standard-normal effects and innovations. Initial observations are drawn from the stationary distribution conditional on each effect: mean \(\alpha_i/(1-0.6)\) and variance \(1/(1-0.6^2)\).

Pooled OLS estimates persistence at \textbf{0.918}, because a high previous outcome also signals a high persistent entity effect. Removing entity means gives \textbf{0.368}, below the true \textbf{0.600}: the transformed lag and transformed disturbance share past innovations. Fixed effects solves the first problem but creates the second in a short dynamic panel.
\end{example}

To address this, one can resort to instrumental-variable regressions. \citet{Anderson_Hsiao_1982} have, in particular, proposed a first-differenced Two Stage Least Squares (2SLS) estimator (see Eq. \eqref{eq:IV} in Section \ref{IV}). This estimation is based on the following transformation of the model:
\begin{equation}
\Delta y_{i,t} = \rho \Delta y_{i,t-1} + (\Delta \mathbf{x}_{i,t})'\boldsymbol\beta + \Delta\varepsilon_{i,t}.\label{eq:paneldynFisrtDiff}
\end{equation}
The OLS estimates of the parameters are biased because \(\varepsilon_{i,t-1}\) ---which is part of the error \(\Delta\varepsilon_{i,t}\)--- is correlated to \(y_{i,t-1}\) ---which is part of the ``explanatory variable'', namely \(\Delta y_{i,t-1}\). But consistent estimates can be obtained using 2SLS with instrumental variables that are correlated with the endogenous regressor \(\Delta y_{i,t-1}\) but orthogonal to \(\Delta\varepsilon_{i,t}\). If level innovations are serially uncorrelated and initial conditions are independent of future innovations, \(y_{i,t-2}\) is orthogonal to \(\Delta\varepsilon_{i,t}\). It is useful as an instrument only if it also predicts \(\Delta y_{i,t-1}\). Valid instruments for the covariates require their own exogeneity assumptions. Note that this approach can be implemented only if there are at least 3 time observations per entity \(i\).

If the explanatory variables \(\mathbf{x}_{i,t}\) are predetermined, they may correlate with past errors but not with current or future errors; then \(\mathbf{x}_{i,t-1}\) is a valid instrument for the differenced equation at date \(t\). If they are strictly exogenous, they are uncorrelated with \(\varepsilon_{i,s}\) at every date \(s\), and contemporaneous \(\mathbf{x}_{i,t}\) can also be used as instruments.

Returning to Example \ref{exm:panel-dynamic-simulation}, first differencing removes \(\alpha_i\). We use the level two periods earlier to instrument the lagged change. There are seven usable differenced observations per entity.

The IV estimate is \textbf{0.585}, compared with a true persistence of \textbf{0.600}. The assumptions are satisfied by construction here; in an empirical application they must be defended. In particular, serial correlation in the level innovations may invalidate the lag instrument, and high persistence can make lagged levels weak predictors of changes.

For a given differenced equation, all sufficiently old levels may provide instruments. At \(t=3\), the available level is \(y_{i,1}\); at \(t=4\), it is possible to use \(y_{i,1}\) and \(y_{i,2}\). For the scalar model without covariates and with five observed dates, the full instrument matrix is
\[
Z_i=\begin{bmatrix}
y_{i,1}&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&y_{i,1}&y_{i,2}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&y_{i,1}&y_{i,2}&y_{i,3}
\end{bmatrix}.
\]
Each row corresponds to one equation, at \(t=3,4,5\); separate columns distinguish instruments used at different dates. With \(\Delta\boldsymbol\varepsilon_i=[\Delta\varepsilon_{i,3},\ldots,\Delta\varepsilon_{i,T}]'\), the moment conditions are
\[
\mathbb{E}(Z_i'\Delta\boldsymbol\varepsilon_i)=0.
\]
Covariates can contribute additional instruments when their timing assumptions justify them. The instrument count can grow quickly with \(T\); using every available moment is not compulsory.

These restrictions are used in the GMM approach employed by \citet{Arellano_Bond_1991}. Specifically, a GMM estimator of the model parameters is given by:
\[
\underset{\rho,\boldsymbol\beta}{\operatorname{argmin}}\;\left(\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n Z_i' \Delta \boldsymbol\varepsilon_i\right)'W_n\left(\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n Z_i' \Delta \boldsymbol\varepsilon_i\right),
\]
where the differenced residuals depend on the candidate parameters \((\rho,\boldsymbol\beta)\). A two-step procedure uses the weighting matrix
\[
W_n = \left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n Z_i'\widehat{\Delta\boldsymbol\varepsilon_i}\widehat{\Delta\boldsymbol\varepsilon_i}'Z_i\right)^{-1},
\]
where the \(\widehat{\Delta\boldsymbol\varepsilon_i}\)'s are residual vectors from a preliminary consistent parameter estimate. In this sense, this estimator is a two-step GMM one.

If the level disturbances are homoskedastic and serially uncorrelated, then it can be shown that an asymptotically equivalent (efficient) GMM estimator can be obtained by using:
\[
W_{1,n} = \left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_i'HZ_i\right)^{-1},
\]
where \(H\) is the \((T-2) \times (T-2)\) matrix of the form:
\[
H = \left[\begin{array}{ccccccc}
2 & -1 & 0 & \dots &0 \\
-1 & 2 & -1 &  & \vdots \\
0 & \ddots& \ddots & \ddots & 0 \\
\vdots &  & -1 & 2&-1\\
0&\dots & 0 & -1 & 2
\end{array}\right].
\]

It is straightforward to extend these GMM methods to cases where there is more than one lag of the dependent variable on the right-hand side of the equation or in cases where disturbances feature limited moving-average serial correlation.

\begin{example}[Using a few additional lag instruments]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:panel-dynamic-gmm}{}\label{exm:panel-dynamic-gmm}We keep the simulated panel from Example \ref{exm:panel-dynamic-simulation}. Difference GMM uses the levels two and three dates earlier when available. We combine each lag's moments across dates, giving just \textbf{two instrument columns}, rather than an instrument for every lag--date combination. This deliberately keeps the instrument set small.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Method & Persistence & Observations\\
\midrule
Pooled OLS & 0.918 & 8000\\
Within & 0.368 & 8000\\
Differenced IV & 0.585 & 7000\\
Difference GMM: one step & 0.580 & 7000\\
Difference GMM: two steps & 0.580 & 7000\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The true persistence is \textbf{0.600}. The two GMM estimates are similar here, and the two-step estimate has an entity-robust, finite-sample-corrected standard error of \textbf{0.031}. This is one realization of a known model; it does not imply that two-step GMM is always closer to the truth or that adding instruments always improves estimation.

\textbf{What should the differenced residuals look like?} Even when level innovations are independent, adjacent differences share one innovation with opposite signs. First-order residual autocorrelation is therefore expected. Second-order autocorrelation would cast doubt on the lag-two instruments. We report both Arellano--Bond tests, along with the two-step overidentification test.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Test & Statistic & p-value\\
\midrule
AR(1) in differenced residuals & -18.063 & <0.001\\
AR(2) in differenced residuals & -0.572 & 0.567\\
Overidentification (two-step J) & 0.155 & 0.694\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The AR tests use a standard-normal reference distribution; the J test has one overidentifying restriction and a chi-squared reference distribution. The pattern is compatible with the model used to generate the data: strong first-order dependence after differencing, without rejection at order two or in the joint moment test. Non-rejection is not proof of valid instruments. In applications, instrument proliferation can weaken these diagnostics; restricting the lag range and combining moments limits that problem.
\end{example}

\section{Introduction to program evaluation}\label{program-evaluation}

Panel methods are especially useful when a policy or intervention affects some entities but not others. Program evaluation studies the causal effects of such treatments, including the implementation or announcement of policy measures. This section introduces potential outcomes, randomized controlled trials, and difference-in-differences. See \citet{Abadie_Cattaneo_2018} for a broad review and \citet{angrist_mostly_2008} for a foundational treatment.

\subsection{Presentation of the problem}\label{presentation-of-the-problem}

For one entity, let \(Y\) be the outcome and \(W\) indicate treatment: \(W=1\) if the entity is treated and \(W=0\) otherwise. Let \(X\) contain characteristics measured before treatment. Assignment may depend on \(X\), but treatment cannot change these pre-treatment measurements. We suppress the entity index temporarily.

We are interested in the effect of the treatment, that is:
\[
Y_1 - Y_0,
\]
where \(Y_1\) corresponds to the outcome obtained under treatment, while \(Y_0\) is the outcome obtained without it. Notice that we have:
\[
Y = (1-W) Y_0 + W Y_1.
\]

The problem is that observing \((Y,W,X)\) is not sufficient to observe the treatment effect \(Y_1 - Y_0\). Additional assumptions are needed to estimate it, or, more precisely, its expectations (\emph{average treatment effect}):
\[
ATE = \mathbb{E}(Y_1 - Y_0).
\]

In general, \(ATE\) need not equal the observed difference between groups:
\[
\alpha = \underbrace{\mathbb{E}(Y|W=1)}_{=\mathbb{E}(Y_1|W=1)} - \underbrace{\mathbb{E}(Y|W=0)}_{=\mathbb{E}(Y_0|W=0)},
\]
that is easier to estimate. Indeed, a consistent estimate of \(\alpha\) is the difference between the means of the outcome variables in two sub-samples: one containing only the treated entities (this gives an estimate of \(\mathbb{E}(Y_1|W=1)\)) and the other containing only the non-treated entities (this gives an estimate of \(\mathbb{E}(Y_0|W=0)\)). Coming back to \(ATE\), the problem is that we won't have direct information regarding \(\mathbb{E}(Y_0|W=1)\) and \(\mathbb{E}(Y_1|W=0)\). However, these two conditional expectations are part of \(ATE\). Indeed, \(ATE = \mathbb{E}(Y_1) - \mathbb{E}(Y_0)\), and:
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}(Y_1) = \mathbb{E}(Y_1|W=0)\mathbb{P}(W=0)+\mathbb{E}(Y_1|W=1)\mathbb{P}(W=1) \label{eq:EY1a}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}(Y_0) = \mathbb{E}(Y_0|W=0)\mathbb{P}(W=0)+\mathbb{E}(Y_0|W=1)\mathbb{P}(W=1). \label{eq:EY0a}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Randomized controlled trials (RCTs)}\label{randomized-controlled-trials-rcts}

The fundamental evaluation problem is that each entity reveals only one potential outcome. Random assignment solves the associated selection problem by making treatment independent of the potential outcomes.

In the context of Randomized controlled trials (RCTs), entities are randomly assigned to receive the treatment. As a result, we have \(\mathbb{E}(Y_1) = \mathbb{E}(Y_1|W=0) = \mathbb{E}(Y_1|W=1)\) and \(\mathbb{E}(Y_0) = \mathbb{E}(Y_0|W=0) = \mathbb{E}(Y_0|W=1)\). Using this into Eqs. \eqref{eq:EY1a} and \eqref{eq:EY0a} yields \(ATE = \alpha\).

Therefore, in this context, estimating \(\mathbb{E}(Y_1-Y_0)\) amounts to computing the difference between two sample means, namely (a) the sample mean of the subset of \(Y_i\)'s corresponding to the entities for which \(W_i=1\), and (b) the one for which \(W_i=0\).

Pre-treatment covariates can improve precision when they predict the outcome. For example, suppose the model is:
\[
Y_{i} = W_{i} \beta_{1} + X_i'\boldsymbol\beta_z + \varepsilon_i,
\]
where \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_i|X_i) = 0\) (and \(W_i\) is independent from \(X_i\) and \(\varepsilon_i\)). In this case, we obtain a consistent estimate of \(\beta_1\) by regressing \(\mathbf{y}\) on \(\mathbf{Z} = [\mathbf{w},\mathbf{X}]\).

\subsection{Difference-in-differences (DiD) approach}\label{did}

Random assignment is often unavailable. Difference-in-differences instead uses changes over time in a treated group and a comparison group, relying on a parallel-trends assumption to construct the missing counterfactual.

DiD does not require random treatment assignment. It exploits two dimensions: entities (\(i\)) and time (\(t\)). To simplify the exposition, we consider only two periods here (\(t=0\) and \(t=1\)).

Consider the following model:
\begin{equation}
Y_{i,t} = W_{i,t} \beta_1 + \mu_i + \delta_t + \varepsilon_{i,t}\label{eq:DiD}
\end{equation}

The parameter of interest is \(\beta_{1}\), which is the treatment effect (recall that \(W_{i,t} \in \{0,1\}\)). Usually, for all entities \(i\), we have \(W_{i,t=0}=0\). But only some of them are treated on date 1, i.e., \(W_{i,1} \in \{0,1\}\).

The treatment indicator may be correlated with the time-invariant effect \(\mu_i\), so \(\mu_i\) may constitute a \emph{confounder}. DiD removes this fixed difference by comparing changes. Its key identifying assumption is \textbf{parallel trends}: in the absence of treatment, the mean change would have been the same in the treated and control groups. In this model, that condition is \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i,1}-\varepsilon_{i,0}\mid W_{i,1}=1)=\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i,1}-\varepsilon_{i,0}\mid W_{i,1}=0)\). We also assume no anticipation: treatment at date 1 does not affect the date-0 outcome.

Let \(D_i=W_{i,1}\) indicate membership of the treated group. Taking first differences removes \(\mu_i\):
\[
\Delta Y_{i,1}=\beta_1 D_i+(\delta_1-\delta_0)+\Delta\varepsilon_{i,1}.
\]
The two groups share the common time change \(\delta_1-\delta_0\). Under parallel trends, they also have the same mean disturbance change. Subtracting their mean changes therefore leaves only the treatment effect:
\[
\boxed{\beta_1=\mathbb{E}(\Delta Y_{i,1}\mid D_i=1)
                   -\mathbb{E}(\Delta Y_{i,1}\mid D_i=0),}
\]
where \(\Delta Y_{i,1}=Y_{i,1}-Y_{i,0}\). The first difference is over time; the second is between groups. The constant-effect model writes this as \(\beta_1\). With heterogeneous effects, the analogous two-period design identifies the average effect on the treated group under parallel trends and no anticipation.

In practice, estimate the two-period DiD with a treated-group indicator, an after-period indicator, and their interaction. The interaction coefficient is the DiD estimate. Unit and time fixed effects give the equivalent formulation when the same entities are observed in both periods. Time-invariant controls do not replace unit effects, and controlling for variables changed by treatment can obscure the effect of interest.

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}

\subsection{Application: speed cameras and injury crashes in France}\label{application-speed-cameras-and-injury-crashes-in-france}

\begin{example}[What happens after a speed camera is installed?]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:panel-did-radars}{}\label{exm:panel-did-radars}Does installing a fixed speed camera reduce injury crashes nearby? \citet{Renne_2026_cameras} studies this question using French accident records and camera installation histories. A before--after comparison is insufficient: road safety changes over time, and cameras may be installed in response to unusually high accident counts. Comparing equipped roads with arbitrary unequipped roads can also confuse the camera effect with differences in road risk.

The comparison group therefore consists of \textbf{similar sites that receive a camera later}. For each newly equipped site, we compare the twelve months before installation with the twelve months after it, omitting the installation month. Controls are observed over \textbf{exactly the same calendar months}, and remain unequipped throughout that post period. They share the treated site's road class and recorded speed limit. This gives a two-period DiD at each installation date; averaging those comparisons accommodates the different installation dates.

\texttt{AEC::FRradars} retains the paper's main sample: \textbf{406 treated-site comparisons}, using \textbf{639 distinct sites}. Crashes are counted near the monitored road, extending two kilometres along it in each direction and at most 100 metres to either side. The paper's geographic and nearby-camera exclusions are retained in the compact data (Appendix \ref{data-radars}). We simplify the presentation, not the principal comparison sample.

\textbf{Start with the four means.} Each row of \texttt{FRradars} is a site appearing in one treated site's comparison set. The stored outcomes are annual counts. We divide by twelve to obtain monthly means and report them per \textbf{100 site-months}, so the small numbers are easier to read. We first average the controls within each comparison, then give each of the 406 comparisons equal weight.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Group & Before & After & Change\\
\midrule
Newly equipped & 4.02 & 3.24 & -0.78\\
Equipped later & 4.19 & 4.89 & 0.70\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The newly equipped sites go from \textbf{4.02} to \textbf{3.24} injury crashes per 100 site-months. At their comparison sites, crashes instead rise from \textbf{4.19} to \textbf{4.89}. If the treated sites had experienced that same increase, their after-period mean would have been \textbf{4.72}. The DiD estimate is therefore
\[
\widehat\tau=
(3.24-4.02)
-(4.89-4.19)
\simeq\boxed{-1.48}.
\]
This is about \textbf{1.48 fewer injury crashes per 100 site-months}, or 0.0148 per site-month. It is equivalent to about 37\% of the treated sites' pre-installation mean. It is not a change in crash risk per journey: the outcome has no traffic denominator.

\begin{figure}

{\centering \includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/panel-radar-did-figure-1} 

}

\caption{The speed-camera comparison. Solid lines show observed means; the dashed line adds the comparison sites' increase to the treated sites' baseline. The vertical gap is the DiD estimate. Means use the same calendar windows within each comparison and equal weights across treated sites.}\label{fig:panel-radar-did-figure}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Why does this remain a DiD?} Let \(g_i\) be the installation month at treated site \(i\), and let \(C_i\) be its eligible comparison sites. Write \(\Delta_i\) for its after-minus-before change, in crashes per 100 site-months, and \(\Delta_{j;g_i}\) for control \(j\)'s change in the same units over those same months. The estimator is simply
\[
\widehat\tau=\frac{1}{406}\sum_{i=1}^{406}
\left[\Delta_i-\frac{1}{|C_i|}\sum_{j\in C_i}\Delta_{j;g_i}\right].
\]
Every bracket is the familiar difference of two changes. A site can act as a control before its own installation and later become treated; it never acts as an already-treated control. The weighted four-mean table reproduces this average exactly. Pooling all control appearances equally would change the weights and would no longer reproduce the paper's estimate.

\textbf{What must we assume?} Without installation, each treated site's mean change would have matched that of its eligible controls. Comparing similar future camera sites helps address persistent differences, but it does not guarantee parallel trends. A temporary pre-installation spike followed by regression to the mean, differential roadworks or local traffic changes could still bias the estimate. We also need no anticipation and no relevant spillovers. The spatial exclusions help separate the measured crash areas, without proving that drivers' responses stop at their boundaries.

\textbf{Check the earlier year, and account for reused sites.} The compact data include months \(-24\) to \(-13\), giving one pre-treatment DiD: compare the final pre-installation year with the preceding year at both groups. This is a diagnostic, not a second treatment effect. For uncertainty, we bootstrap the 639 physical sites, applying each resampled site's weight to every appearance as treated or control and re-normalizing the control means within each comparison. Resampling table rows or treating the 406 comparisons as independent would miss shared-site dependence.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
DiD contrast & Estimate & Bootstrap SE & 95\% lower & 95\% upper\\
\midrule
After vs before & -1.48 & 0.49 & -2.45 & -0.51\\
Before vs earlier year & -0.77 & 0.58 & -1.91 & 0.37\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The first-year interval is approximately \textbf{{[}-2.45, -0.51{]} injury crashes per 100 site-months}. The pre-period contrast is -0.77 and its interval includes zero. This earlier comparison is imprecise; non-rejection does not establish parallel trends. The classroom bootstrap resamples the retained sites and need not reproduce the paper's archived confidence bounds exactly. As in a site-level bootstrap, dependence between distinct sites must be sufficiently weak. The paper also examines finer pre-treatment periods, spatial definitions and other sensitivity checks.

The result concerns local injury-crash counts at these retained camera sites during their first post-installation year. It does not establish an effect for every road, an effect per vehicle, or an effect on total crashes across the road network.
\end{example}

\Needspace{15\baselineskip}

\section{Chapter recap}\label{panel-recap}

\begin{recapbox}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Section \ref{panel-cases} distinguishes pooled, fixed-effects, and random-effects models according to the role of unobserved individual heterogeneity.
\item
  Hypothesis \ref{hyp:FE} permits individual effects to be correlated with regressors; Section \ref{FixedEffect} removes those effects using dummy variables, within transformations, or first differences.
\item
  Section \ref{RandomEffect} gains efficiency by assuming that individual effects are uncorrelated with the regressors and incorporating them into the error covariance structure.
\item
  Examples \ref{exm:electricity-panel} and \ref{exm:electricity-panel-inference} separate the within-meter weekend association from assumptions used to estimate its uncertainty.
\item
  Section \ref{DynPanel} explains why lagged dependent variables become endogenous after fixed-effect transformations and how earlier observations provide instruments.
\item
  Section \ref{program-evaluation} introduces potential outcomes and randomized assignment; Section \ref{did} develops difference-in-differences under parallel trends. Example \ref{exm:panel-did-radars} applies it to French speed cameras, using sites equipped later as controls.
\end{itemize}

\end{recapbox}

\clearpage

\section{Exercises}\label{panel-exercises}

Attempt each question and explain your reasoning, including for true-or-false statements. \textbf{Foundation} exercises apply a result directly; \textbf{Standard} exercises require several steps or a short proof; \textbf{Advanced} exercises combine several results or require a longer derivation. Worked solutions and exercise-specific hints are reserved for teaching sessions and are not included in this student edition.

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Fixed effects in a panel model]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:panel-fixed-effects}{}\label{exr:panel-fixed-effects}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{FixedEffect}.

Consider the following panel regression model: \[y_{i,t} = \beta x_{i,t} + \alpha_i + \varepsilon_{i,t}, \quad i \in \{1, \dots, n\}, \quad t \in \{1, \dots, T\},\] where \(x_{i,t}\) and \(\beta\) are scalars, where the \(\varepsilon_{i,t}\)'s are i.i.d. \(\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)\) and where \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i,t}|\mathbf{X}) = 0\), where \(\mathbf{X} = [\mathbf{x}_1', \dots, \mathbf{x}_n']'\) with \(\mathbf{x}_i = [x_{i,1}, \dots, x_{i,T}]'\) (i.e., \(\mathbf{x}_i\) is of dimension \(T \times 1\) and \(\mathbf{X}\) is of dimension \((nT) \times 1\)). The disturbances \(\varepsilon_{i,t}\) and the explanatory variables \(x_{i,t}\) are assumed to be independent across entities \(i\) and across time \(t\). However, in general, the \(\alpha_i\)s may be correlated to the \(x_{i,t}\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  What is called this type of model?
\item
  For this question only, assume that \(\alpha_i=\alpha\). Explain how to estimate the model.
\end{enumerate}

Return to the general case (where \(\alpha_i\) may be different from \(\alpha_j\) if \(i \neq j\)) and let us use the notation \(\nu_{i,t} = \alpha_i - \alpha + \varepsilon_{i,t}\) where \(\alpha\) is defined by \(\alpha = \mathbb{E}(\alpha_i)\). In this case, the model can be written: \[y_{i,t} = \alpha + \beta x_{i,t} + \nu_{i,t}, \quad i \in \{1, \dots, n\}, t \in \{1, \dots, T\}.\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\item
  What can we say about \(\mathbb{E}(\nu_{i,t}|x_{i,t})\)?
\item
  Explain how to get the best linear unbiased estimators (BLUE) of \(\beta\) and \(\boldsymbol{\alpha} = [\alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_n]'\). (Propose formulas based on matrices that you will specify).
\item
  Give the covariance matrix of these BLUE estimators.
\item
  Explain how one can use an \(F\)-test to test for the equality of the \(\alpha_i\)s.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Individual and time effects: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:panel-effects-true-false}{}\label{exr:panel-effects-true-false}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{Panel}.

Consider the panel regression model:

\[y_{it} = \mathbf{x}_{it}'\boldsymbol{\beta} + \gamma_t + \alpha_i + \varepsilon_{it}\]

where:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \(t \in \{1, \dots, T\}\) indexes the time period.
\item
  \(i \in \{1, \dots, n\}\) indexes the individuals.
\item
  \(\mathbf{x}_{it}\) is a vector of time-varying regressors for individual \(i\) in period \(t\).
\item
  \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) denotes the associated coefficients.
\item
  \(\gamma_t \neq 0\) is a time-specific effect that has a non-zero correlation with \(\mathbf{x}_{it}\).
\item
  \(\alpha_i \neq 0\) is a time-constant unobserved individual effect that has a non-zero correlation with \(\mathbf{x}_{it}\).
\item
  \(\varepsilon_{it}\) is independent across \(i\) and \(t\), independent of all regressors and effects, and satisfies \(\varepsilon_{it} \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)\).
\end{itemize}

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Random effects regression consistently estimates \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) when including both \(\mathbf{x}_{it}\) and \(\gamma_t\) in the regression model.
\item
  In an infinitely large sample, the Hausman test rejects the hypothesis of equal coefficients across random and fixed effects regression when including both \(\mathbf{x}_{it}\) and \(\gamma_t\) in the regression models.
\item
  Fixed effects regression consistently estimates \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) when omitting \(\gamma_t\) in the regression model, while including \(\mathbf{x}_{it}\) in the model.
\item
  The time-varying error term \(\varepsilon_{it}\) satisfies strict exogeneity.
\item
  Random effects regression imposes weaker assumptions on the joint error term \(\alpha_i + \varepsilon_{it}\) than fixed effects regression.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Difference-in-differences from group means]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:panel-difference-in-differences}{}\label{exr:panel-difference-in-differences}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{Panel}.

A policy is introduced between two periods for a treated group but not for a control group. The sample means of the outcome are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Group & Before & After \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Control & 10 & 12 \\
Treated & 11 & 16 \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Compute the before--after change for each group.
\item
  Compute the difference-in-differences estimate.
\item
  State the parallel-trends assumption in this setting and explain its role.
\item
  Consider the regression
  \[
  y_{it}=\alpha+\delta\,\mathrm{Treated}_i+\tau\,\mathrm{After}_t
  +\beta(\mathrm{Treated}_i\times\mathrm{After}_t)+u_{it}.
  \]
  Which coefficient equals the difference-in-differences estimate?
\item
  Give one example of a change occurring between the periods that would violate the identifying assumption.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Endogeneity after first differencing]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:panel-dynamic-panel}{}\label{exr:panel-dynamic-panel}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{DynPanel}.

Consider
\[
y_{it}=\rho y_{i,t-1}+\alpha_i+\varepsilon_{it},
\]
where \(\varepsilon_{it}\) has mean zero, variance \(\sigma^2\), and is serially uncorrelated. Initial conditions are independent of future innovations.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  First-difference the model to remove \(\alpha_i\).
\item
  Show that \(\Delta y_{i,t-1}\) is correlated with \(\Delta\varepsilon_{it}\). In particular, compute \(\mathbb{C}ov(\varepsilon_{i,t-1},\varepsilon_{it}-\varepsilon_{i,t-1})\).
\item
  Explain why OLS applied to the differenced equation is inconsistent when the number of entities grows and the number of periods is fixed.
\item
  Show why \(y_{i,t-2}\) can be a valid instrument for \(\Delta y_{i,t-1}\) under the stated assumptions. Distinguish instrument exogeneity from relevance.
\item
