\section{Chapter recap}\label{estimation-recap}

\begin{recapbox}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Section \ref{secGMM} builds estimators from population moment conditions; Definition \ref{def:GMM} turns their sample analogues into a quadratic criterion.
\item
  Example \ref{exm:gmm-hydro} estimates one Swiss price response from two wind moments. Weighting accounts for their covariance; a non-rejected overidentification test does not establish instrument validity.
\item
  Definition \ref{def:likelihood} views the observed sample as a function of the parameter; Definitions \ref{def:score} and \ref{def:Fisher} summarize its local slope and curvature.
\item
  Definition \ref{def:MLEest} defines the maximum-likelihood estimator, and Proposition \ref{prp:MLEproperties} states its main large-sample properties under regularity conditions.
\item
  Section \ref{TestMLE} compares Wald, LM, and likelihood-ratio tests; Proposition \ref{prp:equivLRLMW} establishes their asymptotic equivalence under the null.
\item
  Section \ref{bayesian} updates the credit-default likelihood with a Beta prior. Examples \ref{exm:bayes-credit} and \ref{exm:mh-credit} distinguish an exact posterior from the Monte Carlo error in approximating it.
\end{itemize}

\end{recapbox}

\clearpage

\section{Exercises}\label{estimation-exercises}

Attempt each question and explain your reasoning, including for true-or-false statements. \textbf{Foundation} exercises apply a result directly; \textbf{Standard} exercises require several steps or a short proof; \textbf{Advanced} exercises combine several results or require a longer derivation. Worked solutions and exercise-specific hints are reserved for teaching sessions and are not included in this student edition.

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Maximum likelihood for an exponential distribution]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-mle-exponential}{}\label{exr:estimation-mle-exponential}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{secMLE}.

Exponential population: Let \(X_1,\dots,X_n\) be i.i.d. with distribution \(\mathcal{E}(\lambda)\), whose density is

\[f_X(x) = \frac{1}{\lambda} e^{-\frac{1}{\lambda}x},\]

with \(\lambda > 0\) and \(x>0\). Suppose the observed sample is \(x_1,\dots,x_n\). Denote it by \(\mathbf{x}=(x_1,\dots,x_n)'\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Construct the likelihood function \(\mathcal{L}(\theta; \mathbf{X})\). What is \(\theta\)?
\item
  Construct the log-likelihood function \(\log \mathcal{L}(\theta; \mathbf{X})\).
\item
  What is the maximum likelihood estimator of \(\theta\)?
\item
  Compute the second derivative of the log-likelihood function \(\log \mathcal{L}(\theta; \mathbf{X})\).
\item
  Noting that \(\mathbb{E}(X) = \lambda\), compute \(\mathbf{I}(\theta_0) = n\mathcal{I}_X(\theta_0)\).
\item
  Find the asymptotic distribution of \(\theta_{MLE}\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Maximum likelihood for a Poisson distribution]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-mle-poisson}{}\label{exr:estimation-mle-poisson}\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{secMLE}.

Poisson population: Let \(X_1,\dots,X_n\) be i.i.d. Poisson random variables with parameter \(\lambda\), so that \(\mathbb{P}(X_i=x)=e^{-\lambda}\lambda^x/x!\) for \(\lambda>0\) and \(x=0,1,2,\dots\). Suppose the observed sample is \(x_1,\dots,x_n\), denoted by \(\mathbf{x}=(x_1,\dots,x_n)'\).

Answer the same questions as in the preceding exponential-distribution exercise.
\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Maximum likelihood for a normal distribution]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-mle-normal}{}\label{exr:estimation-mle-normal}\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{secMLE}.

Normal population: Let \(X_1,\dots,X_n\) be i.i.d. \(\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)\) random variables, with density \[f_X(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}} e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2} (x - \mu)^2},\] where \(\mu \in \mathbb{R}\), \(\sigma > 0\), and \(x \in \mathbb{R}\).\\
Answer the same questions as in the preceding exponential-distribution exercise.
\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Maximum likelihood with GDP crises]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-gdp-crises}{}\label{exr:estimation-gdp-crises}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Application}\\
Review Section \ref{secMLE}.

We denote by \(y_t\) the GDP growth rate of year \(t\). This growth rate is given by:

\[y_t = \varepsilon_t - \gamma d_t,\]

where the variables \(d_t\) and \(\varepsilon_t\) are independent. More precisely: (i) for \(s \neq t\), \(d_t\) and \(d_s\) are independent, as well as \(\varepsilon_t\) and \(\varepsilon_s\), and (ii) for any \(t\) and \(s\), \(d_t\) and \(\varepsilon_s\) are independent. The variable \(d_t\) is a crisis indicator (with \(\gamma > 0\)).

We assume that \(d_t \sim i.i.d.\mathcal{B}(p)\), where \(\mathcal{B}(p)\) denotes a Bernoulli distribution with parameter \(p\) and \(\varepsilon_t \sim i.i.d.\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)\).

Our estimation sample is of length \(T\). We want to estimate \(\theta = [p, \gamma, \mu, \sigma^2]'\). Crucially, we assume that the crisis variables \(d_t\) are not observed; that is, we observe \(y_t\) only.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Express the distribution of \(y_t\) conditional to \(d_t = 0\) (denoted by \(f(y_t|d_t = 0; \theta)\)) and the distribution of \(y_t\) conditional to \(d_t = 1\) (denoted by \(f(y_t|d_t = 1; \theta)\)).
\item
  Show that \(f(y_t; \theta) = p f(y_t|d_t = 1; \theta) + (1 - p) f(y_t|d_t = 0; \theta)\).
\item
  Write the log-likelihood \(\log \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{y}; \theta)\).
\end{enumerate}

The log-likelihood is maximized numerically. The value of \(\theta\) for which the likelihood is maximized is \(\hat{\theta} = [0.04, 2.90, 2.50, 1.10]'\) (the values of \(y_t\) are expressed in percentage points). The inverse of the Hessian matrix of the log-likelihood, computed at \(\hat{\theta}\), is:

\[H = \left( \frac{\partial^2 \log \mathcal{L}(\hat{\theta}; \mathbf{y})}{\partial \theta \partial \theta'} \right)^{-1} =
\begin{bmatrix}
-0.000100 & -0.000015 & -0.000025 & -0.000020 \\
-0.000015 & -0.160002 & 0.019996 & 0.004003 \\
-0.000025 & 0.019996 & -0.042056 & -0.001505 \\
-0.000020 & 0.004003 & -0.001505 & -0.250204
\end{bmatrix}\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{3}
\item
  Explain how to approximate the asymptotic distribution of \(\hat{\theta}\).
\item
  Compute a 95\% confidence interval for \(\gamma\).
\item
  How can the asymptotic variance of \(\hat{\gamma}-\hat{\mu}\) be approximated? Test \(H_0:\gamma=\mu\) at the 5\% significance level.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[OLS and maximum likelihood]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-ols-versus-mle}{}\label{exr:estimation-ols-versus-mle}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Proof}\\
Review Section \ref{secMLE}.

Consider the linear model \[y_i = \beta_1 x_{i,1} + \beta_2 x_{i,2} + \cdots + \beta_K x_{i,K} + \varepsilon_i = \mathbf{x}_i' \boldsymbol{\beta} + \varepsilon_i, \quad i = 1, \dots, n,\] and assume that Assumptions 4.1 to 4.5 of the course on linear regressions hold for this model. Let \(\mathbf{b}_{ML}\) be the maximum-likelihood estimator of \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) and let \(\sigma^2_{ML}\) be the ML estimator of \(\sigma^2\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Show that \(\mathbf{b}_{ML} = \mathbf{b}\), where \(\mathbf{b}\) is the OLS estimator of \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\).
\item
  Compute \(\sigma^2_{ML}\).
\item
  Show that \(\sigma^2_{ML}\) is biased.
\item
  Show that \(\sigma^2_{ML}\) is consistent.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Numerical comparison of OLS and ML inference]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-ols-mle-numerical}{}\label{exr:estimation-ols-mle-numerical}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{secMLE}.

Consider the Gaussian linear model of the preceding exercise, with \(K = 4\). We have a sample of size \(n = 8\). We obtain (with the usual notations):

\[(\mathbf{X}'\mathbf{X})^{-1} =
\begin{pmatrix}
3 & 2 & 2 & 0 \\
2 & 2 & 1 & 0 \\
2 & 1 & 2 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0 & 1
\end{pmatrix},
\quad
\mathbf{b} =
\begin{pmatrix}
1 \\
2 \\
7 \\
7
\end{pmatrix},
\quad
\mathbf{e}'\mathbf{e} = 8\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Compute \(\sigma^2_{ML}\) and \(\sigma^2_{OLS}\) (\(\sigma^2_{OLS}\) is the OLS-based estimate of \(\sigma^2\), that we often denote by \(s^2\)).
\item
  Let \(F_{ML}\) and \(F_{OLS}\) be the test statistics for \(H_0: \mathbf{R}\boldsymbol{\beta} = \mathbf{q}\) when \(\sigma^2_{ML}\) and \(\sigma^2_{OLS}\) are used, respectively. Show that \(F_{OLS} = \frac{F_{ML}}{2}\).
\item
  Compute \(F_{ML}\) and \(F_{OLS}\) for the null hypothesis \(H_0: \beta_2 + \beta_3 = \beta_1 + \beta_4 = 10\). (No final calculation is needed, only write down the matrix form of the calculations that give the test statistics.)
\item
  What are the main issues associated with the use of an F-test based on a Maximum Likelihood estimation (in a small sample)?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Maximum-likelihood theory: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-mle-true-false}{}\label{exr:estimation-mle-true-false}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{secMLE}.

Consider a sample of variables \(y_i\), \(i \in \{1,\dots,n\}\), that are independently and identically distributed. The sample length \(n\) is large. The distribution of the \(y_i\)'s depends on three parameters gathered in vector \(\boldsymbol\theta=[\theta_1,\theta_2,\theta_3]'\). The likelihood function is given by \(\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol\theta;{\bf y})\), where \({\bf y}=[y_1,\dots,y_n]'\).

We denote by \(\boldsymbol\theta_{MLE}=[\theta_{MLE,1},\theta_{MLE,2},\theta_{MLE,3}]'\) the maximum likelihood estimate of \(\boldsymbol\theta\). We obtain \(\boldsymbol\theta_{MLE} = [-2.90,0.82,0.60]'\). The opposite of the inverse of the Hessian matrix of the log-likelihood, evaluated at \(\boldsymbol\theta_{MLE}\), is: \[-\left[\frac{\partial^2\log \mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol\theta_{MLE};{\bf y})}{\partial \boldsymbol\theta \partial \boldsymbol\theta'}\right]^{-1} =
\left[
\begin{array}{ccc}
1.00   &  0.30 & -0.40\\
0.30   & 2.00  & 0.20\\
-0.40  & 0.20  & 0.25
\end{array}
\right].\]

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  An estimate of \(\theta_{MLE,2}\)'s variance is 1.
\item
  An estimate of the variance of \(\theta_{MLE,1}+\theta_{MLE,2}\) is 3.
\item
  \(0\) is not included in the approximate 95\% confidence interval of \(\theta_1\).
\item
  We reject the null hypothesis \(H_0:\;\theta_3=0\) at the 5\% significance level.
\item
  The correlation between the estimates of \(\theta_1\) and \(\theta_3\) is negative.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Exactly identified and overidentified moments]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-gmm-moments}{}\label{exr:estimation-gmm-moments}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{secGMM}.

Let \(Y_1,\dots,Y_n\) be i.i.d. with mean \(\mu\), variance \(v>0\), and finite fourth moment. Let \(\boldsymbol\theta=(\mu,v)'\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Construct two moment conditions based on the mean and variance.
\item
  Show that the exactly identified GMM estimator solves these sample moments and obtain explicit formulas for \(\hat\mu\) and \(\hat v\).
\item
  Does the weighting matrix affect this exactly identified estimator? Explain.
\item
  Suppose the distribution is also assumed symmetric, so that \(\mathbb{E}[(Y_i-\mu)^3]=0\). Explain why the model is now overidentified.
\item
  How many degrees of freedom does the corresponding overidentification test have, and what does rejection mean?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Bayesian updating for a normal mean]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:estimation-bayesian-normal-mean}{}\label{exr:estimation-bayesian-normal-mean}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{secMLE}.

Conditional on \(\mu\), let \(Y_1,\dots,Y_n\) be i.i.d. \(\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^2)\), where \(\sigma^2\) is known. The prior is \(\mu\sim\mathcal{N}(m_0,v_0)\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Show that the posterior distribution is \(\mathcal{N}(m_n,v_n)\), where
  \[
  v_n=\left(\frac1{v_0}+\frac n{\sigma^2}\right)^{-1},
  \qquad
  m_n=v_n\left(\frac{m_0}{v_0}+\frac{n\bar Y_n}{\sigma^2}\right).
  \]
\item
  Interpret \(m_n\) as a precision-weighted average of the prior mean and sample mean.
\item
  Compute the posterior mean and variance when \(m_0=0\), \(v_0=1\), \(\sigma^2=4\), \(n=4\), and \(\bar Y_n=3\).
\item
  Give a central approximate 95\% posterior credible interval for \(\mu\) using 1.96 as the normal critical value.
\item
  Derive the posterior predictive distribution of a new observation \(Y_{n+1}\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\chapter{Binary-choice models}\label{binary-choice-models}

\textbf{Prerequisites.} Conditional probability, Bernoulli variables, linear regression, and maximum-likelihood estimation.

\textbf{After working through this chapter, you should be able to:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Explain why a linear probability model has heteroskedastic disturbances and potentially inadmissible fitted values.
\item
  Interpret probit and logit models through conditional probabilities and latent variables.
\item
  Write and maximize the likelihood of a binary-choice model.
\item
  Compute and interpret marginal effects.
\item
  Compare models using likelihood-based measures and assess predictions with classification rates and ROC curves.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Roadmap.} We start from the linear probability model and then introduce nonlinear probability functions. A latent-variable representation motivates probit and logit specifications. The remaining sections cover estimation, marginal effects, goodness of fit, and predictive evaluation before the exercises in Section \ref{binary-exercises}.

Many economic outcomes are qualitative rather than continuous: a household defaults or does not, an individual participates or does not, and a firm enters a market or stays out. When \(y_i\in\{0,1\}\), the object to model is the conditional probability \(\mathbb{P}(y_i=1\mid\mathbf{x}_i)\). The covariates are collected in a \(K\times1\) vector \(\mathbf{x}_i\).

Examples include participation in the labour force, market entry, and the occurrence of a credit default. A binary outcome need not represent a deliberate choice.

We specify a Bernoulli model:
\begin{equation}
y_i | \mathbf{X} \sim \mathcal{B}(g(\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta)).
\end{equation}
where \(g(\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta)\) is the parameter of the Bernoulli distribution. In other words, conditionally on \(\mathbf{X}\):
\begin{equation}
y_i = \left\{
\begin{array}{cl}
1 & \mbox{ with probability } g(\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta)\\
0 & \mbox{ with probability } 1-g(\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta),
\end{array}
\right.\label{eq:genericBinary}
\end{equation}
where \(\boldsymbol\theta\) is a vector of parameters to be estimated.

An estimation strategy is to assume that \(g(\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta)\) can be proxied by \(\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}'\mathbf{x}_i\) and to run a linear regression to estimate \(\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}\) (a situation called \textbf{Linear Probability Model, LPM}):
\[
y_i = \tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}'\mathbf{x}_i + \varepsilon_i.
\]
If the conditional probability is truly linear, OLS consistently estimates its coefficients; if it is nonlinear, OLS instead estimates the coefficients of the best linear projection. The fitted values can fall below zero or above one. Moreover, because \(\mathbb{V}ar(y_i\mid\mathbf{x}_i)=p_i(1-p_i)\), the disturbances are generally heteroskedastic, so heteroskedasticity-robust standard errors should be used. The disturbances cannot be conditionally Gaussian because \(y_i\in\{0,1\}\).

Figure \ref{fig:LPM} uses simulated observations to compare the linear fit with the probability function that generated the data. A straight line cannot flatten towards both zero and one.

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/LPM-1} \caption{A linear fit and the true probability in a simulated sample of 100 observations. The generating probability is $\Phi(0.5+2x)$, with standard normal covariates.}\label{fig:LPM}
\end{figure}

Nonlinear models instead map an index \(z_i=\boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x}_i\) into a probability \(p_i=g(z_i)\). Table \ref{tab:foo} gives common choices. Here \(\Phi\) and \(\phi\) denote the standard normal distribution and density functions.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\caption{\label{tab:foo} Probability functions and their derivatives. The linear specification is included for comparison.}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
Model & \(g(z)\) & \(g'(z)\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
Model & \(g(z)\) & \(g'(z)\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Probit & \(\Phi(z)\) & \(\phi(z)\) \\
Logit & \([1+\exp(-z)]^{-1}\) & \(g(z)[1-g(z)]\) \\
Complementary log-log & \(1-\exp[-\exp(z)]\) & \(\exp[z-\exp(z)]\) \\
Linear (LPM) & \(z\) & \(1\) \\
\end{longtable}

The \textbf{probit} and \textbf{logit} models use, respectively,
\begin{equation}
g(z)=\Phi(z), \label{eq:probit}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
g(z)=\frac{1}{1+\exp(-z)}. \label{eq:logit}
\end{equation}
Both keep probabilities between zero and one. Their coefficients have different scales, so comparisons should focus on predicted probabilities and marginal effects. The complementary log-log link is asymmetric (Figure \ref{fig:ProbLogit}).

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/ProbLogit-1} \caption{Three links from an index to a probability. Probit and logit are symmetric around zero; the complementary log-log link is asymmetric.}\label{fig:ProbLogit}
\end{figure}

\section{Latent-variable and utility interpretations}\label{latent}

Probability functions specify how covariates map into observed choices. A latent-variable representation supplies an economic interpretation: the observed binary outcome records whether an unobserved index crosses a threshold.

The probit model has an interpretation in terms of latent variables, which, in turn, is often exploited in structural models, called \textbf{Random Utility Models (RUM)}. In such structural models, it is assumed that the agents that have to take the decision do so by selecting the outcome that provides them with the larger utility (for agent \(i\), two possible outcomes: \(y_i=0\) or \(y_i=1\)). Part of this utility is observed by the econometrician ---it depends on the covariates \(\mathbf{x}_i\)--- and part of it is latent.

In the probit model, we have:
\[
\mathbb{P}(y_i=1|\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta) = \Phi(\boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x}_i) = \mathbb{P}(-\varepsilon_{i}<\boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x}_i),
\]
where \(\varepsilon_{i} \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)\). That is:
\[
\mathbb{P}(y_i=1|\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta) = \mathbb{P}(0< y_i^*),
\]
where \(y_i^* = \boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x}_i + \varepsilon_i\), with \(\varepsilon_{i} \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)\). Variable \(y_i^*\) can be interpreted as a (latent) variable that determines \(y_i\) (since \(y_i = \mathbb{I}_{\{y_i^*>0\}}\)).

Figure \ref{fig:Latent} illustrates this situation.

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/Latent-1} \caption{A latent normal index with mean one and variance one. The area to the right of zero is the probability of observing outcome one.}\label{fig:Latent}
\end{figure}

Assume that agent (\(i\)) chooses \(y_i=1\) if the utility associated with this choice (\(U_{i,1}\)) is higher than the one associated with \(y_i=0\) (that is \(U_{i,0}\)). Assume further that the utility of agent \(i\), if she chooses outcome \(j\) (\(\in \{0,1\}\)), is given by
\[
U_{i,j} = V_{i,j} + \varepsilon_{i,j},
\]
where \(V_{i,j}\) is the deterministic component of the utility associated with choice and where \(\varepsilon_{i,j}\) is a random (agent-specific) component. Moreover, posit \(V_{i,j} = \boldsymbol\theta_j'\mathbf{x}_i\). We then have:
\begin{eqnarray}
\mathbb{P}(y_i = 1|\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta) &=& \mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol\theta_1'\mathbf{x}_i+\varepsilon_{i,1}>\boldsymbol\theta_0'\mathbf{x}_i+\varepsilon_{i,0}) \nonumber\\
&=& F(\boldsymbol\theta_1'\mathbf{x}_i-\boldsymbol\theta_0'\mathbf{x}_i) = F([\boldsymbol\theta_1-\boldsymbol\theta_0]'\mathbf{x}_i),\label{eq:utility}
\end{eqnarray}
where \(F\) is the c.d.f. of \(\varepsilon_{i,0}-\varepsilon_{i,1}\).

Note that only the difference \(\boldsymbol\theta_1-\boldsymbol\theta_0\) is identifiable (as opposed to \(\boldsymbol\theta_1\) \emph{and} \(\boldsymbol\theta_0\)). Moreover, replacing \(U\) with \(aU\) (\(a>0\)) gives the same model; this \emph{scaling} issue can be solved by fixing the variance of \(\varepsilon_{i,0}-\varepsilon_{i,1}\).

\begin{example}[Migration and income]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:migration}{}\label{exm:migration}The RUM approach has been used by \citet{Nakosteen_Zimmer_1980} to study migration choices. Their model is based on the comparison of marginal costs and benefits associated with migration. The main ingredients of their approach are as follows:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Wage that can be earned at the present location: \(y_p^* = \boldsymbol\theta_p'\mathbf{x}_p + \varepsilon_p\).
\item
  Migration cost: \(C^*= \boldsymbol\theta_c'\mathbf{x}_c + \varepsilon_c\).
\item
  Wage earned elsewhere: \(y_m^* = \boldsymbol\theta_m'\mathbf{x}_m + \varepsilon_m\).
\end{itemize}

In this context, agents decide to migrate if \(y_m^* >  y_p^* + C^*\), i.e.~if
\[
y^* = y_m^* -  y_p^* - C^* =  \boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x} + \underbrace{\varepsilon}_{=\varepsilon_m - \varepsilon_c - \varepsilon_p}>0,
\]
where \(\mathbf{x}\) is the union of the \(\mathbf{x}_i\)s, for \(i \in \{p,m,c\}\).
\end{example}

\section{Alternative-varying regressors}\label{Avregressors}

When attributes such as fares and travel times vary across alternatives, specify utility for each available choice.

For instance, let \(\mathbf{u}_{i,j}\) collect the fare and travel time of alternative \(j\) for traveller \(i\), while \(\mathbf{v}_i\) collects characteristics of the traveller. A specification with common coefficients on alternative attributes is
\[
V_{i,j}={\theta^{(u)}}'\mathbf{u}_{i,j}+{\theta_j^{(v)}}'\mathbf{v}_i.
\]
Taking the difference between utilities gives
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{P}(y_i=1\mid\mathbf{x}_i)=F\left({\theta^{(u)}}'(\mathbf{u}_{i,1}-\mathbf{u}_{i,0})+
[\theta_1^{(v)}-\theta_0^{(v)}]'\mathbf{v}_i\right). \label{eq:utility3}
\end{equation}
Thus it is the difference in attributes that enters the binary model.

\begin{example}[Train or bus?]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:transport-binary}{}\label{exm:transport-binary}Consider a hypothetical journey with the following options:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Attribute & Train (\(j=1\)) & Bus (\(j=0\)) & Train minus bus \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Fare (CHF) & 12 & 8 & 4 \\
Travel time (minutes) & 25 & 40 & \(-15\) \\
\end{longtable}

A logit specification might assign the train probability
\[
\mathbb{P}(y_i=1)=\Lambda\big(\alpha+\beta_c(c_{i,1}-c_{i,0})+
\beta_t(t_{i,1}-t_{i,0})\big),
\]
where \(\Lambda\) is the logistic distribution function. For this journey the index is \(\alpha+4\beta_c-15\beta_t\). With negative fare and time coefficients, the higher fare lowers the train probability while the shorter journey raises it. These are illustrative attributes, not an estimated model. Estimation would require observations on choices and on the attributes of \textbf{both} available alternatives.
\end{example}

\section{Estimation}\label{binary-estimation}

Once the probability function has been specified, its parameters can be estimated by maximum likelihood. Each observation contributes the probability assigned to the outcome that actually occurred.

Condition on the covariates and assume independent outcomes across entities. Write \(p_i=g(\boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x}_i)\). The conditional probability of the observed outcome is \(p_i^{y_i}(1-p_i)^{1-y_i}\), giving the log-likelihood
\[
\ell(\boldsymbol\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^n
\left[y_i\log p_i+(1-y_i)\log(1-p_i)\right].
\]
Its score is
\[
\frac{\partial\ell}{\partial\boldsymbol\theta}
=\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbf{x}_i\frac{(y_i-p_i)g'(\boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x}_i)}{p_i(1-p_i)}.
\]
Numerical maximization finds the coefficient vector for which this score is zero. In the logit model, \(g'=p_i(1-p_i)\), so the score simplifies to \(\mathbf{X}'(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{p})\) and the Hessian is
\[
\frac{\partial^2\ell}{\partial\boldsymbol\theta\partial\boldsymbol\theta'}
=-\sum_{i=1}^n p_i(1-p_i)\mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}_i'.
\]
The log-likelihood is concave. Full column rank gives strict concavity at finite coefficients, but a finite maximum may fail to exist when regressors perfectly separate the two outcomes.

Under the regularity conditions of Section \ref{secMLE}, the MLE is approximately normal with covariance equal to the inverse information matrix. For either link, the conditional expected information is
\[
\mathbf{I}(\boldsymbol\theta\mid\mathbf{X})=
\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{[g'(\boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x}_i)]^2}{p_i(1-p_i)}
\mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}_i'.
\]
Evaluating its inverse at the estimates gives an estimated covariance matrix under the specified Bernoulli model.

\begin{example}[Predicting next-month credit defaults]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:creditProbit}{}\label{exm:creditProbit}We return to \texttt{AEC::UCIcredit}, described in Appendix \ref{data-credit}. Each of the 30,000 rows records one cardholder and whether a default payment was recorded in the following month. We ask whether the recorded credit limit, age, and latest repayment status help predict this outcome.

We use three predictors: the log of the credit limit (measured relative to NTD 100,000), age in decades relative to age 40, and an indicator for a \textbf{recorded payment delay} in September (\texttt{PAY\_0\ \textgreater{}=\ 1}). The credit limit is the amount of credit available on the card, not income or the outstanding balance. The comparison group for the delay indicator includes all other repayment codes; some of these codes are undocumented, so it should not be described as a group with no repayment problems.

We randomly reserve 9,000 cardholders for evaluation and estimate all models on the remaining 21,000. The reserved outcomes are used only in Section \ref{binary-predictions}. This split evaluates predictions for other clients in the same collection of observations.

The same three predictors enter the logit and probit specifications. Table \ref{tab:credit-binary-coefficients} reports their estimates and standard errors. A positive coefficient raises the predicted probability, holding the other predictors fixed; coefficient magnitudes across links are not directly comparable.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{\label{tab:credit-binary-coefficients}Credit-default models: estimates and standard errors (21,000 training observations).}
\centering
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}[t]{lrrrr}
\toprule
Predictor & Logit & SE logit & Probit & SE probit\\
\midrule
Constant & -1.779 & 0.025 & -1.055 & 0.013\\
Log credit limit & -0.334 & 0.019 & -0.191 & 0.011\\
Age (decades) & 0.085 & 0.019 & 0.049 & 0.011\\
Recorded delay & 1.786 & 0.038 & 1.059 & 0.022\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

The delay coefficient is positive in both models. The following sections translate this association into probability differences and assess whether it improves predictions.
\end{example}

\section{Marginal effects}\label{marginalFX}

The coefficients of probit and logit models affect a latent index, so they are not themselves changes in probability. Marginal effects translate coefficient estimates back into the probability scale and generally depend on the covariate values.

How to measure marginal effects, i.e.~the effect on the probability that \(y_i=1\) of a marginal increase of \(x_{i,k}\)? This object is given by:
\[
\frac{\partial \mathbb{P}(y_i=1|\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol\theta)}{\partial x_{i,k}} = \underbrace{g'(\boldsymbol\theta'\mathbf{x}_i)}_{>0}\theta_k,
\]
which is of the same sign as \(\theta_k\) if function \(g\) is monotonously increasing.

This derivative applies when \(x_{i,k}\) enters the index linearly, without transformations or interactions. Two summaries are common: evaluate it at the mean covariate vector (\textbf{marginal effect at the means}), or average the individual derivatives (\textbf{average marginal effect}, AME). The latter keeps each observation's covariate combination; the former can describe an artificial profile, especially with categorical predictors.

For a binary predictor, use a finite difference instead: predict each observation once with that predictor set to one and once with it set to zero, holding the other predictors fixed, then average the differences. For a transformed continuous predictor, recompute its transformations when changing the underlying variable.

\begin{example}[From coefficients to probabilities]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:credit-binary-effects}{}\label{exm:credit-binary-effects}

In our credit model, the AME of \texttt{age10} is the average link derivative times its coefficient. It is a local slope per decade. For the credit limit we instead compare probabilities before and after a 10\% increase, which adds \(\log(1.1)\) to \texttt{log\_limit}. For the delay indicator we compare its two possible values. All three summaries average over the training sample and describe model-implied associations.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{\label{tab:credit-binary-effects}Average changes in predicted default probability (percentage points).}
\centering
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}[t]{lrr}
\toprule
Comparison & Logit & Probit\\
\midrule
Age: local slope per decade & 1.23 & 1.24\\
Credit limit: +10\% & -0.45 & -0.46\\
Recorded delay: 0 to 1 & 34.14 & 34.31\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

For example, the logit comparison for recorded delay is about 34.1 percentage points. A 10\% higher credit limit corresponds to a much smaller change, about -0.45 percentage points.

A percentage-point change is a difference between probabilities multiplied by 100; it is not a percentage change in risk. The large contrast for recorded delay summarizes a strong predictive association, rather than the effect of an intervention on repayment behaviour.

Figure \ref{fig:credit-binary-profiles} illustrates why a single coefficient does not imply a constant probability difference. It holds age at 40 and varies the credit limit and delay indicator.

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/credit-binary-profiles-1} \caption{Logit predictions at age 40. Curves differ only in the recorded-delay indicator; the comparison group includes all other repayment codes.}\label{fig:credit-binary-profiles}
\end{figure}

\end{example}

\section{Goodness of fit}\label{binary-fit}

After interpreting individual coefficients, we turn to the performance of the model as a whole. For binary outcomes, fit can be assessed through likelihood-based measures, calibration, and the quality of probability predictions.

McFadden's \textbf{pseudo-\(R^2\)} compares the maximized log-likelihood \(\widehat\ell\) with that of an intercept-only model, \(\widehat\ell_0\), on the same observations:
\[
R^2_{MF}=1-\frac{\widehat\ell}{\widehat\ell_0}.
\]
A value of zero means that the fitted model does no better in likelihood terms than the constant-probability benchmark. This measure is not a fraction of variance explained and does not evaluate predictions on new observations.

\begin{example}[Does repayment status add information?]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:credit-binary-fit}{}\label{exm:credit-binary-fit}We compare the intercept-only logit model, the model with credit limit and age, and the model that also includes recorded delay.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{\label{tab:credit-binary-fit}Within-sample fit of nested logit specifications.}
\centering
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}[t]{lrr}
\toprule
Specification & Log-likelihood & Pseudo-R2\\
\midrule
Constant only & -11005.3 & 0.000\\
Limit + age & -10679.9 & 0.030\\
Limit + age + delay & -9543.0 & 0.133\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

Adding recorded delay gives a likelihood-ratio statistic of 2273.7. Against the \(\chi^2_1\) reference distribution, the p-value is below 0.001: delay contributes information conditional on limit and age. Section \ref{binary-predictions} checks predictions on reserved observations.
\end{example}

\section{Predictions and ROC curves}\label{binary-predictions}

A predicted probability becomes a classification only after a threshold is chosen. ROC curves summarize the trade-off between true- and false-positive rates across all possible thresholds, separating the model's ranking ability from any single decision rule.

To classify an outcome, choose a threshold \(c\) and set \(\widehat y_i=1\) when \(\widehat p_i\geq c\). A lower threshold detects more positive outcomes but also produces more false alarms. The appropriate trade-off depends on the consequences of the two mistakes.

The \textbf{receiver operating characteristic (ROC)} curve plots the true-positive rate against the false-positive rate as the threshold varies:
\[
\mathrm{TPR}=\frac{\text{correctly flagged positives}}{\text{all positives}},
\qquad
\mathrm{FPR}=\frac{\text{incorrectly flagged negatives}}{\text{all negatives}}.
\]
A threshold above every score gives \((0,0)\); one below every score gives \((1,1)\). Random ranking has a diagonal ROC in expectation. The \textbf{area under the curve (AUC)} measures ranking quality: it is the probability that a randomly selected positive receives a higher score than a randomly selected negative, counting ties as one half.

Ranking is only one aspect of a probability forecast. \textbf{Calibration} asks whether outcomes occur at the predicted frequencies: among observations assigned a probability near 0.2, approximately one fifth should have outcome one. The \textbf{Brier score}, \(n^{-1}\sum_i(y_i-\widehat p_i)^2\), assesses probability accuracy; smaller values are better.

\begin{example}[Evaluating predictions for reserved cardholders]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:credit-binary-predictions}{}\label{exm:credit-binary-predictions}We now predict outcomes for the 9,000 cardholders excluded from estimation. The benchmark assigns every client the default share in the training sample. None of these models is refitted on the evaluation sample.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{\label{tab:credit-binary-predictions}Probability accuracy and ranking on 9,000 reserved observations.}
\centering
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}[t]{lrr}
\toprule
Model & Brier & AUC\\
\midrule
Benchmark & 0.177 & 0.500\\
Logit & 0.152 & 0.723\\
Probit & 0.152 & 0.723\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

Both fitted models reduce the Brier score relative to the constant-probability benchmark. Their AUC is about 0.72: they rank defaults above non-defaults more often than chance, while leaving substantial uncertainty. The two links give similar predictive performance. Their ROC curves and the logit's calibration plot appear in Figure \ref{fig:credit-binary-validation}. A good ranking does not guarantee calibration: the left panel compares clients with one another, while the right panel compares probabilities with observed frequencies.

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/credit-binary-validation-1} \caption{Evaluation on reserved observations. Left: ROC curves for logit and probit. Right: observed default frequencies against average logit predictions in ten equally sized groups ranked by predicted probability. The diagonal represents perfect calibration.}\label{fig:credit-binary-validation}
\end{figure}

Finally, consider two illustrative logit thresholds, 0.5 and 0.2. They are specified for comparison, rather than selected to maximize performance on the reserved outcomes.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{\label{tab:credit-binary-classification}Logit classifications: true positives (TP), false positives (FP), false negatives (FN), and true negatives (TN).}
\centering
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}[t]{rrrrrrr}
\toprule
Threshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & TPR & FPR\\
\midrule
0.5 & 583 & 449 & 1480 & 6488 & 0.283 & 0.065\\
0.2 & 1164 & 1424 & 899 & 5513 & 0.564 & 0.205\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

Lowering the threshold detects more defaults and flags more clients who do not default. Overall classification accuracy alone can be misleading: predicting no default for everyone would already be correct for 77.1\% of these clients, while detecting none of the defaults. The table makes both types of error visible.
\end{example}

\Needspace{14\baselineskip}

\section{Chapter recap}\label{binary-recap}

\begin{recapbox}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The LPM can give predictions outside \([0,1]\) and requires robust inference. Probit and logit use bounded probability functions (Figure \ref{fig:LPM}).
\item
  Latent indices and utility differences motivate binary-choice models; alternative-specific attributes enter through utility comparisons (Sections \ref{latent} and \ref{Avregressors}).
\item
  Maximum likelihood estimates index coefficients. Derivatives and finite differences translate them into probability comparisons (Sections \ref{binary-estimation} and \ref{marginalFX}).
\item
  Within-sample fit, ranking, calibration and classification answer different questions. Reserved observations assess predictive performance (Sections \ref{binary-fit} and \ref{binary-predictions}).
\end{itemize}

\end{recapbox}

\clearpage

\section{Exercises}\label{binary-exercises}

Attempt each question and explain your reasoning, including for true-or-false statements. \textbf{Foundation} exercises apply a result directly; \textbf{Standard} exercises require several steps or a short proof; \textbf{Advanced} exercises combine several results or require a longer derivation. Worked solutions and exercise-specific hints are reserved for teaching sessions and are not included in this student edition.

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Ordered-probit model]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:binary-multinomial-choice}{}\label{exr:binary-multinomial-choice}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} Analytical}\\
Review Section \ref{latent}.

Let \(y_i\in\{1,\dots,Z\}\) be an ordered outcome observed for entities \(i=1,\dots,n\). Each entity has a \(K\)-dimensional covariate vector \(\mathbf{x}_i\). Assume that \((\mathbf{x}_i,y_i)\) and \((\mathbf{x}_j,y_j)\) are independent whenever \(i\ne j\).

To avoid a redundant location parameter, assume that \(\mathbf{x}_i\) does not contain a constant.

For each entity \(i\), the probability that \(y_i = k\) (with \(k \in \{1, \dots, Z\}\)) is a function of \(\mathbf{x}_i\). Specifically, there exist functions \(g_1, g_2, \dots, g_Z\) that are such that:

\[\mathbb{P}(y_i = k | \mathbf{x}_i) = g_k(\boldsymbol{\beta}' \mathbf{x}_i),\]

where \(\boldsymbol{\beta}\) is a \(K\)-dimensional vector.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Provide examples of situations where a variable of interest \(y_i\) can take a finite number of values.
\item
  Provide an example of a situation where the assumption according to which the \((\mathbf{x}_i, y_i)\) are independent across entities \(i\) would not be satisfied.
\item
  What is the relationship that should be satisfied by \(g_1(x), g_2(x), \dots, g_Z(x)\)?
\end{enumerate}

Introduce the latent variable \(y_i^*\):

\[y_i^* = \boldsymbol{\beta}'\mathbf{x}_i + \varepsilon_i,\]

where \(\varepsilon_i \sim i.i.d. \mathcal{N}(0,1)\). Define \(\alpha_j\), for \(j \in \{1, \dots, Z-1\}\), such that \(\alpha_1 < \alpha_2 < \dots < \alpha_{Z-1}\). We also employ the notation \(\alpha_0 = -\infty\) and \(\alpha_Z = +\infty\). The observed outcome is linked to the latent variable by \(y_i=k\) if and only if \(\alpha_{k-1}<y_i^*\leq\alpha_k\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{3}
\item
  Using \(\Phi\) (the c.d.f. of the standard normal distribution), express \(\mathbb{P}(y_i^* \in ]\alpha_{k-1}, \alpha_k[ | \mathbf{x}_i)\), for \(k \in \{1, \dots, Z\}\).
\item
  Show that the \(\psi_k\) functions defined, for \(k \in \{1, \dots, Z\}\), by:

  \[\psi_k: \quad x \to \mathbb{P}(y_i^* \in ]\alpha_{k-1}, \alpha_k[ | \boldsymbol{\beta}' \mathbf{x}_i = x),\]

  satisfy the relationship obtained in question 3.
\item
  Let \(\boldsymbol{\theta}\) denote the vector of parameters defining the model, that is \(\boldsymbol{\theta} = [\boldsymbol{\beta}', \alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_{Z-1}]'\). Using \(\Phi\), express \(f(y_i | \mathbf{x}_i; \boldsymbol{\theta})\), the distribution of \(y_i\) conditional on \(\mathbf{x}_i\). Deduce the expression of the log-likelihood \(\log \mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\theta}; \mathbf{y})\) associated with \(\mathbf{y} = \{y_1, \dots, y_n\}\) (conditional on \(\mathbf{x} = \{\mathbf{x}_1, \dots, \mathbf{x}_n\}\)).
\item
  Explain how one could obtain estimates of \(\boldsymbol{\theta}\) (denoted by \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\)) and how one could estimate the standard deviations associated with \(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\).
\end{enumerate}

Suppose that one has estimated two versions of such a model. In the first version (Model 1), vectors \(\mathbf{x}_i\) are of dimension 3; in the second model (Model 2), two potential explanatory variables are added, in such a way that the \(\mathbf{x}_i\) are of dimension 5. While the maximum log-likelihood associated with Model 1 is \(\max \log \mathcal{L}_1 = -518.3\), the one associated with Model 2 is \(\max \log \mathcal{L}_2 = -517.1\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{7}
\item
  Explain why it is not possible to have \(\max \log \mathcal{L}_2 < \max \log \mathcal{L}_1\).
\item
  Using a \(\chi^2\) distribution table, what can you say about these two additional explanatory variables?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Comparing probit specifications: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:binary-probit-models-true-false}{}\label{exr:binary-probit-models-true-false}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{marginalFX}.

A binary choice model is estimated. There are \(n\) observations of the dependent binary variable \(y_i\) (i.e., each \(y_i\) is either 0 or 1). For each entity \(i\), there are 6 explanatory variables: \(\mathbf{x}_i = [x_{i,1}, \dots, x_{i,6}]'\), with \(x_{i,1} = 1\) (thus using a constant). The model is:

\[\mathbb{P}(y_i = 1 | \mathbf{x}_i; \boldsymbol{\theta}) = \Phi(\boldsymbol{\theta}'\mathbf{x}_i)\]

where \(\Phi\) is the cumulative distribution function (c.d.f.) of \(\mathcal{N}(0, 1)\). We have \(\boldsymbol{\theta} = [\theta_1, \dots, \theta_6]'\).

Three versions of the model are considered, all estimated by maximum likelihood:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Version A:} Includes all 6 explanatory variables. The maximum log-likelihood value obtained is \(-120\).
\item
  \textbf{Version B:} Removes 3 explanatory variables by imposing \(\theta_4 = \theta_5 = \theta_6 = 0\). The maximum log-likelihood value obtained is \(-125\).
\item
  \textbf{Version C:} Uses only a constant. This is equivalent to imposing \(\theta_2 = \theta_3 = \theta_4 = \theta_5 = \theta_6 = 0\). The maximum log-likelihood value obtained is \(-160\).
\end{itemize}

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  In this model, we have \(\mathbb{P}(y_i = 1 | \mathbf{x}_i; \boldsymbol{\theta}) = \mathbb{P}(y_i^* > 0 | \mathbf{x}_i; \boldsymbol{\theta})\), where \(y_i^* = \boldsymbol{\theta}'\mathbf{x}_i + \varepsilon_i\), with \(\varepsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)\).
\item
  The McFadden pseudo-\(R^2\) associated with model A is 40\%.
\item
  A likelihood ratio test (LR test) is used to test the restrictions when moving from model A to model B. The test leads to rejecting the restricted model (model B) at the 1\% level (i.e., for a test size of 1\%).
\item
  The McFadden pseudo-\(R^2\) associated with model B is greater than that associated with model A.
\item
  If \(\theta_2 > 0\), then an increase in \(x_{i,2}\) by one unit (all else equal) implies an increase in \(\mathbb{P}(y_i = 1 | \mathbf{x}_i; \boldsymbol{\theta})\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Probit maximum likelihood: true or false?]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:binary-probit-mle-true-false}{}\label{exr:binary-probit-mle-true-false}

\textbf{Advanced \textbar{} True or false}\\
Review Section \ref{marginalFX}.

Consider a random variable whose observations \(y_i\) are independent and drawn from a Bernoulli distribution with parameter \(\Phi(\boldsymbol{\theta}'\mathbf{x}_i)\), where \(\Phi\) is the c.d.f. of a standard normal distribution \(\mathcal{N}(0,1)\).

The explanatory variables \(\mathbf{x}_i=[x_{i,1},\dots,x_{i,K}]'\) are regarded as non-random variables. The parameter vector \(\boldsymbol{\theta}=[\theta_1,\dots,\theta_K]'\) is estimated by Maximum Likelihood. The number of observations is large.

For each statement, decide whether it is true or false and justify your answer.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  The conditional variance of \(y_i\) given \(\mathbf{x}_i\) is strictly larger than 1.
\item
  If \(\theta_1>0\), an increase in \(x_{i,1}\) (everything else equal) necessarily leads to a decrease in \(\mathbb{P}(y_i=1|\mathbf{x}_i)\).
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(y_i|\mathbf{x}_i;\boldsymbol{\theta})=\Phi(\boldsymbol{\theta}'\mathbf{x}_i)\).
\item
  By adding three explanatory variables (\(x_{i,K+1}\), \(x_{i,K+2}\), and \(x_{i,K+3}\)), the maximum log-likelihood increases by 2.5. A likelihood ratio test at the 5\% significance level leads us to reject the null hypothesis that \(\theta_{K+1}=\theta_{K+2}=\theta_{K+3}=0\).
\item
  This model is a probit model.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\begin{exercise}[Marginal effects in a probit model]
\protect\hypertarget{exr:binary-marginal-effects}{}\label{exr:binary-marginal-effects}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{marginalFX}.

Consider
\[
\mathbb{P}(Y_i=1\mid x_i)=\Phi(\theta_0+\theta_1x_i),
\]
where \(x_i\) is continuous and \(\Phi\) and \(\phi\) are the standard-normal c.d.f. and p.d.f.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Derive the marginal effect of \(x_i\) on the success probability.
\item
  Evaluate it at \(x_i=2\) when \(\theta_0=-1\) and \(\theta_1=0.5\).
\item
  Does \(\theta_1=0.5\) mean that increasing \(x_i\) by one unit always raises the probability by 0.5? Explain.
\item
  Compare the marginal effect at the mean with the average marginal effect.
\item
  If \(x_i\) were binary, what quantity should replace the derivative?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\end{exercisebox}

\Needspace{16\baselineskip}

\begin{exercisebox}

\protect\hypertarget{exr:binary-classification-roc}{}\label{exr:binary-classification-roc}

\textbf{Standard \textbar{} Calculation}\\
Review Section \ref{binary-predictions}.

For six observations, a fitted binary-choice model gives:

Classify an observation as positive when \(\hat p_i\ge c\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  For \(c=0.50\), compute the numbers of true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives.
\item
  Compute the true-positive rate, false-positive rate, and accuracy.
\item
  Repeat the true-positive and false-positive rates for \(c=0.25\).
\item

\chapter{Time Series}\label{TS}

\textbf{Prerequisites.} Expectations, variances and covariances, the central limit theorem, linear regression, and basic matrix algebra.

\textbf{After working through this chapter, you should be able to:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Distinguish white noise, martingale differences, strict stationarity, and covariance stationarity.
\item
  Compute autocovariances and long-run variances.
\item
  Determine whether AR and MA representations are stable and causal.
\item
  Use autocorrelation and partial autocorrelation for model identification.
\item
  Construct point forecasts and forecast-error variances.
\item
  Evaluate forecasts in and out of sample.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Roadmap.} The chapter first develops the probabilistic language needed for time-indexed data. It then studies moving-average and autoregressive processes, including their identification, before turning to forecasting and forecast evaluation. Section \ref{time-series-exercises} provides analytical applications.

\section{Introduction to time series}\label{introduction-to-time-series}

Our running application is the monthly number of recorded injury accidents in Zurich. Chapter 4 separated a time trend from calendar-month effects; here we study dependence between successive months and use past observations to forecast new ones. Appendix \ref{data-accidents} describes the data.

A time series is a sequence of random variables indexed by time: \(\{y_t\}_{t=-\infty}^{+\infty}=\{\dots,y_{-1},y_0,y_1,\dots\}\), with \(y_t\in\mathbb{R}^k\). In practice, we observe only a finite stretch, typically \(\{y_1,\dots,y_T\}\). Unlike a random sample, these observations may be dependent across dates, so the order in which they occur contains information.

Time-series models describe current observations in terms of past information and new \textbf{shocks}, usually denoted by \(\varepsilon_t\). Although shocks are sometimes assumed i.i.d., many results need only weaker restrictions. We begin with white noise and martingale differences, then introduce the lag operator and stationarity.

\begin{definition}[White noise]
\protect\hypertarget{def:whitenoise}{}\label{def:whitenoise}

The process \(\{\varepsilon_t\}_{t \in] -\infty,+\infty[}\) is a white noise if, for all \(t\):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\alph{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_t)=0\),
\item
  \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_t^2)=\sigma^2<\infty\) and
\item
  for all \(s\ne t\), \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_t \varepsilon_s)=0\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{definition}

A stronger restriction concerns what can be predicted from past information:

\begin{definition}[Martingale Difference Sequence]
\protect\hypertarget{def:MDS}{}\label{def:MDS}The process \(\{\varepsilon_t\}_{t = -\infty}^{+\infty}\) is a martingale difference sequence (MDS) if \(\mathbb{E}(|\varepsilon_{t}|)<\infty\) and if, for all \(t\),
\[
\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{t-1}(\varepsilon_{t})}_{\mbox{Expectation conditional on the past}}=0.
\]
\end{definition}

Here the information set must be specified; unless stated otherwise, it contains the history of the process. An MDS with constant finite unconditional variance is white noise, but its conditional variance can change over time.

By definition, if \(y_t\) is a martingale, then \(y_{t}-y_{t-1}\) is a MDS.

\begin{example}[ARCH process]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:ARCH}{}\label{exm:ARCH}The Autoregressive conditional heteroskedasticity (ARCH) process is an example of shock that satisfies the MDS definition but that is not i.i.d.:
\[
\varepsilon_{t} = \sigma_t \times z_{t},
\]
where \(z_t \sim i.i.d.\,\mathcal{N}(0,1)\), \(w>0\), \(\alpha\ge0\), and \(\sigma_t^2 = w + \alpha \varepsilon_{t-1}^2\). A finite, time-invariant unconditional variance additionally requires \(\alpha<1\).
\end{example}

\begin{example}
\protect\hypertarget{exm:whiteNotMDS}{}\label{exm:whiteNotMDS}A (weak) white noise process is not necessarily a MDS. This is for instance the following process:
\[
\varepsilon_{t} = z_t + z_{t-1}z_{t-2},
\]
where \(z_t \sim i.i.d.\mathcal{N}(0,1)\). Its autocovariances vanish at nonzero lags, yet \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_t\varepsilon_{t-1}\varepsilon_{t-2})=1\). It therefore cannot have zero conditional mean given its own past.
\end{example}

In the following, to simplify the exposition, we will essentially consider \textbf{strong white noises}. A strong white noise is a particular case of white noise where the \(\varepsilon_{t}\)'s are serially independent. Using the law of iterated expectation, it can be shown that a strong white noise is a martingale difference sequence. Indeed, when the \(\varepsilon_{t}\)'s are serially independent, we have that \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{t}|\varepsilon_{t-1},\varepsilon_{t-2},\dots)=\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{t})=0\).

Let us now introduce the lag operator. The lag operator, denoted by \(L\), is defined on the time series space and is defined by:
\begin{equation}
L: \{y_t\}_{t=-\infty}^{+\infty} \rightarrow \{w_t\}_{t=-\infty}^{+\infty} \quad \mbox{with} \quad w_t = y_{t-1}.\label{eq:lagOp}
\end{equation}

We have: \(L^2 y_t = y_{t-2}\) and, more generally, \(L^k y_t = y_{t-k}\).

Consider a time series \(y_t\) defined by \(y_t = c + \phi y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\), where the \(\varepsilon_t\)'s are i.i.d. \(\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2)\). Using the lag operator, the dynamics of \(y_t\) can be expressed as follows:
\[
(1-\phi L) y_t = c + \varepsilon_t.
\]

If it exists, the \textbf{unconditional (or marginal) mean} of the random variable \(y_t\) is given by:
\[
\mu_t := \mathbb{E}(y_t) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} y_t f_{Y_t}(y_t) dy_t,
\]
where \(f_{Y_t}\) is the unconditional (or marginal) density of \(y_t\). Similarly, if it exists, the \textbf{unconditional (or marginal) variance} of the random variable \(y_t\) is:
\[
\mathbb{V}ar(y_t) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} (y_t - \mathbb{E}(y_t))^2 f_{Y_t}(y_t) dy_t.
\]

\begin{definition}[Autocovariance]
\protect\hypertarget{def:autocov}{}\label{def:autocov}The lag-\(j\) autocovariance is
\[
\gamma_{j,t}=\mathbb{C}ov(y_t,y_{t-j})
=\mathbb{E}\big[(y_t-\mathbb{E}y_t)(y_{t-j}-\mathbb{E}y_{t-j})\big].
\]
It measures linear dependence between observations \(j\) periods apart. Unlike a correlation, it retains the squared units of the variable.
\end{definition}

In particular, \(\gamma_{0,t} = \mathbb{V}ar(y_t)\).

\begin{definition}[Covariance stationarity]
\protect\hypertarget{def:covstat}{}\label{def:covstat}The process \(y_t\) is covariance stationary ---or weakly stationary--- if, for all \(t\) and \(j\),
\[
\mathbb{E}(y_t) = \mu \quad \mbox{and} \quad \mathbb{E}\{(y_t - \mu)(y_{t-j} - \mu)\} = \gamma_j.
\]
\end{definition}

Figure \ref{fig:nonstat1} displays the simulation of a process that is not covariance stationary. This process follows \(y_t = 0.1t + \varepsilon_t\), where \(\varepsilon_t \sim\,i.i.d.\,\mathcal{N}(0,1)\). Indeed, for such a process, we have: \(\mathbb{E}(y_t)=0.1t\), which depends on \(t\).

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/nonstat1-1} \caption{A deterministic trend makes the mean change with time. The dashed line is the generating mean, $0.1t$.}\label{fig:nonstat1}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[Strict stationarity]
\protect\hypertarget{def:strictstat}{}\label{def:strictstat}The process \(y_t\) is strictly stationary if, for all \(t\) and all sets of integers \(J=\{j_1,\dots,j_n\}\), the distribution of \((y_{t},y_{t+j_1},\dots,y_{t+j_n})\) depends on \(J\) but not on \(t\).
\end{definition}

The following process is covariance stationary but not strictly stationary:
\[
y_t = \mathbb{I}_{\{t<1000\}}\varepsilon_{1,t}+\mathbb{I}_{\{t\ge1000\}}\varepsilon_{2,t},
\]
where \(\{\varepsilon_{1,t}\}\) and \(\{\varepsilon_{2,t}\}\) are independent i.i.d. sequences, \(\varepsilon_{1,t} \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)\), \(\varepsilon_{2,t} \sim \sqrt{\frac{\nu - 2}{\nu}} t(\nu)\), and \(\nu = 4\). Both regimes have mean zero and variance one, but their marginal distributions differ.

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/nonstat2-1} \caption{Equal means and variances do not imply equal distributions. From date 1,000, standardized Student shocks replace normal shocks. Dashed lines mark the central 99\% range of a standard normal variable, not a confidence interval for a mean.}\label{fig:nonstat2}
\end{figure}

\begin{proposition}
\protect\hypertarget{prp:gammaMinus}{}\label{prp:gammaMinus}If \(y_t\) is covariance stationary, then \(\gamma_j = \gamma_{-j}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(y_t\) is covariance stationary, the covariance between \(y_t\) and \(y_{t-j}\) (i.e \(\gamma_j\)) is the same as that between \(y_{t+j}\) and \(y_{t+j-j}\) (i.e.~\(\gamma_{-j}\)).
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Auto-correlation]
\protect\hypertarget{def:autocor}{}\label{def:autocor}The \(j^{th}\) auto-correlation of a covariance-stationary process is:
\[
\rho_j = \frac{\gamma_j}{\gamma_0}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Seasonality and dependence in Zurich accidents]
\protect\hypertarget{exm:zurich-time-patterns}{}\label{exm:zurich-time-patterns}

The packaged series \texttt{AEC::CHaccidents\_monthly} contains all 180 months from January 2011 to December 2025. We study \textbf{accidents involving injury}, including fatal accidents, rather than counts of injured people. Total accident counts include property-only incidents, whose reporting definition changed in 2015; we do not use that total here.

We initially use only January 2011--December 2022 (144 months) and reserve 2023--2025 for forecast evaluation. Figure \ref{fig:autocov} shows the initial sample and its calendar-month averages. There is a marked annual pattern; a constant-mean stationary model for the unadjusted counts would miss it.

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/autocov-1} \caption{Initial sample, 2011--2022. Left: monthly recorded injury accidents in Zurich. Right: averages for each calendar month, computed from these same 12 years.}\label{fig:autocov}
\end{figure}

To examine dependence beyond this regular pattern, define the \textbf{seasonal deviation}
\[
u_t=y_t-\widehat m_{s(t)},
\]
where \(s(t)\) is the calendar month and \(\widehat m_s\) its average in the initial sample. This is a simple seasonal adjustment, not proof that the remaining series is stationary. The scatterplots in Figure \ref{fig:autocov2} compare deviations at different lags: a positive deviation tends to follow another positive deviation, particularly at lag one.

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/autocov2-1} \caption{Seasonal deviations in the initial sample. Each point pairs two months one or three periods apart. The fitted line describes their sample association; its slope need not equal the sample ACF exactly because the endpoint samples differ.}\label{fig:autocov2}
\end{figure}

\end{example}

\begin{definition}[Mean ergodicity]
\protect\hypertarget{def:ergodicity}{}\label{def:ergodicity}The covariance-stationary process \(y_t\) is ergodic for the mean if:
\[
\mbox{plim}_{T \rightarrow +\infty} \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^T y_t = \mathbb{E}(y_t).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Second-moment ergodicity]
\protect\hypertarget{def:ergod2nd}{}\label{def:ergod2nd}The covariance-stationary process \(y_t\) is ergodic for second moments if, for all \(j\):
\[
\mbox{plim}_{T \rightarrow +\infty} \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^T (y_t-\mu) (y_{t-j}-\mu) = \gamma_j.
\]
\end{definition}

It should be noted that ergodicity and stationarity are different properties. Typically if the process \(\{x_t\}\) is such that, \(\forall t\), \(x_t \equiv y\), where \(y \sim\,\mathcal{N}(0,1)\) (say), then \(\{x_t\}\) is stationary but not ergodic.

\begin{theorem}[Sample mean of a weakly dependent stationary process]
\protect\hypertarget{thm:CLTcovstat}{}\label{thm:CLTcovstat}If process \(y_t\) is covariance stationary and the series of autocovariances is absolutely summable (\(\sum_{j=-\infty}^{+\infty} |\gamma_j| <\infty\)), then:
\begin{equation}
\bar{y}_T \overset{m.s.}{\rightarrow} \mu = \mathbb{E}(y_t) \label{eq:TCL20}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
\mbox{lim}_{T \rightarrow +\infty} T \mathbb{E}\left[(\bar{y}_T - \mu)^2\right] = \sum_{j=-\infty}^{+\infty} \gamma_j. \label{eq:TCL2}
\end{equation}

Covariance stationarity and summable autocovariances alone do \textbf{not} imply asymptotic normality. If the process additionally satisfies a suitable central-limit condition---for example, standard strong-mixing and moment conditions---then
\begin{equation}
\sqrt{T}(\bar{y}_T - \mu) \overset{d}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{N}\left(0,\sum_{j=-\infty}^{+\infty} \gamma_j \right). \label{eq:TCL4ts}
\end{equation}

{[}Mean square (m.s.) and distribution (d.) convergences: see Definitions \ref{def:cvgceDistri} and \ref{def:convergenceLr}.{]}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If \(T\mathbb E[(\bar y_T-\mu)^2]\) has a finite limit, then the mean squared error tends to zero, proving Eq. \eqref{eq:TCL20}. For Eq. \eqref{eq:TCL2}, see Appendix \ref{AppendixProof}. Equation \eqref{eq:TCL4ts} follows only after imposing an appropriate central-limit condition.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Long-run variance]
\protect\hypertarget{def:LRV}{}\label{def:LRV}Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:CLTcovstat}, the limit appearing in Eq. \eqref{eq:TCL2} exists and is called \textbf{long-run variance}. It is denoted by \(S\), i.e.:
\[
S = \sum_{j=-\infty}^{+\infty} \gamma_j  = \mbox{lim}_{T \rightarrow +\infty} T \mathbb{E}[(\bar{y}_T - \mu)^2].
\]
\end{definition}

If \(y_t\) is ergodic for second moments (see Def. \ref{def:ergod2nd}), a natural estimator of \(S\) is:
\begin{equation}
\hat\gamma_0 + 2 \sum_{\nu=1}^{q} \hat\gamma_\nu, \label{eq:covSmplMean}
\end{equation}
where \(\hat\gamma_\nu = \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=\nu+1}^{T} (y_t - \bar{y})(y_{t-\nu} - \bar{y})\).

However, for small samples, Eq. \eqref{eq:covSmplMean} can be negative (or, in the multivariate case, fail to be positive semidefinite). \citet{Newey_West_1987} proposed the following nonnegative estimator:
\begin{equation}
S^{NW}=\hat\gamma_0 + 2 \sum_{\nu=1}^{q}\left(1-\frac{\nu}{q+1}\right) \hat\gamma_\nu.\label{eq:NWest}
\end{equation}
For consistency, the bandwidth \(q=q_T\) must be chosen together with the sample size; standard sufficient conditions include \(q_T\rightarrow\infty\) and \(q_T/T\rightarrow0\), along with moment and weak-dependence conditions.

Theorem \ref{thm:CLTcovstat} implies that positive serial correlation reduces the precision of a sample mean for a given sample size. Figure \ref{fig:TVTCL} illustrates this point with three processes that have the same marginal variance but autocorrelations of 0\%, 70\%, and 99.9\%. As persistence rises, the sample mean (dashed orange line) can remain farther from the population mean (solid orange line).

For a stationary AR(1) with marginal variance one, the long-run variance is \((1+\rho)/(1-\rho)\). With \(\rho=0.7\), it is about 5.67: asymptotically, the variance of the sample mean is about 5.67 times the independent-observation value.

\begin{figure}[H]
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/TVTCL-1} \caption{Persistence and sample means. Each stationary AR(1) has mean two and variance one, with its first observation drawn from the stationary distribution. Solid orange lines mark the population mean; dashed orange lines mark each sample mean. At very high persistence, 200 observations can cover little of the stationary distribution.}\label{fig:TVTCL}
\end{figure}

\section{Univariate processes}\label{univariate-processes}

The preceding concepts provide the language for studying particular models. We now build two basic classes from white-noise shocks: moving-average processes, which depend on current and past shocks, and autoregressive processes, which depend on their own past values.

\subsection{Moving Average (MA) processes}\label{moving-average-ma-processes}

\begin{definition}
Consider a white noise process \(\{\varepsilon_t\}_{t = -\infty}^{+\infty}\) (Def. \ref{def:whitenoise}). Then \(y_t\) is a first-order moving average process if, for all \(t\):
\[
y_t = \mu + \varepsilon_t + \theta \varepsilon_{t-1}.
\]
\end{definition}

If \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_t^2)=\sigma^2\), it is easily obtained that the unconditional mean and variances of \(y_t\) are:
\[
\mathbb{E}(y_t) = \mu, \quad \mathbb{V}ar(y_t) = (1+\theta^2)\sigma^2.
\]

The first auto-covariance is:
\[
\gamma_1=\mathbb{E}\{(y_t - \mu)(y_{t-1} - \mu)\} = \theta \sigma^2.
\]

Higher-order auto-covariances are zero (\(\gamma_j=0\) for \(j>1\)). Therefore: An MA(1) process is covariance-stationary (Def. \ref{def:covstat}).

For a MA(1) process, the autocorrelation of order \(j\) (see Def. \ref{def:autocor}) is given by:
\[
\rho_j =
\left\{
\begin{array}{lll}
1 &\mbox{ if }& j=0,\\
\theta / (1 + \theta^2) &\mbox{ if }& j = 1\\
0 &\mbox{ if }& j>1.
\end{array}
\right.
\]

For \(\theta\ne0\), the process \(y_t\) defined through:
\[
y_t = \mu + \varepsilon_t +\theta \varepsilon_{t-1}, 
\]
where \(\mathbb{V}ar(\varepsilon_t)=\sigma^2\), has the same mean and autocovariances as
\[
y_t = \mu + \varepsilon^*_t +\frac{1}{\theta}\varepsilon^*_{t-1},
\]
where \(\mathbb{V}ar(\varepsilon^*_t)=\theta^2\sigma^2\). That is, even if we perfectly know the mean and auto-covariances of this process, it is not possible to identify which specification is the one that has been used to generate the data. For \(\theta\ne0\) and \(|\theta|\ne1\), exactly one representation satisfies \(|\theta|<1\). This \textbf{invertible} representation allows shocks to be recovered as a convergent linear function of present and past observations. At \(|\theta|=1\), neither reciprocal representation is invertible.

\begin{definition}[MA(q) process]
\protect\hypertarget{def:MAq}{}\label{def:MAq}A \(q^{th}\) order Moving Average process is defined through:
\[
y_t = \mu + \varepsilon_t + \theta_1 \varepsilon_{t-1} + \dots + \theta_q \varepsilon_{t-q}.
\]
where \(\{\varepsilon_t\}_{t = -\infty}^{+\infty}\) is a white noise process (Def. \ref{def:whitenoise}).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Covariance-stationarity of an MA(q) process]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:covMAq}{}\label{prp:covMAq}Finite-order Moving Average processes are covariance-stationary.

Moreover, the autocovariances of an MA(q) process (as defined in Def. \ref{def:MAq}) are given by:
\begin{equation}
\gamma_j = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sigma^2(\theta_j\theta_0 + \theta_{j+1}\theta_{1} +  \dots + \theta_{q}\theta_{q-j}) &\mbox{for} \quad j \in \{0,\dots,q\} \\ 0 &\mbox{for} \quad j>q, \end{array} \right.\label{eq:autocovMA}
\end{equation}
where we use the notation \(\theta_0=1\), and \(\mathbb{V}ar(\varepsilon_t)=\sigma^2\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The unconditional expectation of \(y_t\) does not depend on time, since \(\mathbb{E}(y_t)=\mu\). Let's turn to autocovariances. We can extend the series of the \(\theta_j\)'s by setting \(\theta_j=0\) for \(j>q\). We then have:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{E}((y_t-\mu)(y_{t-j}-\mu)) &=& \mathbb{E}\left[(\theta_0 \varepsilon_t +\theta_1 \varepsilon_{t-1} + \dots +\theta_j \varepsilon_{t-j}+\theta_{j+1} \varepsilon_{t-j-1} + \dots) \right.\times \\
&&\left. (\theta_0 \varepsilon_{t-j} +\theta_1 \varepsilon_{t-j-1} + \dots)\right].
\end{eqnarray*}
Then use the fact that \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_t\varepsilon_s)=0\) if \(t \ne s\) (because \(\{\varepsilon_t\}_{t = -\infty}^{+\infty}\) is a white noise process).
\end{proof}

Figure \ref{fig:simMA} illustrates two finite MA models. In the MA(4), a shock affects five successive observations, but no observation beyond that range.

\begin{figure}
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{EcoStat_files/figure-latex/simMA-1} \caption{Simulated moving averages with mean one and unit-variance shocks. Left: MA(1), coefficient 0.6. Right: MA(4), coefficients 0.5, 0.3, 0.2, 0.1.}\label{fig:simMA}
\end{figure}

What if the order \(q\) of an MA(q) process gets infinite? The notion of \textbf{infinite-order Moving Average process} exists and is important in time series analysis. The (infinite) sequence of \(\theta_j\) has to satisfy some conditions for such a process to be well-defined (see Theorem \ref{thm:infMA} below). These conditions relate to the ``summability'' of \(\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\) (see Definition \ref{def:summability}).

\begin{definition}[Absolute and square summability]
\protect\hypertarget{def:summability}{}\label{def:summability}The sequence \(\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\) is absolutely summable if \(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}|\theta_i| < + \infty\), and it is square summable if \(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty} \theta_i^2 < + \infty\).
\end{definition}

According to Prop. \ref{prp:absMs}, absolute summability implies square summability.

\begin{theorem}[Existence condition for an infinite MA process]
\protect\hypertarget{thm:infMA}{}\label{thm:infMA}If \(\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\) is square summable (see Def. \ref{def:summability}) and if \(\{\varepsilon_t\}_{t = -\infty}^{+\infty}\) is a white noise process (see Def. \ref{def:whitenoise}), then
\[
\mu + \sum_{i=0}^{+\infty} \theta_{i} \varepsilon_{t-i}
\]
defines a well-behaved {[}covariance-stationary{]} process, called infinite-order MA process (MA(\(\infty\))).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
See Appendix 3.A in Hamilton. ``Well behaved'' means that \(\sum_{i=0}^{N} \theta_i \varepsilon_{t-i}\) converges in mean square (Def. \ref{def:convergenceLr}) to some random variable \(Z_t\). The proof makes use of the fact that:
\[
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=N}^{M}\theta_{i} \varepsilon_{t-i}\right)^2\right] = \sum_{i=N}^{M}|\theta_{i}|^2 \sigma^2,
\]
and that, when \(\{\theta_{i}\}\) is square summable, \(\forall \eta>0\), \(\exists N\) s.t. the right-hand-side term in the last equation is lower than \(\eta\) for all \(M \ge N\) (static Cauchy criterion, Theorem \ref{thm:cauchycritstatic}). This implies that \(\Sigma_{i=0}^{T} \theta_{i} \varepsilon_{t-i}\) converges in mean square (stochastic Cauchy criterion, see Theorem \ref{thm:cauchycritstochastic}).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[First two moments of an infinite MA process]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:momentsMAinf}{}\label{prp:momentsMAinf}

If \(\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\) is absolutely summable, i.e.~if \(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}|\theta_i| < + \infty\), then

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\roman{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(y_t = \mu + \sum_{i=0}^{+\infty} \theta_{i} \varepsilon_{t-i}\) exists (Theorem \ref{thm:infMA}) and is such that:
  \begin{eqnarray*}
  \mathbb{E}(y_t) &=& \mu\\
  \gamma_0 = \mathbb{E}([y_t-\mu]^2) &=& \sigma^2(\theta_0^2 +\theta_1^2 + \dots)\\
  \gamma_j = \mathbb{E}([y_t-\mu][y_{t-j}-\mu]) &=& \sigma^2(\theta_0\theta_j + \theta_{1}\theta_{j+1} + \dots).
  \end{eqnarray*}
\item
  Process \(y_t\) has absolutely summable auto-covariances, so the mean-square and long-run-variance conclusions of Theorem \ref{thm:CLTcovstat} apply. Asymptotic normality additionally needs a central-limit condition; independent identically distributed shocks with finite variance are sufficient for this absolutely summable linear process.
\end{enumerate}

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The absolute summability of \(\{\theta_{i}\}\) and the fact that \(\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon^2)<\infty\) imply that the order of integration and summation is interchangeable (see Hamilton, 1994, Footnote p.~52), which proves (i). For (ii), see end of Appendix 3.A in Hamilton (1994).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Autoregressive (AR) processes}\label{ARsection}

Moving-average models describe dependence directly through past shocks. Autoregressive models describe it recursively through past observations, which makes them especially natural for persistence and forecasting.

\begin{definition}[First-order AR process (AR(1))]
\protect\hypertarget{def:AR1}{}\label{def:AR1}Consider a white noise process \(\{\varepsilon_t\}_{t = -\infty}^{+\infty}\) (see Def. \ref{def:whitenoise}). Process \(y_t\) is an AR(1) process if it is defined by the following difference equation:
\[
y_t = c + \phi y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t.
\]
\end{definition}

We study \textbf{causal} solutions, built from current and past shocks, with \(\sigma^2>0\). Iterating the equation gives
\[
y_{t+k}=\phi^k y_t+c\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\phi^j+
\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\phi^j\varepsilon_{t+k-j}.
\]
The future shocks are uncorrelated with the causal history. Their contribution to the forecast-error variance is
\[
\sigma^2\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\phi^{2j}.
\]
If \(|\phi|\ge1\), this grows without bound, ruling out a causal covariance-stationary solution. This statement concerns causal solutions; allowing dependence on future shocks changes the question.

By contrast, if \(|\phi| < 1\), one can see that:
\[
y_t = c + \varepsilon_t + \phi  ( c + \varepsilon_{t-1})+ \phi^2  ( c + \varepsilon_{t-2})+ \dots + \phi^k  ( c + \varepsilon_{t-k}) + \dots
\]
Hence, if \(|\phi| < 1\), the unconditional mean and variance of \(y_t\) are:
\[
\mathbb{E}(y_t) = \frac{c}{1-\phi} =: \mu \quad \mbox{and} \quad \mathbb{V}ar(y_t) = \frac{\sigma^2}{1-\phi^2}.
\]

Let us compute the \(j^{th}\) autocovariance of the AR(1) process:
\begin{eqnarray*}
\mathbb{E}([y_{t} - \mu][y_{t-j} - \mu]) &=& \mathbb{E}([\varepsilon_t + \phi  \varepsilon_{t-1}+ \phi^2 \varepsilon_{t-2} + \dots + \color{red}{\phi^j \varepsilon_{t-j}} + \color{blue}{\phi^{j+1} \varepsilon_{t-j-1}} \dots]\times \\
&&[\color{red}{\varepsilon_{t-j}} + \color{blue}{\phi \varepsilon_{t-j-1}} + \phi^2 \varepsilon_{t-j-2} + \dots + \phi^k \varepsilon_{t-j-k} + \dots])\\
&=& \mathbb{E}(\color{red}{\phi^j \varepsilon_{t-j}^2}+\color{blue}{\phi^{j+2} \varepsilon_{t-j-1}^2}+\phi^{j+4} \varepsilon_{t-j-2}^2+\dots)\\
&=& \frac{\phi^j \sigma^2}{1 - \phi^2}.
\end{eqnarray*}

Therefore \(\rho_j = \phi^j\).

By what precedes, we have:

\begin{proposition}[Causal covariance-stationarity of an AR(1) process]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:statioAR1}{}\label{prp:statioAR1}The AR(1) equation in Def. \ref{def:AR1} has a causal covariance-stationary solution---one expressible in current and past shocks---if and only if \(|\phi|<1\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}[AR(p) process]
\protect\hypertarget{def:ARp}{}\label{def:ARp}Consider a white noise process \(\{\varepsilon_t\}_{t = -\infty}^{+\infty}\) (see Def. \ref{def:whitenoise}). Process \(y_t\) is a \(p^{th}\)-order autoregressive process (AR(p)) if its dynamics is defined by the following difference equation (with \(\phi_p \ne 0\)):
\begin{equation}
y_t = c + \phi_1 y_{t-1} + \phi_2 y_{t-2} + \dots + \phi_p y_{t-p} + \varepsilon_t.\label{eq:AR}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

As we will see, the covariance-stationarity of process \(y_t\) hinges on matrix \(F\) defined as:
\begin{equation}
F = \left[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\phi_1 & \phi_2 & \dots& & \phi_p \\
1 & 0 &\dots && 0 \\
0 & 1 &\dots && 0 \\
\vdots &  & \ddots && \vdots \\
0 & 0 &\dots &1& 0 \\
\end{array}
\right].\label{eq:F}
\end{equation}

Note that this matrix \(F\) is such that if \(y_t\) follows Eq. \eqref{eq:AR}, then process \(\mathbf{y}_t\) follows:
\[
\mathbf{y}_t = \mathbf{c} + F \mathbf{y}_{t-1} + \boldsymbol\xi_t
\]
with
\[
\mathbf{c} =
\left[\begin{array}{c}
c\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0
\end{array}\right],
\quad
\boldsymbol\xi_t =
\left[\begin{array}{c}
\varepsilon_t\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0
\end{array}\right],
\quad
\mathbf{y}_t =
\left[\begin{array}{c}
y_t\\
y_{t-1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{t-p+1}
\end{array}\right].
\]

\begin{proposition}[The eigenvalues of matrix F]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:Feigen}{}\label{prp:Feigen}The eigenvalues of \(F\) (defined by Eq. \eqref{eq:F}) are the solutions of:
\begin{equation}
\lambda^p - \phi_1 \lambda^{p-1} - \dots - \phi_{p-1}\lambda - \phi_p = 0.\label{eq:Feigen}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[Causal stability of an AR(p) process]
\protect\hypertarget{prp:stability}{}\label{prp:stability}

These four statements are equivalent:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\roman{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The AR(p) equation in Def. \ref{def:ARp}, with \(\sigma^2>0\), admits a causal covariance-stationary solution.
\item
  The eigenvalues of \(F\) (as defined Eq. \eqref{eq:F}) lie strictly within the unit circle.
\item
  The roots of Eq. \eqref{eq:outside} (below) lie strictly outside the unit circle.
  \begin{equation}
  1 - \phi_1 z - \dots - \phi_{p-1}z^{p-1} - \phi_p z^p = 0.\label{eq:outside}
  \end{equation}
\item
  The roots of Eq. \eqref{eq:inside} (below) lie strictly inside the unit circle.
  \begin{equation}
  \lambda^p - \phi_1 \lambda^{p-1} - \dots - \phi_{p-1}\lambda - \phi_p = 0.\label{eq:inside}
  \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
When all eigenvalues have modulus below one, powers of \(F\) decay sufficiently fast for
\[
\mathbf{y}_t-\boldsymbol\mu=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}F^j\boldsymbol\xi_{t-j}
\]
to converge in mean square. This constructs the causal stationary solution. Conversely, iterating a causal stationary solution gives a bounded innovation-variance sum. For the companion matrix in Eq. \eqref{eq:F}, with positive shock variance, this requires all eigenvalues to have modulus below one. This establishes (i)--(ii). Proposition \ref{prp:Feigen} gives (ii)--(iv); taking reciprocal roots gives (iii)--(iv).
\end{proof}

Using the lag operator (see Eq \eqref{eq:lagOp}), if \(y_t\) is a stable causal AR(p) process (Def. \ref{def:ARp}), we can write:
\[
y_t = \mu + \psi(L)\varepsilon_t,
\]
where
\begin{equation}
\psi(L) = (1 - \phi_1 L - \dots - \phi_p L^p)^{-1},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\mu = \mathbb{E}(y_t) = \dfrac{c}{1-\phi_1 -\dots - \phi_p}.\label{eq:EAR}
\end{equation}

To assess stationarity, we compute the eigenvalues of the \(F\) matrices associated with the following processes (where \(\varepsilon_t\) is a white noise):
\begin{eqnarray*}
x_t &=& 0.9 x_{t-1} -0.2 x_{t-2} + \varepsilon_t\\
y_t &=& 1.1 y_{t-1} -0.3 y_{t-2} + \varepsilon_t\\
w_t &=& 1.4 w_{t-1} -0.7 w_{t-2} + \varepsilon_t\\
z_t &=& 0.9 z_{t-1} +0.2 z_{t-2} + \varepsilon_t
\end{eqnarray*}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering\begingroup\fontsize{10}{12}\selectfont

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth\linewidth\else\width\fi}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Process & Eigenvalue 1 & Eigenvalue 2\\
\midrule
x & 0.500+0.000i & 0.400+0.000i\\
y & 0.600+0.000i & 0.500+0.000i\\
w & 0.700+0.458i & 0.700-0.458i\\
z & 1.084+0.000i & -0.184+0.000i\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\endgroup{}
\end{table}

The absolute values of the eigenvalues associated with process \(w_t\) are both equal to 0.837.
